{"mainEntity":{"headline":"Fixed-Parameter Extrapolation and Aperiodic Order","description":"Fix any \n                $$\\lambda \\in \\mathbb {C}$$\n                \n              . We say that a set \n                $$S\\subseteq \\mathbb {C}$$\n                \n               is \n                $$\\lambda $$\n                \n              -convex if, whenever a and b are in S, the point \n                $$(1-\\lambda )a+\\lambda b$$\n                \n               is also in S. We investigate the properties of \n                $$\\lambda $$\n                \n              -convex sets and their (topological) closures, and we prove a number of facts about them. Let \n                $$Q_\\lambda \\subseteq \\mathbb {C}$$\n                \n               be the least \n                $$\\lambda $$\n                \n              -convex superset of \n                $$\\{0,1\\}$$\n                \n              . Generalizing results of R. G. E. Pinch, we give a sufficient condition on \n                $$\\lambda $$\n                \n               for \n                $$Q_\\lambda $$\n                \n               and some other related \n                $$\\lambda $$\n                \n              -convex sets to be discrete by introducing the notion of a strong PV number. These conditions give rise to a number of periodic and aperiodic Meyer sets (often regarded as the mathematical counterpart of “quasicrystals”). This paper is in two parts: Part I (Theory, Sections 1–6) gives general results about \n                $$\\lambda $$\n                \n              -convex sets and explores the connections between \n                $$\\lambda $$\n                \n              -convex sets and quasicrystals; Part II (Applications, Sections 7–11) applies the results of Part I to a number of special cases. In Part II, we also display several aperiodic \n                $$\\lambda $$\n                \n              -convex sets, including several with dihedral symmetry. Section 11 in Part II contains conjectures, open problems, and other suggestions for further research. Our work combines elementary concepts and techniques from algebra and plane geometry. Our results extend and generalize previous work of Pinch, Berman & Moody, and Masáková, Patera, & Pelantová. An extended paper that includes the results given here among many others is available     function refreshCartState(json) {
        var indicator = document.querySelector("#ecommerce-header-cart-icon-link .cart-info");
        /* istanbul ignore else */
        if (indicator && json.itemCount) {
            indicator.style.display = 'block';
            indicator.textContent = json.itemCount > 9 ? '9+' : json.itemCount.toString();
            var moreThanOneItem = json.itemCount > 1;
            indicator.setAttribute('title', "there ".concat(moreThanOneItem ? "are" : "is", " ").concat(json.itemCount, " item").concat(moreThanOneItem ? "s" : "", " in your cart"));
        }
        return json;
    }
};
exports.headerWidgetClientInit = headerWidgetClientInit;

            
            headerWidgetClientInit(
              function () {
                return window.fetch("https://cart.springer.com/cart-info", {
                  credentials: "include",
                  headers: { Accept: "application/json" }
                })
              }
            )
        }})()

                                Fixed-Parameter Extrapolation and Aperiodic Order

Stephen Fenner
 
            
ORCID: 
orcid.org/0000-0002-6028-9323
1
, 
Frederic Green
2
 & 
Steven Homer
3

Abstract
Fix any 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {C}\)
. We say that a set 
\(S\subseteq \mathbb {C}\)
 is 
\(\lambda \)
-convex
 if, whenever 
a
 and 
b
 are in 
S
, the point 
\((1-\lambda )a+\lambda b\)
 is also in 
S
. We investigate the properties of 
\(\lambda \)
-convex sets and their (topological) closures, and we prove a number of facts about them. Let 
\(Q_\lambda \subseteq \mathbb {C}\)
 be the least 
\(\lambda \)
-convex superset of 
\(\{0,1\}\)
. Generalizing results of R. G. E. Pinch, we give a sufficient condition on 
\(\lambda \)
 for 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 and some other related 
\(\lambda \)
-convex sets to be discrete by introducing the notion of a 
strong PV number
. These conditions give rise to a number of periodic and aperiodic Meyer sets (often regarded as the mathematical counterpart of “quasicrystals”). This paper is in two parts: Part I (Theory, Sections 1–6) gives general results about 
\(\lambda \)
-convex sets and explores the connections between 
\(\lambda \)
-convex sets and quasicrystals; Part II (Applications, Sections 7–11) applies the results of Part I to a number of special cases. In Part II, we also display several aperiodic 
\(\lambda \)
-convex sets, including several with dihedral symmetry. Section 
11
 in Part II contains conjectures, open problems, and other suggestions for further research. Our work combines elementary concepts and techniques from algebra and plane geometry. Our results extend and generalize previous work of Pinch, Berman & Moody, and Masáková, Patera, & Pelantová. An extended paper that includes the results given here among many others is available online.

-hyperconvexity in quasi-metric spaces

Part I: Theory
1 
Introduction

                
Definition 1.1

                
Fix a number 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {C}\)
. For any 
\(a,b\in \mathbb {C}\)
 define 
\(a \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}b \mathrel {:=}(1-\lambda )a + \lambda b\)
.

                
Then we say that a set 
\(S\subseteq \mathbb {C}\)
 is 
\(\lambda \)
-convex
 iff for every 
\(a,b\in S\)
, the point 
\(a\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}b\)
 is in 
S
. Such an 
S
 is 
nontrivial
 if 
S
 contains at least two distinct elements.

              
For fixed 
\(\lambda \)
, we define 
\({\mathop {\star }}\mathrel {:=}\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}\)
 as a binary operation on 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
. We may drop the subscript if 
\(\lambda \)
 is clear from the context. We call 
\(a{\mathop {\star }}b\)
 the 
\(\lambda \)
-extrapolant
 of 
a
 and 
b
, and we say that 
\(a{\mathop {\star }}b\)
 is obtained from 
a
 and 
b
 by 
\(\lambda \)
-extrapolation
. Then the first property in Definition 
1.1
 just says that 
S
 is closed under 
\(\lambda \)
-extrapolation. Of course, if 
\(0\le \lambda \le 1\)
, then this might more appropriately be called 
\(\lambda \)
-interpolation, but as we will see, the case where 
\(\lambda \notin {\left[ {0,1}\right] }\)
 is much more interesting. When we are not explicit about 
\(\lambda \)
, we refer to the operation 
\(\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}\)
 as 
fixed-parameter extrapolation
 or 
fixed-parameter affine combination
.
By the definition of convexity, a set 
\(S\subseteq \mathbb {C}\)
 is convex if and only if, for all 
\(\lambda \in {\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
, 
S
 is 
\(\lambda \)
-convex. Observe, however, that if 
S
 is a 
closed
 set, then for 
any fixed
 
\(\lambda \in {\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
, we have that 
S
 is convex if and only if 
S
 is 
\(\lambda \)
-convex. This suggests that special emphasis be given to 
\(\lambda \)
-convex sets that are also closed (in this intro, with a straight face, we will call such sets 
\(\lambda \)
-clonvex
). We are generally interested in 
\(\lambda \)
-c[l]onvexity for 
\(\lambda \notin {\left[ {0,1}\right] }\)
, and we are particularly interested in minimal nontrivial 
\(\lambda \)
-clonvex sets.

                
Definition 1.2

                
For any 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {C}\)
 and any set 
\(S\subseteq \mathbb {C}\)
, we define the 
\(\lambda \)
-convex hull
 of 
S
, denoted 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
, to be the 
\(\subseteq \)
-minimum 
\(\lambda \)
-convex superset of 
S
.
Footnote 
1
 We let 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 be shorthand for 
\(Q_\lambda (\{0,1\})\)
, the 
\(\lambda \)
-convex hull of 
\(\{0,1\}\)
. We define 
\(K_\lambda (S):= \textrm{cl}({Q_\lambda (S)})\)
, the (topological) closure of 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
. We let 
\(K_\lambda \)
 be shorthand for 
\(K_\lambda (\{0,1\})\)
.

              
One can easily show (Lemma 
2.6
 below) that 
\(K_\lambda (S)\)
 is 
\(\lambda \)
-convex, and is thus the smallest 
\(\lambda \)
-clonvex superset of 
S
. 
\(K_\lambda \)
 is a minimal nontrivial 
\(\lambda \)
-clonvex set because it is generated by just two distinct points. We choose the points 0 and 1 for convenience, but since 
\(\lambda \)
-clonvexity is invariant under orientation-preserving similarity transformations (i.e., 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
-affine transformations, i.e., polynomials of degree 1; see Definition 2.3, below), any two initial points would yield a set with the same essential properties. A general goal of our research, then, is to characterize 
\(K_\lambda \)
 for as many 
\(\lambda \)
 as we can. A more specific goal of the current paper is to clarify, for certain 
\(\lambda \)
 such that 
\(K_\lambda \)
 is discrete (and thus equal to 
\(Q_\lambda \)
), the geometric properties of 
\(K_\lambda = Q_\lambda \)
.
We conclude this introduction with some historical background and motivation. The notion of fixed-parameter extrapolation was first investigated for its own intrinsic interest by Calvert [
10
] and, some years later, by Pinch [
40
]. Berman & Moody [
8
] were the first to notice its application to discrete 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 in the context of 
quasicrystals
 (see below), where they call it “quasicrystal addition,” specifically in the case of 
\(\lambda = 1+\varphi \)
 (where 
\(\varphi \)
 is the golden ratio), which we cover in this paper as well. This value of 
\(\lambda \)
 is significant because it gives the simplest example where 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is discrete and aperiodic.
The comparatively young field of 
aperiodic order
 [
6
, 
7
] arose, in part, to explain such mathematical phenomena as aperiodic tilings of the plane (perhaps the most well-known of which are Penrose tilings [
12
, 
39
]) and natural phenomena such as quasicrystals—spatial atomic arrangements that share many properties of true crystals but are aperiodic, i.e., have no translational symmetry (see, e.g., [
42
]). Traditionally, aperiodic order has been studied through paradigms such as local substitution, inflation tilings, and so-called “cut-and-project” sets (see Definitions 
4.7
 and 
4.8
). Roughly, a cut-and-project set (also called a model set) is obtained by starting with a lattice in 
\(\mathbb {R}^n\)
, restricting it to a “slice” of 
\(\mathbb {R}^n\)
 bounded by hyperplanes, then projecting the resulting subset into a Euclidean space of smaller dimension. The points in the projected set are regularly spaced, but the set itself may be aperiodic, depending on how the hyperplanes are oriented. As Berman and Moody [
8
] have pointed out, and as investigated further by other authors [
29
, 
30
, 
34
], fixed-parameter extrapolation offers an alternative approach to aperiodic order. Indeed, as is hinted in [
8
] and stated explicitly in [
30
], one may view the 
\({\mathop {\star }}\)
 operation as a mathematical model of quasicrystal growth. The discrete sets generated by fixed-parameter extrapolation share many properties with 
Meyer sets
 [
6
, 
35
], widely regarded as the mathematical counterpart of quasicrystals. Indeed, we can establish in many cases that these sets 
are
 Meyer sets. In this context, other authors have investigated fixed-parameter extrapolation under various other names, including quasiaddition and 
\(\tau \)
-inflation [
29
, 
30
, 
34
]. More recent work dealing with algebraically similar ideas can be found in [
20
, 
21
] or, more broadly related, in [
2
,
3
,
4
, 
28
].
This paper is divided into two parts: Part I (Theory) and Part II (Applications).
The goals of Part I of this paper are threefold. First, we work towards a systematic and unified theory of fixed-parameter extrapolation (the 
\(\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}\)
 operator) and the point sets closed under 
\(\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}\)
 for various 
\(\lambda \)
. We then characterize those 
\(K_\lambda \)
 that are convex. Secondly, we extend aspects of the theory already accomplished in previous work (notably [
8
, 
10
, 
29
, 
30
, 
40
], and [
34
]) from the case of 
real
 
\(\lambda \)
 to 
complex
 
\(\lambda \)
. To our knowledge, our work is the first to examine 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {C}\setminus \mathbb {R}\)
. We study precise connections between fixed-parameter extrapolation and aperiodic order, specifically the model sets obtained from cut-and-project schemes. We give a partial characterization of parameters that lead to discrete sets, using a refinement of Pisot-Vijayaraghavan (PV) numbers, which we call 
strong PV
 numbers. A strong PV number is an algebraic integer 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {C}\)
 all of whose Galois conjugates (other than 
\(\lambda \)
 and its complex conjugate) are in the unit interval. These numbers generalize the (unnamed) subset of real irrationals considered in [
40
]. We show that 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 is uniformly discrete for all strong PV numbers 
\(\lambda \)
 and all finite 
\(S\subseteq \mathbb {Q}(\lambda )\)
. All of the sets we find to be discrete are in fact subsets of model sets. Such sets are called 
weak
 Meyer sets (see, e.g., [
5
, 
22
]). Thirdly, we build up techniques to show in many cases that discrete 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 are relatively dense, making them Meyer sets.
In Part II, we apply the general results and techniques of Part I to a wide array of special cases. We answer questions posed in [
30
] as well as displaying several subsets of 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
 with dihedral symmetry, showing them all to be Meyer sets. We present conjectures, open problems, and suggestions for further research in Section 
11
. Plots and Voronoi diagrams of several of the sets studied are in Appendices B and C, respectively.
The techniques of this paper draw on diverse fields, including complex analysis, algebra, algebraic number theory, topology, combinatorics, and computer science. In support of theoretical studies, computation (including symbolic computation) and computer graphics have been used to guide our work and determine future problems and directions.
As mentioned, a main focus of this paper is on the connection between fixed-parameter extrapolation and Meyer sets (or quasicrystals), but the paper forms only part of an extended body of results on fixed-parameter extrapolation [
17
]. There are several results there that are not included here and will be the treated in other papers. Some of these include: a complete characterization of 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 for (
\(\mathbb {Z}\)
-)integer 
\(\lambda \)
; a proof that any convex 
\(K_\lambda \)
 is either 
\({\left[ {0,1}\right] }\)
, 
\(\mathbb {R}\)
, or 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
; a dichotomy theorem saying for all 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {C}\)
 that 
\(K_\lambda \)
 is either convex or uniformly discrete (whence 
\(K_\lambda = Q_\lambda \)
); a proof for all 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {C}\)
 that if 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is discrete then 
\(\lambda \)
 is an algebraic integer. (Pinch proved versions of the last three results for real 
\(\lambda \)
 [
40
]. The algebraic integer result is a nontrivial generalization of Pinch’s.)
2 
Basics
We start with a few basic definitions and results. In this paper, we call a theorem a “Fact” when it is either immediately obvious or has a routine, straightforward proof. We omit the proofs of Facts.
For 
\(z\in \mathbb {C}\)
, we let 
\(Re (z)\)
 and 
\(Im (z)\)
 denote the real and imaginary parts of 
z
, respectively, and we let 
\(\overline{z}\)
 denote the complex conjugate of 
z
. If 
\(S \subseteq \mathbb {C}\)
, then 
\(\overline{S}\)
 means 
\(\{\overline{z} \mid z \in S\}\)
.
For 
X
 a topological space and 
\(A\subseteq X\)
, we let 
\(\textrm{cl}({A})\)
 denote the topological closure of 
A
 in 
X
. We assume the usual topology on 
\(\mathbb {R}^n\)
 (
\(n\ge 1\)
) and 
\(\mathbb {C}\cong \mathbb {R}^2\)
.
If 
\({{f}:{X}\rightarrow {Y}}\)
 is some function with domain 
X
, and 
A
 is any subset of 
X
, then we let 
\(f|_A\)
 denote the function 
f
 restricted to domain 
A
.
We use the symbol 
\(:=\)
 to mean, “equals by definition.” For any 
\(x\in \mathbb {R}\)
, let 
\(\{x\} = x \bmod 1\)
 denote the fractional part of 
x
, i.e., the unique 
\(y\in {\left[ {0,1}\right) }\)
 such that 
\(x-y\in \mathbb {Z}\)
.
We let 
\({\mathbb {Z}^+}\)
 denote the set of positive integers.
Whenever a ring 
R
 is mentioned, it will be assumed to be commutative and unital, that is, possessing a multiplicative identity denoted 
\(1_R\)
. If an 
R
-module 
M
 possesses a topology 
\(\mathcal {T}\)
, then we assume that vector addition in 
M
 and multiplication by any fixed scalar in 
R
 are continuous with respect to 
\(\mathcal {T}\)
. If 
R
 also has a topology, then we assume multiplying a fixed element of 
M
 by scalars is continuous.
Operations on vectors, numbers, etc. lift to operations on sets thereof in the usual way. This includes subtraction, and so 
\( S-T = \{x-y \mid x\in S \;\mathrel { \& }\;y\in T\}\)
, i.e., the Minkowski difference of 
S
 and 
T
. We use 
\(S \setminus T\)
 to denote the relative complement of 
T
 in 
S
.
We start the indexing of vectors and matrices with 0 instead of with 1. If 
E
 is some expression of matrix type, we let 
\([E]_{ij}\)
 denote the 
\({{(i,j)}^{\textit{th}}}\)
 entry of 
E
, for any appropriate integers 
\(i,j\ge 0\)
. We let an expression of the form 
\((x_0,x_1,x_2,\ldots )\)
 denote either a row vector or a column vector, depending on the context.
2.1 
General Structures

                  
Notation 2.1

                  
Throughout this subsection, 
R
 will denote an arbitrary commutative ring and 
M
 and 
N
 arbitrary 
R
-modules. If 
\(R=M\)
, then we assume scalar multiplication equals ring multiplication.

                
                  
Definition 2.2

                  
A map 
\({{f}:{M}\rightarrow {N}}\)
 is 
R
-
affine
 if there exist a homomorphism 
\({{g}:{M}\rightarrow {N}}\)
 of 
R
-modules and a 
\(v\in N\)
 such that 
\(f(u) = g(u)+v\)
 for all 
\(u\in M\)
. If 
\(v=0\)
, then 
\(f=g\)
 is 
R
-linear
.

                
Definition 
2.3
 below extends Definitions 
1.1
 and 
1.2
 to more general structures.

                  
Definition 2.3

                  
For any 
\(\lambda \in R\)
 and 
\(u,v\in M\)
, we define both 
\(u\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}v\)
 and 
\(\rho _{u,v}(\lambda )\)
 to be the point 
\((1-\lambda )u + \lambda v = u + \lambda (v-u)\in M\)
. For fixed 
\(\lambda \in R\)
, this defines the binary operator 
\({{\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}}:{M\times M}\rightarrow {M}}\)
, which we call the 
\(\lambda \)
-extrapolation operator
.
Footnote 
2
 For fixed 
\(u,v\in M\)
, this defines the 
R
-affine map 
\({{\rho _{u,v}}:{R}\rightarrow {M}}\)
.

                  
A set 
\(S\subseteq M\)
 is 
\(\lambda \)
-convex
 iff 
S
 is closed under the 
\(\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}\)
 operation, that is, iff 
\(S\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}S \subseteq S\)
. For any 
\(T\subseteq M\)
, we define 
\(Q_\lambda (T)\subseteq M\)
, the 
\(\lambda \)
-convex hull
 of 
T
, to be the least 
\(\lambda \)
-convex superset of 
T
. We also say that 
T
 
generates 
 
\(Q_\lambda (T)\)
. If 
M
 has a topology, then we define 
\(K_\lambda (T):= \textrm{cl}({Q_\lambda (T)})\)
. If 
\(R=M\)
, then we define 
\(Q_\lambda := Q_\lambda (\{0,1\})\)
 (and 
\(K_\lambda := \textrm{cl}({Q_\lambda })\)
 if 
M
 has a topology).

                
We may drop parentheses in an expression involving 
\({\mathop {\star }}\)
 or 
\(\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}\)
, assuming that this operator binds more tightly than + or − but less tightly than multiplication or division.
We note the following simple properties of the 
\(\lambda \)
-extrapolation operator.

                  
Fact 2.4

                  
We have 
\(a\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}a = a\)
 and 
\((xa+yb)\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}(xc+yd) = x(a\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}c) + y(b\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}d)\)
 for all 
\(\lambda ,x,y\in R\)
 and 
\(a,b,c,d\in M\)
. In particular, setting 
\(x:= 1-\mu \)
 and 
\(y:=\mu \)
 gives 
\((a\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\mu }}b)\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}(c\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\mu }}d) = (a\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}c)\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\mu }}(b\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}d)\)
 for any 
\(\mu \in R\)
. If 
\(\mu = \lambda \)
, then we have the 
entropic law
$$\begin{aligned} (a\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}b) \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}(c \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}d) = (a \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}c) \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}(b \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}d)\;. \end{aligned}$$

                
                  
Remark

                  
A groupoid satisfying the identity 
\((ab)(cd) \approx (ac)(bd)\)
 is known as a 
medial
 groupoid. If the operation is also idempotent (
\(aa\approx a\)
), then the groupoid is sometimes called a 
medial band
, 
(groupoid) mode
, or 
idempotent medial groupoid
, as well as other names. These structures have been studied extensively in the literature. See, for example, [
11
, 
13
, 
14
, 
24
]. These two identities are not the only ones universally satisfied by 
\(\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}\)
. For example, 
\((u \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}(v \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}w)) \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}((x \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}y) \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}z) = (u \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}(x \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}w)) \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}((v \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}y) \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}z)\)
 for all 
\(u,v,w,x,y,z\in M\)
 and 
\(\lambda \in R\)
, and this identity does not follow from idempotence and the entropic law alone. 
\(\square \)

                
                  
Notation 2.5

                  
For the rest of this section, we use 
\({\mathop {\star }}\)
 with no subscript to mean 
\(\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}\)
.

                
                  
Lemma 2.6

                  
If 
M
 has a topology, then 
\(K_\lambda (S)\)
 is 
\(\lambda \)
-convex for any 
\(\lambda \in R\)
 and 
\(S\subseteq M\)
.

                
                  
Proof

                  
By the continuity of 
\({\mathop {\star }}\)
, for all 
\(A,B\subseteq M\)
, we have 
\(\textrm{cl}({A}){\mathop {\star }}\textrm{cl}({B}) = {\mathop {\star }}\left( \textrm{cl}({A})\times \textrm{cl}({B})\right) = {\mathop {\star }}\left( \textrm{cl}({A\times B})\right) \subseteq \textrm{cl}({{\mathop {\star }}(A\times B)})= \textrm{cl}({A{\mathop {\star }}B})\)
. Setting 
\(A \mathrel {:=}B \mathrel {:=}Q_\lambda (S)\)
 gives 
\(K_\lambda (S){\mathop {\star }}K_\lambda (S) \subseteq \textrm{cl}({Q_\lambda (S){\mathop {\star }}Q_\lambda (S)}) \subseteq \textrm{cl}({Q_\lambda (S)}) = K_\lambda (S)\)
. 
\(\square \)

                
By Lemma 
2.6
, 
\(K_\lambda (S)\)
 is the least 
\(\lambda \)
-clonvex superset of 
S
.
For 
\(\lambda \in R\)
 and 
\(S\subseteq M\)
, we can stratify the set 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 as follows:

                  
Definition 2.7

                  
For any 
\(\lambda \in R\)
 and 
\(S\subseteq M\)
, we define 
\(Q_\lambda ^{(0)}(S):= S\)
, and for all integers 
\(n\ge 0\)
 we inductively define 
\(P_\lambda (S):= S{\mathop {\star }}S = \left\{ a{\mathop {\star }}b: a,b \in S \right\} \)
. and 
\(Q_\lambda ^{(n+1)}(S):= P_\lambda (Q_\lambda ^{(n)}(S))\)
.

                
                  
Fact 2.8

                  
For any 
\(\lambda \in R\)
 and 
\(S\subseteq M\)
,

                    

                      
\(Q_\lambda ^{(n)}(S) \subseteq Q_\lambda ^{(n+1)}(S)\)
 for all integers 
\(n\ge 0\)
 (noticing that 
\({\mathop {\star }}\)
 is idempotent),

                    

                    

                      
\(Q_\lambda ^{(n)}(S) = P_\lambda ^n(S)\)
, and

                    

                    

                      
\(\bigcup _{n=0}^\infty Q_\lambda ^{(n)}(S) = \bigcup _{n=0}^\infty P_\lambda ^n(S) = Q_\lambda (S)\)
.

                    

                    

                      
S
 is countable if and only if 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 is countable.

                    

                  

                
Next we give a general lemma from which many of the results of this section follow easily. This lemma will also be used in Part II. The idea is that the extrapolation operator 
\(x\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}y\)
 is linear in 
x
 and 
y
, e.g., 
\((ax)\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}(ay) = a(x\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}y)\)
. Since 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 is obtained by iterating this operation on 
S
, intuitively one expects that “
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 is linear in 
S
", i.e., 
\(Q_\lambda (xS) = xQ_\lambda (S)\)
, and perhaps also 
\(Q_\lambda (S+T) = Q_\lambda (S) + Q_\lambda (T)\)
 for sets 
S
, 
T
. Indeed this is true.

                  
Lemma 2.9

                  
\(Q_\lambda (xS+yT) = xQ_\lambda (S) + yQ_\lambda (T)\)
 for all 
\(\lambda ,x,y\in R\)
 and 
\(S,T\subseteq M\)
. If 
M
 also has a topology, then 
\(K_\lambda (xS+yT) = \textrm{cl}({xK_\lambda (S) + yK_\lambda (T)})\)
.

                
                  
Proof

                  
We have
$$\begin{aligned} xP_\lambda (S)  &   = \{x(a \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}b) \mid a, b \in S\} = \{(xa) \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}(xb) \mid a, b \in S\}\\  &   = \{w \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}z \mid w,z \in xS\} = P_\lambda (xS)\;. \end{aligned}$$
Thus by induction on 
n
, 
\(Q_\lambda ^{(n)}(xS) = xQ_\lambda ^{(n)}(S)\)
. Hence, by Fact 
2.8
, 
\(Q_\lambda (xS) = xQ_\lambda (S)\)
.

                  
Next, using Fact 
2.4
 with 
\(x=y=1\)
, observe that for any 
\(S,T \subseteq M\)
,
$$\begin{aligned} P_\lambda (S + T)&= \{(a+b)\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}(c+d) \mid a,c \in S {{\textrm{and}}} b,d \in T\}\\&= \{a \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}c + b\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}d \mid a,c \in S {{\textrm{and }}} b,d \in T\}\\&= P_\lambda (S) + P_\lambda (T)\;. \end{aligned}$$
Hence, by induction, 
\(P_\lambda ^n(S + T) = P_\lambda ^n(S) + P_\lambda ^n(T)\)
 and therefore, again by Fact 
2.8
, 
\(Q_\lambda (S+T) = Q_\lambda (S) + Q_\lambda (T)\)
. Combining this with the first part of the proof, we conclude the first part of the lemma.

                  
The second part follows from the first by taking the closure of both sides and using Lemma 
2.6
 and the fact that 
\(xC+yD \subseteq x\cdot \textrm{cl}({C}) + y\cdot \textrm{cl}({D})\subseteq \textrm{cl}({xC+yD})\)
 for any 
\(C,D\subseteq M\)
.

                  
\(\square \)

                
                  
Fact 2.10

                  
Let 
\({{g}:{M}\rightarrow {N}}\)
 be 
R
-linear. Then 
\(g(u{\mathop {\star }}v) = g(u){\mathop {\star }}g(v)\)
 for any 
\(\lambda \in R\)
 and 
\(u,v\in M\)
.

                
                  
Lemma 2.11

                  
Let 
\({{f}:{M}\rightarrow {N}}\)
 be an 
R
-affine map. Then 
\(f(Q_\lambda (S)) = Q_\lambda (f(S))\)
 for any 
\(\lambda \in R\)
 and 
\(S\subseteq M\)
. If 
M
 and 
N
 have topologies such that 
f
 is continuous, then 
\(\textrm{cl}({f(K_\lambda (S))}) = K_\lambda (f(S))\)
.

                
                  
Proof

                  
Let 
\({{g}:{M}\rightarrow {N}}\)
 be an 
R
-module homomorphism and let 
\(w\in N\)
 such that 
\(f(u) = g(u)+w\)
 for all 
\(u\in M\)
. Using Fact 
2.10
, 
\(P_\lambda (g(S)) = P_\lambda (\{g(u) \mid u \in S\}) = \{g(u)\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}g(v) \mid u,v \in S\} = \{g(u\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}v) \mid u,v \in S\} = g(P_\lambda (S))\)
. Hence, by induction, 
\(P_\lambda ^n(g(S)) = g(P_\lambda ^n(S))\)
, whence, by Fact 
2.8
, we have 
\(Q_\lambda (g(S)) = g(Q_\lambda (S))\)
.

                  
Using the above and Lemma 
2.9
, we now have 
\(f(Q_\lambda (S)) = g(Q_\lambda (S)) + w = Q_\lambda (g(S)) + w = Q_\lambda (g(S)) + Q_\lambda (\{w\}) = Q_\lambda (g(S)+w) = Q_\lambda (f(S))\)
. From this we get 
\(\textrm{cl}({f(K_\lambda (S))}) = \textrm{cl}({f(\textrm{cl}({Q_\lambda (S)}))}) = \textrm{cl}({f(Q_\lambda (S))}) = \textrm{cl}({Q_\lambda (f(S))}) = K_\lambda (f(S))\)
. 
\(\square \)

                
The following corollary helps to justify our arbitrary choice of 0 and 1 in the definition of 
\(Q_\lambda \)
.

                  
Corollary 2.12

                  
For any 
\(\lambda \in R\)
, 
\(S\subseteq R\)
, and 
\(a,b\in M\)
, 
\(\rho _{a,b}(Q_\lambda (S)) = Q_\lambda (\rho _{a,b}(S))\)
. In particular, 
\(\rho _{a,b}(Q_\lambda )\)
 is the 
\(\lambda \)
-convex hull of 
\(\{a,b\}\)
. If 
R
 and 
M
 have topologies such that 
\(\rho _{a,b}\)
 is continuous, then 
\(\textrm{cl}({\rho _{a,b}(K_\lambda (S))}) = K_\lambda (\rho _{a,b}(S))\)
.

                
In Part II we will need to consider a special case of Definition 
2.3
: Let 
V
 be any vector space over some field 
k
, and fix a 
k
-linear map 
\({{\Lambda }:{V}\rightarrow {V}}\)
. Then 
\(\Lambda \)
 naturally turns 
V
 into a 
k
[
x
]-module, where scalar multiplication is defined for all 
\(g\in k[x]\)
 and 
\(v\in V\)
 as 
\(gv:= (g(\Lambda ))(v)\)
 (see, for example, Jacobson [
23
, Chapter 3]). We denote this 
k
[
x
]-module by 
\(V_\Lambda \)
. We will only use the case where 
\(\lambda = x\in k[x]\)
, and thus we can identify 
\(\lambda \)
 with 
\(\Lambda \)
.

                  
Definition 2.13

                  
Let 
V
 be a vector space over a field 
k
, and let 
\({{\Lambda }:{V}\rightarrow {V}}\)
 be a 
k
-linear map. 

                    

                      
1.

                      
                        
For any vectors 
\(u,v\in V\)
, define 
\(u\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\Lambda }}v:= (I-\Lambda )u + \Lambda v = u + \Lambda (v - u)\)
.

                      
                    

                    

                      
2.

                      
                        
A set 
\(S\subseteq V\)
 is 
\(\Lambda \)
-convex
 iff 
\(u\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\Lambda }}v \in S\)
 whenever 
\(u,v\in S\)
.

                      
                    

                    

                      
3.

                      
                        
For any set of vectors 
\(S\subseteq V\)
, we define 
\(Q_\Lambda (S)\subseteq V\)
 as the least 
\(\Lambda \)
-convex superset of 
S
.

                      
                    

                  

                
The following is used in Section 
6.2.2
.

                  
Fact 2.14

                  
If 
\(\Lambda \)
 is invertible, 
V
 as in Definition 
2.13
, then for any 
\(x,y \in V\)
, we have 
\(x\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\Lambda }}(x{\mathop {\star }}_{\Lambda ^{-1}} y) = y\)
.

                
                  
Lemma 2.15

                  
Let 
U
 and 
V
 be vector spaces over some field 
k
, and let 
\({{A}:{U}\rightarrow {U}}\)
, 
\({{B}:{V}\rightarrow {V}}\)
, and 
\({{t}:{U}\rightarrow {V}}\)
 be 
k
-linear maps such that 
\(B \circ t = t \circ A\)
. For any 
\(S\subseteq U\)
, we have
$$\begin{aligned} t(Q_A(S)) = Q_B(t(S))\;. \end{aligned}$$

                
                  
Proof

                  
This is a special case of Lemma 
2.11
. The combined condition that 
t
 is linear and 
\(B \circ t = t \circ A\)
 is equivalent to 
t
 being a homomorphism of 
k
[
x
]-modules 
\(U_A\rightarrow V_B\)
. 
\(\square \)

                
The next fact can be seen by noticing that 
\(a\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}b = b{\mathop {\star }}_{1-\lambda } a\)
 for all 
\(a,b\in M\)
 and 
\(\lambda \in R\)
.

                  
Fact 2.16

                  
A subset of 
M
 is 
\(\lambda \)
-convex if and only if it is 
\((1-\lambda )\)
-convex. Thus 
\(Q_\lambda (S) = Q_{1-\lambda }(S)\)
 for any 
\(S\subseteq M\)
 and 
\(\lambda \in R\)
. If 
M
 has a topology, then 
\(K_\lambda (S) = K_{1-\lambda }(S)\)
.

                
The next lemma will be used in the proof of Theorems 
6.12
, 
6.13
, and Lemma 
10.2
.

                  
Lemma 2.17

                  
Suppose 
\(R=M\)
. Let 
\(\lambda ,\mu \in M\)
 and 
\(S\subseteq M\)
 be arbitrary. 

                    

                      
1.

                      
                        
If 
\(\mu \in Q_\lambda \)
, then 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 is 
\(\mu \)
-convex, and consequently, 
\(Q_\mu (S) \subseteq Q_\lambda (S)\)
;

                      
                    

                    

                      
2.

                      
                        
If 
\(\mu \in K_\lambda \)
, then 
\(K_\lambda (S)\)
 is 
\(\mu \)
-convex, and consequently, 
\(K_\mu (S) \subseteq K_\lambda (S)\)
 (assuming 
R
 and 
M
 have topologies).

                      
                    

                  

                
                  
Proof

                  
For part (1.), suppose 
\(\mu \in Q_\lambda \)
. Then for any 
\(a,b\in Q_\lambda (S)\)
,
$$ \begin{aligned}a\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\mu }}b&= \rho _{a,b}(\mu ) \in \rho _{a,b}(Q_\lambda ) \\  &\subseteq Q_\lambda (\rho _{a,b}(\{0,1\})) = Q_\lambda (\{a,b\}) \subseteq Q_\lambda (Q_\lambda (S)) = Q_\lambda (S)\;, \end{aligned}$$
where the first subset inclusion follows from Corollary 
2.12
 with 
\(S = \{0,1\}\)
. This shows that 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 is 
\(\mu \)
-convex. Similarly for part (2.), noting that 
\(\rho _{a,b}\)
 is continuous for all 
\(a,b\in M\)
. 
\(\square \)

                
2.2 
Special Case: 
\(R = M = \mathbb {C}\)

                        
In this subsection, we assume 
\(R = M = \mathbb {C}\)
 to briefly explore some of the geometry of 
\(\lambda \)
-convexity.

                  
Fact 2.18

                  
For every 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {C}\)
 and 
\(S\subseteq \mathbb {C}\)
, we have 
\(\overline{Q_\lambda (S)} = Q_{\overline{\lambda }}(\overline{S})\)
 and 
\(\overline{K_{\lambda }(S)} = K_{\overline{\lambda }}(\overline{S})\)
. Thus 
\(\overline{Q_\lambda } = Q_{\overline{\lambda }}\)
 and 
\(\overline{K_\lambda } = K_{\overline{\lambda }}\)
. In particular, for any 
\(\lambda \in {\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
 and 
\(S\subseteq \mathbb {C}\)
, 
\(K_\lambda (S)\)
 is the closure of the convex hull of 
S
. Thus if 
\(S\subseteq \mathbb {C}\)
 is closed, then for any fixed 
\(\lambda \in {\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
, we have that 
S
 is convex if and only if 
S
 is 
\(\lambda \)
-convex.

                
The geometric picture in Figure 
1
 of 
a
, 
b
 and 
\(a{\mathop {\star }}b\)
 is especially useful for constructions involving 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {C}\setminus \mathbb {R}\)
.
Fig. 1
Full size image
The 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
-affine transformation that takes 
\((0,1,\lambda ) \mapsto (a, b, a\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}b)\)
 preserves angles, so that the triangles 
\((0,1,\lambda )\)
 and 
\((a, b, a\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}b))\)
 are similar and have the same orientation
3 

                        
\(Q_{[x]}\)
 and Its Characterizations
In this section, we introduce the set 
\(Q_{[x]}\subseteq \mathbb {Z}[x]\)
 (Notation 
3.1
) and a characterization of it due to Pinch [
40
] (Lemma 
3.5
). 
\(Q_{[x]}\)
 plays a central role in the sequel, and we give another useful characterization of it later in the section (Theorem 
3.6
).

                
Notation 3.1

                
For any polynomials 
\(s,t\in \mathbb {Z}[x]\)
, we denote 
\((1-x)s + xt\)
 by 
\(s\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{[x]}}t\)
 (which is clearly also in 
\(\mathbb {Z}[x]\)
). Let 
\(Q_{[x]} \subseteq \mathbb {Z}[x]\)
 denote the least set of polynomials such that the constant polynomials 
\(0, 1 \in Q_{[x]}\)
, and for every 
\(s,t\in Q_{[x]}\)
, 
\(s\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{[x]}}t \in Q_{[x]}\)
.

              
Notation 
3.1
 is a notational convention according to Definition 
2.3
, where 
\(R:= M:= \mathbb {Z}[x]\)
 and 
\(\lambda := x \in \mathbb {Z}[x]\)
. We include brackets to emphasize the fact that 
x
 is a formal variable, not to be instantiated.

                
Fact 3.2

                
For any commutative ring 
R
 and 
\(\lambda \in R\)
, the evaluation map 
\(p\mapsto p(\lambda )\)
 is a ring homomorphism from 
\(\mathbb {Z}[x]\)
 into 
R
, in which 
\(1_{\mathbb {Z}[x]} \mapsto 1_R\)
 and 
\((s\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{[x]}}t)(\lambda ) = s(\lambda )\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}t(\lambda )\)
 for all 
\(s,t\in \mathbb {Z}[x]\)
.

              
The following formalizes the fact that 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 and 
\(Q_{[x]}\)
 are constructed by the same process to produce polynomials in 
\(\lambda \)
 or 
x
.

                
Fact 3.3

                
For any 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {C}\)
, 
\(Q_\lambda = \{ p(\lambda ) \mid p\in Q_{[x]} \}\)
.

              
To prove Theorem 
3.6
 of this section, we need the following lemma, which is known.
Footnote 
3

                
Lemma 3.4

                
For any natural number 
n
, the set 
\( \{ x^k(1-x)^{n-k} \mid k\in \mathbb {Z}\;\mathrel { \& }\;0 \le k \le n \}\)
 is linearly independent over 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
, and is thus a basis for the space of all polynomials in 
\(\mathbb {C}[x]\)
 of degree 
\(\le n\)
.

              
Pinch proved the following using a straightforward induction [
40
, Proposition 4, Corollary 4.1].

                
Lemma 3.5

                
(Pinch) Fix any integer 
\(n\ge 0\)
. For any polynomial 
\(p\in \mathbb {Z}[x]\)
, 
p
 is in 
\(Q_{[x]}^{(n)}\)
 if and only if there exist integers 
\(b_0,\ldots ,b_n\)
 such that 
\(0 \le b_k \le \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ k\end{array}}\right) \)
 for all 
\(0\le k\le n\)
 and
$$\begin{aligned} p(x) = \sum _{k=0}^n b_k x^k (1-x)^{n-k}\;. \end{aligned}$$

              
In the remainder of the section we prove a simple characterization of 
\(Q_{[x]}\)
 (Theorem 
3.6
, below) different from the characterization of Lemma 
3.5
 (and by extension, a new characterization of 
\(Q_\lambda \)
). This characterization will be used, via Lemma 
6.19
, to prove relative density results in Section 
6
.

                
Theorem 3.6

                
Let 
f
 be any polynomial in 
\(\mathbb {Z}[x]\)
. Then 
\(f\in Q_{[x]}\)
 if and only if either 
\(f=0\)
 or 
\(f=1\)
 or 
\(0<f(\lambda )<1\)
 for all 
\(0<\lambda <1\)
.

              
                
Corollary 3.7

                
\(Q_{[x]}\)
 and 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 are both closed under multiplication and the operator 
\(p\mapsto 1-p\)
, and 
\(Q_{[x]}\)
 is closed under composition.

              
                
Remark

                
Closure under composition uses the fact that 
\(\{f(0),f(1)\}\subseteq \{0,1\}\)
 by continuity, for any 
\(f\in Q_{[x]}\)
. 
\(\square \)

              
                
Corollary 3.8

                
\( Q_\lambda = \{0,1\} \cup \{ p(\lambda ) \mid p\in \mathbb {Z}[x] \;\mathrel { \& }\;0<p(\mu )<1 \text{ for } \text{ all } 0<\mu <1 \}\)
 for any 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {C}\)
.

              
                
Corollary 3.9

                
For every 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {C}\setminus \{0,1\}\)
, 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 contains arbitrarily long finite arithmetic progressions. In particular, 
\( \left\{ k\lambda ^n(1-\lambda )^n \mid k\in \mathbb {Z}\;\mathrel { \& }\;0\le k \le \left( {\begin{array}{c}2n\\ n\end{array}}\right) \right\} \subseteq Q_\lambda \)
 for every integer 
\(n\ge 0\)
.

              
                
Remark

                
When 
\(0< \lambda < 1\)
, the set 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is bounded, and hence it cannot contain an infinite arithmetic sequence. 
\(\square \)

              
Before proving Theorem 
3.6
, we need a definition and a few lemmas. We extend the definition of the binomial coefficient 
\(\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ k\end{array}}\right) \)
 in the usual way for all 
\(n,k\in \mathbb {Z}\)
 with 
\(n\ge 0\)
, namely, by defining 
\(\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ k\end{array}}\right) := 0\)
 if 
\(k<0\)
 or 
\(k>n\)
. Then the recurrence 
\(\left( {\begin{array}{c}n+1\\ k\end{array}}\right) = \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ k\end{array}}\right) + \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ k-1\end{array}}\right) \)
 holds for all such 
n
 and 
k
.

                
Definition 3.10

                
Let 
\(f\in \mathbb {C}[x]\)
 be any polynomial, and let 
n
 be any nonnegative integer such that 
\(\deg (f) \le n\)
. We let 
\(f^{(n)}_0,f^{(n)}_1,\ldots ,f^{(n)}_n \in \mathbb {C}\)
 denote the coordinates with respect to the basis of Lemma 
3.4
, i.e., the unique complex numbers such that 
\(f(x) = \sum _{k=0}^n f^{(n)}_k x^k(1-x)^{n-k}\)
 (cf. Lemma 
3.4
). We define 
\(f^{(n)}_k := 0\)
 for all 
\(k<0\)
 and 
\(k>n\)
.

              
The next two lemmas relate the 
\(f^{(n)}\)
-coefficients for different 
n
. The first lemma says that the 
\(f^{(n)}\)
-coefficients satisfy the same “Pascal’s triangle” recurrence as the binomial coefficients.

                
Lemma 3.11

                
Let 
f
 and 
n
 be as in Definition 
3.10
. Then for any 
\(k\in \mathbb {Z}\)
, 
\(f^{(n+1)}_k = f^{(n)}_k + f^{(n)}_{k-1}\)
.

              
                
Proof

                
This is clearly true for 
\(k<0\)
 and 
\(k>n+1\)
, since both sides are 0. Moreover, we have
$$\begin{aligned} f(x)&= \sum _{k=0}^n f^{(n)}_k x^k(1-x)^{n-k} = (x + (1 - x))\sum _{k=0}^n f^{(n)}_k x^k(1-x)^{n-k} \\&= \sum _{k=0}^n f^{(n)}_k x^{k+1}(1-x)^{n-k} + \sum _{k=0}^n f^{(n)}_k x^k(1-x)^{n+1-k} \\&= \sum _{k=1}^{n+1} f^{(n)}_{k-1} x^k(1-x)^{n+1-k} \\&\quad + \sum _{k=0}^n f^{(n)}_k x^k(1-x)^{n+1-k} = \sum _{k=0}^{n+1} \left( f^{(n)}_{k-1} + f^{(n)}_k\right) x^k(1-x)^{n+1-k}\;. \end{aligned}$$
Comparing coefficients with the equation 
\(f(x) = \sum _{k=0}^{n+1} f^{(n+1)}_k x^k(1-x)^{n+1-k}\)
, we see that 
\(f^{(n+1)}_k = f^{(n)}_{k-1} + f^{(n)}_k\)
 for all 
\(0\le k\le n+1\)
. 
\(\square \)

              
The next lemma extends the previous one in a natural way.

                
Lemma 3.12

                
Let 
\(f\in \mathbb {C}[x]\)
 be any polynomial, and let 
m
 be any natural number such that 
\(m \ge \deg (f)\)
. For any integers 
n
 and 
k
 such that 
\(n\ge m\)
,
$$\begin{aligned} f^{(n)}_k = \sum _{i=0}^m f^{(m)}_i\left( {\begin{array}{c}n-m\\ k-i\end{array}}\right) \;. \end{aligned}$$

              
                
Proof

                
We observe,
$$\begin{aligned} f(x)&= (x + (1-x))^{n-m}\sum \limits _{i=0}^m f_i^{(m)}x^i(1-x)^{m-i}\\&= \sum \limits _{j=0}^{n-m}\left( {\begin{array}{c}n-m\\ j\end{array}}\right) x^j(1-x)^{n-m-j}\sum \limits _{i=0}^m f_i^{(m)}x^i(1-x)^{m-i}\\&= \sum \limits _{k=0}^n\left[ \sum \limits _{i=0}^m f_i^{(m)}\left( {\begin{array}{c}n-m\\ k-i\end{array}}\right) \right] x^k(1-x)^{n-k}\;, \end{aligned}$$
where in the last equality we applied the change of variable 
\(k = i+j\)
. Comparing coefficients with 
\(f(x) = \sum _{k=0}^n f_k^{(n)} x^k(1-x)^{n-k}\)
, the result follows. 
\(\square \)

              
The next lemma forms the technical heart of the proof of Theorem 
3.6
. It relates a coefficient 
\(f^{(n)}_k\)
 of an 
\(f\in \mathbb {C}[x]\)
 with 
f
’s value at 
k
/
n
, for 
\(k/n\in {\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
 sufficiently far from 0 and 1.
Footnote 
4
 It is crucial in Lemma 
3.13
 that 
t
 is independent of 
n
. If 
k
/
n
 is too close to 0 or 1, we need a completely different technique, given in the second half of the proof of Lemma 
3.14
, below. Theorem 
3.6
 applies Lemma 
3.14
 only in the special case where 
\(f\in \mathbb {Z}[x]\)
 and 
\(0<f(\lambda )<1\)
 for all 
\(0<\lambda <1\)
, but the full power of Lemma 
3.14
 (which uses Lemma 
3.13
) is apparently needed for its inductive proof to work correctly.

                
Lemma 3.13

                
Let 
\(f\in \mathbb {C}[x]\)
 be any polynomial, and let 
\(d:= \deg (f)\)
. For every 
\(\varepsilon > 0\)
 there exists an integer 
\(t\ge d\)
 such that, for any positive integer 
n
 with 
\(n > 2t + 1\)
 and all integers 
k
 such that 
\(t< k < n-t\)
,
$$\begin{aligned} {\left| {f(k/n) - \frac{f^{(n)}_k(1-k/n)^d}{\left( {\begin{array}{c}n-d\\ k\end{array}}\right) }}\right| } \le \varepsilon \;. \end{aligned}$$

              
                
Proof

                
We can assume WLOG that 
\(f \ne 0\)
 (and thus 
\(d\ge 0\)
). Given integer 
\(n > 2d+1\)
 and 
k
 any integer such that 
\(d< k < n-d\)
, set 
\(\lambda := k/n\)
. Then 
\(d/n<\lambda <1-d/n\)
, and from Lemma 
3.12
, we have
$$\begin{aligned}&f^{(n)}_k = \sum _{i=0}^d f^{(d)}_i\left( {\begin{array}{c}n-d\\ k-i\end{array}}\right) \\&\quad = \left( {\begin{array}{c}n-d\\ k\end{array}}\right) \left( f^{(d)}_0 + f^{(d)}_1\frac{k}{n-d-k+1} + f^{(d)}_2\frac{k(k-1)}{(n-d-k+1)(n-d-k+2)} + \cdots \right) \\&\quad = \left( {\begin{array}{c}n-d\\ k\end{array}}\right) \sum _{i=0}^d f^{(d)}_i \prod _{j=1}^i \frac{k-j+1}{n-d-k+j} = \left( {\begin{array}{c}n-d\\ k\end{array}}\right) \sum _{i=0}^d f^{(d)}_i \prod _{j=1}^i \frac{\lambda -\frac{j-1}{n}}{1-\lambda -\frac{d-j}{n}}\;. \end{aligned}$$
We then have
$$\begin{aligned}&{\left| {f(\lambda ) - \frac{f^{(n)}_k(1-\lambda )^d}{\left( {\begin{array}{c}n-d\\ k\end{array}}\right) }}\right| } = {\left| {\sum _{i=0}^d f^{(d)}_i \lambda ^i(1-\lambda )^{d-i} - (1-\lambda )^d\sum _{i=0}^d f^{(d)}_i \prod _{j=1}^i \frac{\lambda -\frac{j-1}{n}}{1-\lambda -\frac{d-j}{n}}}\right| } \nonumber \\&\quad \le \sum _{i=0}^d{\left| {f^{(d)}_i}\right| }\cdot {\left| {\lambda ^i(1-\lambda )^{d-i} - (1-\lambda )^d\prod _{j=1}^i \frac{\lambda -\frac{j-1}{n}}{1-\lambda -\frac{d-j}{n}}}\right| } \nonumber \\&\quad = \sum _{i=0}^d{\left| {f^{(d)}_i}\right| }\left( \lambda ^i(1-\lambda )^{d-i}\right) {\left| {1 - \prod _{j=1}^i \left( \frac{\lambda -\frac{j-1}{n}}{\lambda }\right) \left( \frac{1-\lambda }{1-\lambda -\frac{d-j}{n}}\right) }\right| } \nonumber \\&\quad \le \sum _{i=0}^d{\left| {f^{(d)}_i}\right| }\cdot {\left| {1 - \prod _{j=1}^i \left( \frac{\lambda -\frac{j-1}{n}}{\lambda }\right) \left( \frac{1-\lambda }{1-\lambda -\frac{d-j}{n}}\right) }\right| }\;. \end{aligned}$$

                    (1)
                
The product 
\(\prod _{j=1}^i\cdots \)
 in (
1
) above is positive, all its factors being positive. If 
k
 and 
\(n-k\)
 are both large compared to 
d
, then it is also close to 1, but it may be less than or greater than 1, depending on 
\(\lambda \)
. We will bound it from above and below. Observe that, for 
\(1\le j \le d\)
,
$$\begin{aligned} 1- \frac{d}{k} = 1 - \frac{d}{\lambda n} < \frac{\lambda - \frac{j-1}{n}}{\lambda } \le 1 \end{aligned}$$
and, noting that 
\(1-\lambda> d/n > (d-j)/n\)
,
$$\begin{aligned} 1 \le \frac{1-\lambda }{1-\lambda -\frac{d-j}{n}} = \frac{1-\lambda -\frac{d-j}{n}+\frac{d-j}{n}}{1-\lambda -\frac{d-j}{n}}< 1 + \frac{d/n}{1-\lambda - d/n} = 1 + \frac{d}{n-k-d}\;. \end{aligned}$$
Letting 
\(M:= \sum _{i=0}^d {\left| {f^{(d)}_i}\right| }\)
, we see that (
1
) above is then less than or equal to
$$\begin{aligned} M\cdot \max&\left\{ 1 - \left( 1 - \frac{d}{k}\right) ^d,\;\left( 1 + \frac{d}{n - k - d}\right) ^d - 1\right\} \\&\le M \cdot \max \left\{ \left( 1 + \frac{d}{k}\right) ^d - 1,\;\left( 1 + \frac{d}{n-k - d}\right) ^d - 1\right\} \\&\le M \cdot \left( \max \left\{ \exp (d^2/k),\; \exp (d^2/(n-k-d))\right\} - 1 \right) \end{aligned}$$
(The first inequality follows from the Generalized Mean Inequality, or can be easily checked via the Binomial Theorem.) Now we just need to let 
\(t\ge d\)
 be large enough so that this quantity is at most 
\(\varepsilon \)
 when 
\(t< k < n-t\)
. Letting
$$\begin{aligned} t := {\Bigg \lceil {d + \frac{d^2}{\log \left( 1 + \frac{\varepsilon }{M}\right) }}\Bigg \rceil } \end{aligned}$$
suffices. (Note that 
\(M>0\)
 (because 
\(f\ne 0\)
) and that 
t
 depends only on 
f
 and 
\(\varepsilon \)
 and not on 
n
.) 
\(\square \)

              
                
Lemma 3.14

                
Let 
\(f\in \mathbb {R}[x]\)
 be a polynomial such that 
\(f(\lambda ) > 0\)
 for all 
\(0< \lambda < 1\)
. Then for all sufficiently large 
\(n\ge \deg (f)\)
, 
\(f^{(n)}_0, \ldots , f^{(n)}_n \ge 0\)
.

              
                
Proof

                
We use induction on 
\(d:= \deg (f)\)
. If 
\(d = 0\)
, then 
f
(
x
) is some constant 
\(c>0\)
. Then for any 
\(n\ge 0\)
 and 
\(0\le i\le n\)
, we have 
\(f^{(n)}_i = c\left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ i\end{array}}\right) > 0\)
, and so any 
\(n \ge 0\)
 suffices. Now suppose 
\(d > 0\)
 and the lemma holds for all polynomials of degree less than 
d
. If 
\(f(0) = 0\)
, then 
\(f(x) = xg(x)\)
 for some polynomial 
\(g\in \mathbb {R}[x]\)
 of degree 
\(d-1\)
 such that 
\(g(\lambda )>0\)
 for all 
\(0<\lambda <1\)
. By the inductive hypothesis, for all sufficiently large 
\(m \ge d-1\)
, 
\(g^{(m)}_0,\ldots ,g^{(m)}_m\ge 0\)
. Then 
\(f^{(m+1)}_0 = f(0) = 0\)
, and for all 
\(1\le k\le m+1\)
, we see that 
\(f^{(m+1)}_k = g^{(m)}_{k-1} \ge 0\)
. Thus the lemma holds for 
f
 witnessed by 
\(n:= m+1\)
.

                
A similar argument applies if 
\(f(1) = 0\)
: we get 
\(f(x) = (1-x)h(x)\)
 for some 
h
 of degree 
\(d-1\)
 satisfying 
\(h(\lambda )>0\)
 for all 
\(0<\lambda <1\)
. Letting 
m
 be sufficiently large such that 
\(h^{(m)}_0,\ldots ,h^{(m)}_m \ge 0\)
, we have 
\(f^{(m+1)}_k = h^{(m)}_k \ge 0\)
 for all 
\(0\le k\le m\)
, and furthermore, 
\(f^{(m+1)}_{m+1} = f(1) = 0\)
.

                
We can now assume (by the continuity of 
f
) that 
\(f(0) > 0\)
 and 
\(f(1) > 0\)
. By the compactness of 
\({\left[ {0,1}\right] }\)
, there exists 
\(\varepsilon > 0\)
 such that 
\(f(\lambda ) \ge \varepsilon \)
 for all 
\(0\le \lambda \le 1\)
. Now given 
f
 and 
\(\varepsilon \)
, let 
\(t\ge d\)
 be the number obtained from Lemma 
3.13
. Then for all 
\(n >2t+1\)
 and all 
k
 such that 
\(t< k < n - t\)
, that lemma implies 
\(f^{(n)}_k \ge 0\)
, because 
\(f^{(n)}_k(1-k/n)^d/\left( {\begin{array}{c}n-d\\ k\end{array}}\right) \ge f(k/n) - \varepsilon \ge 0\)
.

                
It remains to show that if 
n
 is sufficiently large, then 
\(f^{(n)}_k \ge 0\)
 and 
\(f^{(n)}_{n-k} \ge 0\)
 for all 
\(0\le k\le t\)
. To this end, it suffices to prove the following statement for all 
k
, which we do by induction on 
k
:

                  
There exists an integer 
\(n_k \ge d\)
 such that, for all integers 
\(n \ge n_k\)
, 
\(f^{(n)}_k \ge \varepsilon \)
 and 
\(f^{(n)}_{n-k} \ge \varepsilon \)
.

                
For 
\(k=0\)
, we have 
\(f^{(n)}_0 {=} f(0) \ge \varepsilon \)
 and 
\(f^{(n)}_n {=} f(1) \ge \varepsilon \)
 for all 
\(n{\ge } d\)
, and so we can set 
\(n_0{:=} d\)
. Now let 
\(k>0\)
, and assume the statement holds for 
\(k-1\)
. Let 
\(y:= f^{(n_{k-1})}_k\)
. (It could be that 
\(y<0\)
.) By Lemma 
3.11
,
$$\begin{aligned} f^{(n_{k-1}+1)}_k = f^{(n_{k-1})}_k + f^{(n_{k-1})}_{k-1} \ge y + \varepsilon \;. \end{aligned}$$
Similarly,
$$\begin{aligned} f^{(n_{k-1}+2)}_k = f^{(n_{k-1}+1)}_k + f^{(n_{k-1}+1)}_{k-1} \ge y + 2\varepsilon \;, \end{aligned}$$
and so on, yielding, for all 
\(\ell \ge n_{k-1}\)
,
$$\begin{aligned} f^{(\ell )}_k \ge y + (\ell - n_{k-1})\varepsilon \;. \end{aligned}$$
Thus 
\(f^{(\ell )}_k \ge \varepsilon \)
 for all 
\(\ell \)
 large enough. By a similar argument, letting 
\(z:= f^{(n_{k-1})}_{n_{k-1}-k}\)
, we get
$$\begin{aligned} f^{(m)}_{m-k} \ge z + (m-n_{k-1})\varepsilon \end{aligned}$$
for all 
\(m \ge n_{k-1}\)
. Now setting 
\(n_k:= {\Bigg \lceil {\max \left\{ n_{k-1},\;n_{k-1} + 1 - y/\varepsilon ,\;n_{k-1} + 1 - z/\varepsilon \right\} }\Bigg \rceil }\)
, the statement holds for 
k
. We finally have that 
\(f^{(n)}_0, \ldots , f^{(n)}_n \ge 0\)
 for all 
\(n\ge \max \{2t+2,n_t\}\)
. 
\(\square \)

              
                
Proof of Theorem 3.6

                
For the “only if” part: If 
\(f\in Q_{[x]}\)
, then by Lemma 
3.5
, there exist integers 
\(n\ge 0\)
 and 
\(b_0,\ldots ,b_n\)
 such that 
\(0\le b_k\le \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ k\end{array}}\right) \)
 for all 
\(0\le k\le n\)
 and 
\(f(x) = \sum _{k=0}^n b_k x^k(1-x)^{n-k}\)
. (In fact, 
\(b_k = f^{(n)}_k\)
 by Lemma 
3.4
.) If 
\(b_k = 0\)
 for all 
k
, then 
\(f=0\)
. If 
\(b_k = \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ k\end{array}}\right) \)
 for all 
k
, then 
\(f = (x+(1-x))^n = 1\)
 by the Binomial Theorem. Otherwise, some 
\(b_i>0\)
, which implies 
\(f(\lambda ) > 0\)
 for all 
\(0<\lambda <1\)
; also, some 
\(b_j < \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ j\end{array}}\right) \)
, which similarly implies 
\(f(\lambda ) < 1\)
 for all 
\(0<\lambda <1\)
.

                
Now for the “if” part. If 
\(f=0\)
 or 
\(f{=}1\)
, then 
\(f\in Q_{[x]}^{(0)}\)
, and we are done. Otherwise, if 
\(0<f(\lambda )<1\)
 for all 
\(0<\lambda <1\)
, then by Lemma 
3.14
, there exists 
\(n'\)
 such that for all 
\(n\ge n'\)
 and 
\(0\le k\le n\)
, we have 
\(f^{(n)}_k \ge 0\)
. Letting 
\(g:= 1-f\)
, we have 
\(0<g(\lambda )\)
 for all 
\(0<\lambda <1\)
, and so also by Lemma 
3.14
, there exists 
\(n''\)
 such that 
\(g^{(n)}_k \ge 0\)
 for all 
\(n\ge n''\)
 and 
\(0\le k\le n\)
. Now let 
\(n:= \max \{n',n''\}\)
. From the Binomial Theorem,
$$\begin{aligned} 1 = (x + (1-x))^n = \sum _{k=0}^n \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ k\end{array}}\right) x^k(1-x)^{n-k}\;, \end{aligned}$$
viewed as a polynomial in 
x
. Then
$$\begin{aligned} g(x) = 1 - f(x) = \sum _{k=0}^n\left[ \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ k\end{array}}\right) -f^{(n)}_k\right] x^k(1-x)^{n-k}\;. \end{aligned}$$
Comparing coefficients, we have 
\(0\le g^{(n)}_k = \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ k\end{array}}\right) -f^{(n)}_k\)
, and thus 
\(0\le f^{(n)}_k \le \left( {\begin{array}{c}n\\ k\end{array}}\right) \)
, for all 
\(0\le k\le n\)
.

                
We can now apply Lemma 
3.5
 again (letting 
\(b_k:= f^{(n)}_k\)
) to put 
f
 into 
\(Q_{[x]}^{(n)}\)
 and be done, provided 
\(f^{(n)}_0,\ldots ,f^{(n)}_n\)
 are all integers.

                
This is true, and one way to see it is as follows: We show by induction on 
n
 that for any polynomial 
\(f(x) = \sum _{k=0}^n b_k x^k(1-x)^{n-k} \in \mathbb {Z}[x]\)
, we have 
\(b_k \in \mathbb {Z}\)
. For 
\(n = 0\)
, we have 
\(f(x) = b_0 \in \mathbb {Z}\)
 since 
\(f(x)\in \mathbb {Z}[x]\)
. Now assume 
\(n>0\)
 and the result is true for 
\(n-1\)
. Suppose 
\(f(x) = \sum _{k=0}^{n} b_k x^k (1-x)^{n-k}\in \mathbb {Z}[x]\)
. The 
\(k = n\)
 term is 
\(b_nx^n\)
, and thus 
\(b_n = f(1) \in \mathbb {Z}\)
, since 
\(f(x) \in \mathbb {Z}[x]\)
. Let
$$\begin{aligned} g(x):= \sum _{k=0}^{n-1}b_kx^k(1-x)^{n-1-k} = \frac{1}{1-x} \sum _{k=0}^{n-1}b_kx^k(1-x)^{n-k} = \frac{f(x) - b_nx^n}{1-x}. \end{aligned}$$
Clearly, 
\(f(x) - b_n x^n \in \mathbb {Z}[x]\)
 and 
\(f(x) - b_n x^n = (1-x)g(x)\)
, which implies 
\(g(x)\in \mathbb {Z}[x]\)
. Thus by the inductive hypothesis, 
\(b_k \in \mathbb {Z}\)
 for 
\(0 \le k < n\)
 as well. 
\(\square \)

              
4 
Discreteness of 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 for a Class of Algebraic Integers 
\(\lambda \)

                     
In this section, we prove a result (Theorem 
4.12
, below) that gives some sufficient conditions for 
\(Q_\lambda \subseteq \mathbb {C}\)
 to be discrete for certain algebraic integers 
\(\lambda \)
, including some (e.g., 
\(1+\varphi \)
) not belonging to any discrete subring of 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
. In fact, all cases we currently know of where 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is discrete follow from Theorem 
4.12
. As we will see, if 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is discrete, then it is also closed, whence 
\(Q_\lambda = K_\lambda \)
. Thus all our results apply to 
\(K_\lambda \)
 as well.
A 
Pisot-Vijayaraghavan number
 (or 
PV number
 for short) is an algebraic integer 
\(\alpha > 1\)
 whose Galois conjugates 
\(\alpha '\)
 (other than 
\(\alpha \)
) all lie inside the unit disk in 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
, i.e., satisfy 
\(|\alpha '| < 1\)
. This notion can be relaxed to allow for non-real 
\(\alpha \)
 by excluding both 
\(\alpha \)
 and its complex conjugate 
\(\overline{\alpha }\)
 from the norm requirement. We need a stronger definition.

                
Definition 4.1

                
We call an algebraic integer 
\(\alpha \in \mathbb {C}\)
 a 
strong PV number
 iff its Galois conjugates, other than 
\(\alpha \)
 and 
\(\overline{\alpha }\)
, all lie in the unit interval 
\({\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
. In this case, we also say that 
\(\alpha \)
 
is sPV
. We say that a strong PV number 
\(\alpha \)
 is 
trivial
 if it has no conjugates other than 
\(\alpha \)
 and 
\(\overline{\alpha }\)
 (i.e., no conjugates in 
\({\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
). Otherwise, 
\(\alpha \)
 is 
nontrivial
.

              
Nontrivial strong PV numbers include 
\(1+\varphi \)
 and 
\(2+\sqrt{2}\)
, and there are infinitely many real, irrational—hence nontrivial—strong PV numbers (Corollary 
5.1
, below). Every strong PV number greater than 1 is a PV number, but not conversely; for example, 
\(\varphi \)
 and 
\(1+\sqrt{2}\)
 are PV numbers but not sPV. Theorem 
4.12
 implies that 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is discrete for all strong PV numbers 
\(\lambda \)
. This result does not extend to all PV numbers; for example, 
\(K_\varphi = K_{1+\sqrt{2}} = \mathbb {R}\)
.

                
Fact 4.2

                
If 
\(\alpha \)
 is sPV, then so are 
\(\overline{\alpha }\)
, 
\(1-\alpha \)
, and 
\(1-\overline{\alpha }\)
; furthermore, 
\(\alpha \notin {\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
.

              
The trivial strong PV numbers come in two types:

                
Fact 4.3

                
Every trivial sPV number is of one of the following two types: 

                  

                    
1.

                    
                      
The strong PV numbers of degree 1 coincide with the integers.

                    
                  

                  

                    
2.

                    
                      
All non-real quadratic integers are (trivial) sPV. These numbers coincide with the non-real members of discrete subrings of 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
 and are of the form either 
\(a+ib\sqrt{n}\)
 or 
\(a+b(1+i\sqrt{4n-1})/2\)
, for integers 
a
, 
b
, 
n
 with 
\(b\ne 0\)
 and 
\(n>0\)
.

                    
                  

                

              
Pinch [
40
] proved that if 
\(\lambda \)
 is a real sPV number, then 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is discrete ([
40
, Proposition 12]). His proof also generalizes in a straightforward way to complex 
\(\lambda \)
. That result in turn leads to an alternative route to proving discreteness. However, this more analytic technique does not make the relationship between extrapolation and quasicrystals as clear as the proof we present here. Theorem 
4.12
, below, substantially strengthens Pinch’s result, and leads to sharper results about the nature of 
\(Q_\lambda \)
. Its proof here was found independently of Pinch’s paper [
40
]; similarly, the connections we show to cut-and-project schemes were found independently of the results in [
8
], which applied to one specific 
\(\lambda \)
. These explicitly show the relationship between extrapolation and Meyer sets. We reserve the alternative approach (which has other interesting aspects) for another publication.
4.1 
Meyer Sets and Cut-and-Project Schemes
Before stating and proving Theorem 
4.12
, we recall some concepts from discrete geometry, particularly concepts relating to ordered but aperiodic point sets in Euclidean space. The theory of such sets has gained intense interest recently, following the discovery of so-called “quasicrystals”—materials whose atomic arrangement shares many properties of crystals (e.g., sharp spikes in X-ray diffraction patterns), but—unlike true crystals—lack translational symmetry. Much of the mathematical theory of the corresponding point sets is due to Meyer [
35
, 
36
], and it also relates closely to aperiodic tilings of the plane [
12
, 
19
, 
39
]. This section draws somewhat from the recent exposition of Baake & Grimm [
6
] as well as papers by Moody [
37
]. Also consulted are some related papers by Lagarias [
25
, 
26
]. We do not need to present the concepts in their full generality. (In the following definition, 
\(B_r(x)\)
 denotes the closed ball of radius 
r
 centered at 
\(x \in X\)
.)

                  
Definition 4.4

                  
Let 
X
 be any metric space, and let 
S
 be any subset of 
X
 with at least two elements.

                    

                      
S
 is 
uniformly discrete
 iff there exists an 
\(r>0\)
 such that 
\(B_r(x) \cap B_r(y) = \emptyset \)
 for all distinct 
\(x,y\in S\)
. The supremum of the set of such 
r
 is the 
packing radius
 of 
S
, denoted 
\({\mu _p }(S)\)
.

                    

                    

                      
S
 is 
relatively dense
 (in 
X
) iff there exists an 
\(R>0\)
 such that 
\(\bigcup _{x\in S} B_R(x) = X\)
. The infimum of the set of such 
R
 is the 
covering radius
 of 
S
 (in 
X
), denoted 
\({\mu _c }(S)\)
.

                    

                    

                      
S
 is a 
Delone set
 (in 
X
) iff it is both uniformly discrete and relatively dense (in 
X
).

                    

                  

                
We will use the following fact a couple of times in the proof of Theorem 
4.12
. Stronger statements are possible, but this is all we need.

                  
Fact 4.5

                  
If 
\(A\subseteq \mathbb {R}^n\)
 is uniformly discrete and 
\(B\subseteq \mathbb {R}^n\)
 is bounded, then 
\(A\cap B\)
 is finite.

                
The next definition is not Meyer’s original definition, but was shown equivalent to it by Lagarias [
25
].

                  
Definition 4.6

                  
A subset 
\(S\subseteq \mathbb {R}^n\)
 is a 
Meyer set
 iff 
S
 is relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {R}^n\)
 and 
\(S-S\)
 is uniformly discrete.

                
There are many equivalent characterizations of Meyer sets; see, for example, Moody [
37
, Theorem 9.1]. All Meyer sets are Delone sets, but Meyer sets have additional properties, such as finite local complexity, not shared by all Delone sets. Other properties of interest include repetivity, aperiodicity, and diffractivity
Footnote 
5
. For definitions, see [
6
, 
37
].
One way of producing aperiodic Meyer sets is through a 
cut-and-project scheme
. Our definition here follows Baake and Grimm [
6
] with some minor alterations. Recall that if 
\({{f}:{X}\rightarrow {Y}}\)
 is some function with domain 
X
, and 
A
 is any subset of 
X
, then we let 
\(f|_A\)
 denote the function 
f
 restricted to domain 
A
.

                  
Definition 4.7

                  
A 
cut-and-project scheme
 is a triple 
\((\mathbb {R}^n,H,\mathcal {L})\)
, where

                    

                      
H
 is a locally compact abelian (topological) group,

                    

                    

                      
n
 is a positive integer,

                    

                    

                      
\(\mathcal {L}\)
 is a discrete subgroup of 
\(\mathbb {R}^n \times H\)
 such that the quotient group 
\((\mathbb {R}^n \times H)/\mathcal {L}\)
 is compact,

                    

                    

                      
letting 
\({{\pi _{\textit{int}}}:{\mathbb {R}^n \times H}\rightarrow {H}}\)
 and 
\({{\pi }:{\mathbb {R}^n \times H}\rightarrow {\mathbb {R}^n}}\)
 be the two canonical projection maps, 

                        

                          
1.

                          
                            
\(\pi _{\textit{int}}(\mathcal {L})\)
 is dense in 
H
, and

                          
                        

                        

                          
2.

                          
                            
\(\pi |_\mathcal {L}\)
 is one-to-one.

                          
                        

                      

                    

                  
H
 is called the 
internal space
 and 
\(\mathbb {R}^n\)
 the 
physical space
 of the cut-and-project scheme. We write 
\(L = \pi (\mathcal {L})\)
.

                
The mapping 
\({\mathop {*}}: L \rightarrow H\)
 specified by 
\(x \mapsto x^{{\mathop {*}}}:= \pi _{\textit{int}}((\pi |_{\mathcal {L}})^{-1}(x))\)
 is called the 
star-map
 or 
\({\mathop {*}}\)
-map
Footnote 
6
. We then write 
\(L^{{\mathop {*}}} = \pi _{\textit{int}}(\mathcal {L})\)
. We may interpret 
\(\mathcal {L}\)
 as 
\(\{(x,x^{{\mathop {*}}}) \mid x \in L\}\)
, with 
\(\pi \)
 projecting onto the first coordinate and 
\(\pi _{\textit{int}}\)
 onto the second. This is called the 
diagonal embedding
, one form of the 
Minkowski embedding
 [
6
].

                  
Definition 4.8

                  
Fix a cut-and-project scheme 
\(\mathcal {M}= (\mathbb {R}^n,H,\mathcal {L})\)
. A 
window
 of 
\(\mathcal {M}\)
 is any relatively compact
Footnote 
7
 subset 
\(W\subseteq H\)
 with nonempty interior. The 
projection set
 of 
W
 is defined
Footnote 
8
 as 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(W):= \{x \in L \mid x^{{\mathop {*}}} \in W\}\)
. A set 
\(M\subseteq \mathbb {R}^n\)
 is a 
model set
 (of 
\(\mathcal {M}\)
) iff 
\(M = \mathord {\curlywedge }(W)\)
 for some window 
W
.

                
All model sets are Delone sets. Model sets can also be used to characterize Meyer sets. Citing work of Meyer [
35
] and Lagarias [
25
] as well as his own work, Moody [
37
] gives various characterizations of Meyer sets, one of which is the following:

                  
Fact 4.9

                  
([
37
]) Let 
S
 be a relatively dense subset of 
\(\mathbb {R}^n\)
. Then 
S
 is a Meyer set (by Definition 
4.6
) if and only if 
S
 is a subset of a model set.

                
In this paper, 
H
 will always be 
\(\mathbb {R}^m\)
 (for some positive 
m
) with the usual vector addition, and 
\(\mathcal {L}\)
 will always be an integer lattice spanning 
\(\mathbb {R}^m\times \mathbb {R}^n \cong \mathbb {R}^{m+n}\)
.

                  
Remark

                  
Another means of generating Delone and Meyer sets is via substitution and inflation (see Lagarias & Wang [
27
]). 
\(\square \)

                
We end this subsection with a standard result about cut-and-project schemes used to show aperiodicity of the corresponding model sets.

                  
Lemma 4.10

                  
Let 
\(\mathcal {M}= (\mathbb {R}^n,\mathbb {R}^m,\mathcal {L})\)
 be a cut-and-project scheme with internal space 
\(\mathbb {R}^m\)
 and associated projectors 
\(\pi \)
 and 
\(\pi _{\textit{int}}\)
 as in Definition 
4.7
. If 
\(\pi _{\textit{int}}|_\mathcal {L}\)
 is finite-to-one, then no model set of 
\(\mathcal {M}\)
 intersects any arithmetic progression at infinitely many points.

                
                  
Proof

                  
Let 
\(M = \mathord {\curlywedge }(W)\)
 be a model set with corresponding window 
\(W\subseteq \mathbb {R}^m\)
 as in Definition 
4.8
. Let 
A
 be an arithmetic progression in 
\(\mathbb {R}^n\)
. Since 
\(\pi (\mathcal {L})\)
 is an additive subgroup of 
\(\mathbb {R}^n\)
, we find that 
\(B:= A\cap \pi (\mathcal {L})\)
 is an arithmetic subprogression of 
A
. Moreover, 
\(M\cap A = M\cap B\)
, because 
\(M\subseteq L = \pi (\mathcal {L})\)
. By linearity, the image 
\(B^{{\mathop {*}}}\)
 of 
B
 under the star-map is also an arithmetic progression. However, 
\(B^{{\mathop {*}}}\subseteq W\)
 (by definition of 
M
). But 
W
 is bounded, and hence 
\(B^{{\mathop {*}}}\)
 is finite. Since 
\(\pi _{\textit{int}}\)
 is finite-to-one, 
\(A\cap \pi (\mathcal {L}) = B = \pi |_{\mathcal {L}}(\pi _{\textit{int}}^{-1}(B^{{\mathop {*}}}))\)
 must also be finite. 
\(\square \)

                
We explore the connection between cut-and-project schemes and 
\(\lambda \)
-convex sets in the following section, below. As a warm-up, we have the following proposition, which is an easy corollary to Corollary 
3.8
:

                  
Proposition 4.11

                  
For any algebraic integer 
\(\lambda \)
 of degree 
\(d>0\)
,
$$ \begin{aligned} Q_\lambda \subseteq \{0,1\} \cup \{ p(\lambda ) \mid p\in \mathbb {Z}[x] \;\mathrel { \& }\;\deg (p)< d \end{aligned}$$
$$ \begin{aligned} \;\mathrel { \& }\;0<p(\mu )<1 \text{ for } \text{ all } 0<\mu <1 \text{ conjugate } \text{ to } \lambda \}\;. \end{aligned}$$

                
                  
Proof

                  
Let 
\(m\in \mathbb {Z}[x]\)
 be the minimal (monic) polynomial of 
\(\lambda \)
 of degree 
d
. Corollary 
3.8
 says that 
\( Q_\lambda = \{0,1\} \cup \{ p(\lambda ) \mid p\in \mathbb {Z}[x] \;\mathrel { \& }\;0<p(\mu )<1 \text{ for } \text{ all } 0<\mu <1 \}\)
. Since 
m
 is monic, we can write any 
\(p\in \mathbb {Z}[x]\)
 as 
\(p = qm+r\)
 for some 
\(q,r\in \mathbb {Z}[x]\)
 with 
\(\deg (r) < d\)
. Moreover, if 
\(\mu \)
 is conjugate to 
\(\lambda \)
, then 
\(p(\mu ) = r(\mu )\)
, which means that we can restrict the degree of the polynomial in the set-former to be less than 
d
 by substituting 
r
 for 
p
. 
\(\square \)

                
We will see below that if 
\(\lambda \)
 is an sPV number, then the right-hand side of Proposition 
4.11
 is discrete. In fact, it is the model set of a cut-and-project scheme.
4.2 
Connection with Cut-and-Project Sets and Discreteness
The following is the main theorem of this section:

                  
Theorem 4.12

                  
(Discreteness Theorem) If 
\(\lambda \)
 is sPV, then 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 is uniformly discrete for any finite set 
\(S\subseteq \mathbb {Q}(\lambda )\)
. Moreover, 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 is a subset of a model set.

                
If 
\(\lambda \)
 belongs to some discrete subring 
D
 of 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
 (which must then be uniformly discrete), then 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is discrete for an “easy” reason: 
\(Q_\lambda \subseteq D\)
. The Discreteness Theorem gives us infinitely many examples of values of 
\(\lambda \)
 such that 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is discrete but 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
 is not.
To denote the model set containing 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
, we use the following notation generalizing that of Berman & Moody [
8
]:

                  
Definition 4.13

                  
Let 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {C}\setminus {\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
 be an algebraic integer and let 
\(\mu _0,\ldots ,\mu _{k-1}\)
 be the conjugates of 
\(\lambda \)
 that are in 
\({\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
. For 
\(x\in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
, let 
\(x^{{\mathop {*}}}\)
 denote the vector of conjugates 
\((x'_0,\dots ,x'_{k-1}) \in \mathbb {R}^k\)
, where 
\(x'_i\)
 is the 
\({{i}^{\textit{th}}}\)
 conjugate of 
x
, i.e., the image of 
x
 under the ring isomorphism 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\rightarrow \mathbb {Z}[\mu _i]\)
 sending 
\(\lambda \)
 to 
\(\mu _i\)
, for all 
\(0\le i < k\)
. Let 
P
 be any interval of 
\(\mathbb {R}^k\)
, defined as 
\(P:= {\left[ {\ell _0,h_0}\right] }\times \cdots \times {\left[ {\ell _{k-1},h_{k-1}}\right] }\)
, where for 
\(0 \le i < k\)
, we have 
\(\ell _i, h_i \in \mathbb {R}\)
 and 
\(\ell _i < h_i\)
. We define
$$\begin{aligned} \Sigma ^{(\lambda )}(P) := \{ x\in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \mid x^{{\mathop {*}}} \in P\}\;. \end{aligned}$$

                    (2)
                
If 
\(k=0\)
, then we take 
\(x^{{\mathop {*}}}\)
 to be the empty tuple for every 
\(x\in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
, and we take 
P
 to be the singleton set containing this tuple, whence 
\(\Sigma ^{(\lambda )}(P) = \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
.

                
For any set 
\(S \subseteq \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
, let 
\(S^{{\mathop {*}}}:= \{s^{{\mathop {*}}} \mid s \in S\}\)
 denote the image of 
S
 under the mapping 
\({{{\mathop {*}}}:{\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]}\rightarrow {\mathbb {Z}[\mu _0]\times \cdots \times \mathbb {Z}[\mu _{k-1}]}}\)
 defined above. When we use Definition 
4.13
, we generally assume 
P
 contains some set 
\(S'\)
 of conjugates of a finite set 
S
. Furthermore, if 
\(S = \{0,1\}\)
, it turns out that 
\(P = {\left[ {0,1}\right] }^{\times k}\)
 is the appropriate choice. More generally, the proof of the next theorem relies crucially on the rectilinear nature of 
P
; for other choices of window, the proof fails because the conjugates 
\(\mu _i\)
 (
\(0 \le i \le k\)
) of 
\(\lambda \)
 are pairwise distinct. The interpolation argument in the theorem below would not work for, say, the convex hull of the points 
\(S^{{\mathop {*}}}\)
. (See footnote 10 on page 24.) Also, although here we are assuming 
\(S \subseteq \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
, we will see in the proof of Theorem 
4.12
 that some choice for 
P
 is sufficient for proving that 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 is uniformly discrete for any finite 
\(S \subseteq \mathbb {Q}[\lambda ]\)
.
The key result is that 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 is always contained in a set of the form 
\(\Sigma ^{(\lambda )}(P)\)
.

                  
Theorem 4.14

                  
For any algebraic integer 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {C}\setminus {\left[ {0,1}\right] }\)
 and any finite 
\(S \subseteq \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
, let 
P
 be any interval of 
\(\mathbb {R}^k\)
—as defined in Definition 
4.13
—that includes 
\(S^{{\mathop {*}}}\)
. Then
$$\begin{aligned} Q_\lambda (S) \subseteq \Sigma ^{(\lambda )}(P). \end{aligned}$$

                    (3)
                

                
                  
Proof

                  
For convenience, we fix 
\(\lambda \)
 and write 
\(\Sigma (P)\)
 for 
\(\Sigma ^{(\lambda )}(P)\)
. First we observe that 
\(S \subseteq \Sigma (P).\)
 We now show that 
\(\Sigma (P)\)
 is closed under 
\({\mathop {\star }}_\lambda \)
. Let 
\(x, y \in \Sigma (P)\)
. Then the respective vectors of conjugates 
\(x^{{\mathop {*}}}\)
 and 
\(y^{{\mathop {*}}}\)
 are contained in 
P
. Now let 
\(z:= x \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}y\)
. The 
\({{i}^{\textit{th}}}\)
 coordinate of 
\(z^{{\mathop {*}}}\)
 is 
\(z'_i = (1-\mu _i)x'_i + \mu _i y'_i\)
. But by definition, 
\(\mu _i \in {\left[ {0,1}\right] }\)
 for each 
i
 between 0 and 
\(k-1\)
. We conclude that 
\(\ell _i \le (1-\mu _i)x'_i + \mu _i y'_i \le h_i\)
, so that 
\(z'_i \in [\ell _i,h_i]\)
 for all 
i
. Clearly, 
\(z \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
, and hence 
\(z \in \Sigma (P)\)
, so 
\(\Sigma (P)\)
 is closed under 
\(\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}\)
 as claimed. It therefore contains the closure of 
S
 under 
\(\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}\)
, i.e., 
\(\Sigma (P) \supseteq Q_\lambda (S)\)
. 
\(\square \)

                
The proof of the main theorem of this section now readily follows from known results on cut-and-project schemes.

                  
Proof of the Discreteness Theorem (Theorem 4.12)

                  
It is well known that sets of the form 
\(\Sigma ^{(\lambda )}(P)\)
 are uniformly discrete. This is easily seen by expressing it in terms of a cut-and-project scheme, as follows. Let 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {C}\)
 be an sPV number (hence algebraic integer) of degree 
d
. Let 
\(F = \mathbb {R}\)
 or 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
, according to whether 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {R}\)
 or 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {C}\setminus \mathbb {R}\)
, respectively, and define the mapping 
\({\mathop {*}}\)
 that maps any algebraic integer 
\(x \in F\)
 to the vector of its Galois conjugates 
\((x'_0,\dots ,x'_{k-1}) \in \mathbb {R}^k\)
, as in Definition 
4.13
. Let 
\(\mathcal {L}:= \{(x,x^{{\mathop {*}}}) \mid x \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\}\subseteq F\times \mathbb {R}^k\)
 and 
\(H:= \mathbb {R}^{k}\)
. (Here 
\(k = d-1\)
 if 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {R}\)
, or 
\(k = d-2\)
 if 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {C}\setminus \mathbb {R}\)
.) Further define the projection maps 
\(\pi _{\textit{int}} : F \times \mathbb {R}^{k} \rightarrow \mathbb {R}^k\)
 that maps 
\(z \in F \times \mathbb {R}^{k}\)
 to its second component (which consists of 
k
 coordinates), and 
\(\pi : F\times \mathbb {R}^k \rightarrow F\)
 to its first component. Note that the 
\(*\)
-map takes any 
\(x \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
 to 
\(x^* \in \pi _{\textit{int}}((\pi |_{\mathcal {L}})^{-1}(x))\)
. Furthermore it is known that 
\(\pi _{\textit{int}}(\mathcal {L})\)
 is dense in 
\(\mathbb {R}^k\)
 and that 
\(\pi |_{\mathcal {L}}\)
 is one-one (see, e.g., [
6
, Sec. 7.3] or [
35
, Prop. 6, p. 57]). Thus 
\(*\)
 follows the prescription for the star-map as defined in Definition 
4.7
. Briefly, the discreteness of 
\(\Sigma ^{(\lambda )}(P)\)
 follows from the fact that 
\(\mathcal {L}\)
 has finite local complexity (and is hence locally finite), which property, due to the fact that 
P
 is bounded, is inherited by 
\(\pi (\mathcal {L}\cap \pi _{\textit{int}}^{-1}(P)) = \Sigma ^{(\lambda )}(P)\)
. This is spelled out in detail in, e.g., [
6
, Sec. 7.2, Prop. 7.5].

                  
Since (by hypothesis) 
\(S \subseteq \mathbb {Q}(\lambda )\)
 and is finite, there exists an 
\(m \in \mathbb {Z}\)
 such that 
\(mS \subseteq \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
. Taking 
\((mS)^* = \{(x'_0,\dots ,x'_{k-1}) \mid x \in mS\}\)
 as in Definition 
4.13
, and any interval 
\(P \supseteq (mS)^*\)
, we then have 
\(Q(mS) \subseteq \Sigma ^{(\lambda )}(P)\)
 by Theorem 
4.14
. But since 
\(Q_\lambda (mS) = mQ_\lambda (S)\)
 (via Lemma 
2.9
), we find 
\(Q_\lambda (S) \subseteq \frac{1}{m}\Sigma ^{(\lambda )}(P)\)
. Since 
\(\Sigma ^{(\lambda )}(P)\)
 is uniformly discrete
Footnote 
9
, so is 
\(\frac{1}{m}\Sigma ^{(\lambda )}(P)\)
. Thus 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 is a subset of a uniformly discrete set, so it too is uniformly discrete.

                  
Thus according to the above identifications, 
\({\mathcal {M}} = (F, H,\mathcal {L})\)
 is a cut and project scheme, and thus for any sPV number 
\(\lambda \)
, we conclude that 
\(\Sigma ^{(\lambda )}(P)\)
 is indeed a projection set associated with that scheme, and thus a model set. 
\(\square \)

                
                  
Remark

                  
Henceforth, we adopt the standard notation 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }^{(\lambda )}(P)\)
 (resp. 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
, etc.) in place of 
\(\Sigma ^{(\lambda )}(P)\)
 (resp. 
\(\Sigma (P)\)
, etc.). 
\(\square \)

                
                  
Remark

                  
Case 1 of the proof of the Discreteness Theorem (the case where 
\(k=d-1\)
) would still go through if we allowed 
\(p\in D[x]\)
 for any discrete subring 
\(D\subseteq \mathbb {C}\)
 (instead of insisting that 
\(p\in \mathbb {Z}[x]\)
), that is, we could relax the definition of strong PV number to allow algebraic 
D
-integers 
\(\lambda \)
 rather than 
\(\mathbb {Z}\)
-integers, and we would still get discrete 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 in Case 1. This generalization does not yield any new values of 
\(\lambda \)
 that are not already sPV by our original definition, however. 
\(\square \)

                
                  
Remark

                  
Unfortunately, Theorem 
4.12
 does not show that the various discrete 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 are Meyer sets, only because we do not know in general whether any particular 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 is relatively dense. We address the issue of relative density in Section 
6
