, below, where we prove relative density in many cases. Arbitrary subsets of Meyer sets are sometimes called “weak Meyer sets." 
\(\square \)

                
The next proposition speaks to the aperiodicity of various 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
. It follows immediately from Lemma 
4.10
 and Theorem 
4.12
.

                  
Proposition 4.15

                  
Let 
\(\lambda \)
 be nontrivial sPV and 
\(S\subseteq \mathbb {Q}(\lambda )\)
 be finite. 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 intersects any arithmetic progression in only finitely many points (and is therefore aperiodic).

                
                  
Proof

                  
By scaling everything up by some appropriate integer as in the proof of Theorem 
4.12
, we may assume that 
\(S\subseteq \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
.

                  
By Theorem 
4.14
 and Theorem 
4.12
, 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 is a subset of 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
, the model set of a cut-and-project scheme 
\(\mathcal {M}\)
, and furthermore the map 
\(\pi _{\textit{int}}|_{\mathcal {L}}\)
 is one-to-one. Then 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 intersects any arithmetic progression in only finitely many points by Lemma 
4.10
. 
\(\square \)

                
We conclude this section by collecting some technical details which, in the sequel, are convenient for applying Theorem 
4.12
.

                  
Definition 4.16

                  
Let 
\(p(x) = x^d + \sum _{j=0}^{d-1} c_jx^j\)
 be some monic polynomial in 
\(\mathbb {C}[x]\)
 of degree 
\(d>0\)
 with coefficients 
\(c_0,\ldots ,c_{d-1}\in \mathbb {C}\)
. We let 
\(\Lambda _p\)
 denote the companion matrix of 
p
, that is, the 
\(d\times d\)
 matrix
$$\begin{aligned} \Lambda _p := {\left[ \begin{matrix} 0 & \quad 0 & \quad 0 & \quad \cdots & \quad 0 & \quad -c_0 \\ 1 & \quad 0 & \quad 0 & \quad \cdots & \quad 0 & \quad -c_1 \\ 0 & \quad 1 & \quad 0 & \quad \cdots & \quad 0 & \quad -c_2 \\ 0 & \quad 0 & \quad 1 & \quad \cdots & \quad 0 & \quad -c_3 \\ \vdots & \quad \vdots & \quad \vdots & \quad \ddots & \quad \vdots & \quad \vdots \\ 0 & \quad 0 & \quad 0 & \quad \cdots & \quad 1 & \quad -c_{d-1}\end{matrix}\right] }\;. \end{aligned}$$

                    (4)
                

                
We can view 
\(\Lambda _p\)
 as representing a linear map 
\(\mathbb {C}^d \rightarrow \mathbb {C}^d\)
 relative to the standard basis 
\(\{\textbf{e}_0,\ldots ,\textbf{e}_{d-1}\}\)
 of 
\(\mathbb {C}^d\)
. It is well-known that 
\(\Lambda _p\)
 has characteristic polynomial 
\(\pm p\)
, and so its eigenvalues are the roots of 
p
.
The next lemma is standard.

                  
Lemma 4.17

                  
Let 
p
(
x
) and 
\(\Lambda _p\)
 be as in Definition 
4.16
 where 
p
 has degree 
\(d>0\)
. Let 
\(\mu _0,\ldots ,\mu _{d-1}\in \mathbb {C}\)
 be the (not necessarily distinct) roots of 
p
. Let
$$\begin{aligned} V := V(\mathbf {\mu }) := {\left[ \begin{matrix} 1 &  \mu _0 &  (\mu _0)^2 &  \cdots &  (\mu _0)^{d-1} \\ 1 &  \mu _1 &  (\mu _1)^2 &  \cdots &  (\mu _1)^{d-1} \\ \vdots &  \vdots &  \vdots &  \ddots &  \vdots \\ 1 &  \mu _{d-1} &  (\mu _{d-1})^2 &  \cdots &  (\mu _{d-1})^{d-1} \end{matrix}\right] } \end{aligned}$$

                    (5)
                
be the 
\(d\times d\)
 Vandermonde matrix with respect to 
\(\mathbf {\mu }:= (\mu _0,\ldots ,\mu _{d-1})\)
 (that is, 
\([V]_{ij} = (\mu _i)^j\)
 for all 
\(i,j\in \{0,\ldots ,d-1\}\)
). For any 
\(1\le \ell \le d\)
, let 
U
 be the matrix of the first 
\(\ell \)
 rows of 
V
, and let 
D
 be the 
\(\ell \times \ell \)
 diagonal matrix with diagonal entries 
\([D]_{ii} := \mu _i\)
 for all 
\(i\in \{0,\ldots ,\ell -1\}\)
. Then
$$\begin{aligned} U\Lambda _p = DU\;. \end{aligned}$$

                
                  
Proof

                  
For any 
\(i\in \{0,\ldots ,\ell -1\}\)
 and 
\(k\in \{0,\ldots ,d-1\}\)
, the 
\({{(i,k)}^{\textit{th}}}\)
 entry of 
\(U\Lambda _p\)
 is given by
$$ [U\Lambda _p]_{ik} = \sum _{j=0}^{d-1}[U]_{ij}[\Lambda _p]_{jk} = \sum _{j=0}^{d-1}(\mu _i)^j[\Lambda _p]_{jk} = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} (\mu _i)^{k+1} &  \text{ if } {k<d-1,} \\ -\sum _{j=0}^{d-1} c_j(\mu _i)^j &  \text{ if } k=d-1. \end{array} \right. $$
Since 
\(\mu _i\)
 is a root of 
p
, we have 
\(-\sum _{j=0}^{d-1} c_j(\mu _i)^j = (\mu _i)^d\)
, and so in either case we get 
\([U\Lambda _p]_{ik} = (\mu _i)^{k+1} = \mu _i(\mu _i)^k = [D]_{ii}[U]_{ik} = [DU]_{ik}\)
. 
\(\square \)

                
                  
Remark

                  
If 
V
 is invertible (which is true iff all the 
\(\mu _i\)
 are pairwise distinct), then Lemma 
4.17
 (with 
\(\ell = d\)
) says that the columns of 
\(V^{-1}\)
 are eigenvectors of 
\(\Lambda _p\)
 with respective eigenvalues 
\(\mu _0,\ldots ,\mu _{d-1}\)
. 
\(\square \)

                
Putting together Definitions 
4.8
 and 
4.13
, we have,

                  
Fact 4.18

                  
Let 
\(\lambda \)
 be a nontrivial sPV number of degree 
\(d>0\)
 and let 
\(\mu _0,\dots \mu _{k-1}\)
 be the conjugates of 
\(\lambda \)
 that are in 
\({\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
. Then we may write,
$$ \begin{aligned} \mathord {\curlywedge }(P) = \left\{ \sum \limits _{i=0}^{d-1} a_i\lambda ^i \left| ~(\forall i,\,0 \le i < k), ~~ a_i \in \mathbb {Z}\;\mathrel { \& }\;\ell _i \le \sum \limits _{j=0}^{d-1} a_j(\mu _i)^j \le h_i \right. \right\} \;. \end{aligned}$$

                    (6)
                

                
We summarize the most important findings of Theorems  
4.12
, 
4.14
, and Fact 
4.18
—Eqs. (
3
) and (
6
) in particular—in the following fact (cf. Eq. (
6
)) for any finite 
\(S\subseteq \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
.

                  
Fact 4.19

                  
Let 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {C}\setminus {\left[ {0,1}\right] }\)
 be an algebraic integer of degree 
\(d>0\)
. Let 
\(\mu _0,\ldots ,\mu _{d-1}\in \mathbb {C}\)
 be the conjugates of 
\(\lambda \)
 such that 
\(\mu _0,\ldots ,\mu _{k-1}\)
 are elements of (0, 1), for some 
\(k \ge 1\)
. Let 
\(S\subseteq \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
 be finite. Then letting

                    

                      
\(W:= V(\mu _0,\ldots ,\mu _{k-1})\)
,

                    

                    

                      
for all 
\(0\le j <k\)
, 
\(\ell _j:= \min S_j\)
 and 
\(h_j:= \max S_j\)
, where 
$$ \begin{aligned} S_j := \left\{ \sum _{i=0}^{d-1} a_i(\mu _j)^i \;:\; a_0,\ldots ,a_{d-1}\in \mathbb {Z}\;\mathrel { \& }\;\sum _{i=0}^{d-1} a_i\lambda ^i \in S \right\} \;, \end{aligned}$$
 and

                    

                    

                      
\(\Omega := W^{-1}P\)
, where 
\(P:= {\left[ {\ell _0,h_0}\right] }\times {\left[ {\ell _1,h_1}\right] }\times \cdots \times {\left[ {\ell _{k-1},h_{k-1}}\right] }\)
,

                    

                  
we have that 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)^{{\mathop {*}}}\)
 is dense in 
P
, and 
\(Q_\lambda (S) \subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
, where 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 is given by Eq. (
6
).
Footnote 
10
 In the special case where 
\(S = \{0,1\}\)
, we have 
\(\ell _0 = \cdots = \ell _{k-1} = 0\)
 and 
\(h_0 = \cdots = h_{k-1} = 1\)
, whence
$$ \begin{aligned} Q_\lambda \subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P) = \left\{ \left. \sum _{j=0}^{d-1} a_j\lambda ^j \;\right| \; (a_0,\ldots ,a_{d-1}) \in \mathbb {Z}^d \;\mathrel { \& }\;0 \le \sum _{j=0}^{d-1} a_j(\mu _i)^j \le 1 \text{ for } \text{ all } 0 \le i < k \right\} \;. \end{aligned}$$

                    (7)
                

                
5 
Characterizing Nontrivial Strong PV Numbers of Low Degree
In this section, we characterize all sPV numbers of degree 
\(\le 3\)
.
The next corollary characterizes the sPV numbers 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {R}\)
 of degree 2 and gives bounds on the corresponding 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 sets. It uses the Discreteness Theorem (Theorem 
4.12
) for 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {R}\)
, with 
\(d=2\)
. It only applies to negative 
\(\lambda \)
; for positive numbers, we can use the fact that 
\(Q_{1-\lambda } = Q_\lambda \)
 and the fact (Fact 
4.2
) that 
\(\lambda \)
 is sPV if and only if 
\(1-\lambda \)
 is.

                
Corollary 5.1

                
Let 
\(\lambda \)
 be any negative (real) number. Then 
\(\lambda \)
 is sPV of degree 2 if and only if 
\(\lambda = (-m - \sqrt{m^2+4n})/2\)
 for some integers 
m
 and 
n
 with 
\(0 < n \le m\)
. If this is the case, then
$$ \begin{aligned}  &   \!\!\!\!\! Q_\lambda \subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P) \nonumber \\  &   = \{ a - b\lambda \mid a,b\in \mathbb {Z}\;\mathrel { \& }\;b\mu \le a \le b\mu + 1\} = \{1\} \cup \left\{ {\lceil {b\mu }\rceil } - b\lambda \mid b\in \mathbb {Z}\right\} \;, \end{aligned}$$

                    (8)
                
where 
\(\mu := (-m + \sqrt{m^2+4n})/2\)
 is the conjugate of 
\(\lambda \)
 and 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 is as in Definition 
4.8
 and Eq. (
6
), with 
\(P:= {\left[ {0,1}\right] }\)
 and the particular value of 
\(\lambda \)
 understood. Furthermore, 
\(\lambda < -1\)
, and except for 0 and 1, any two adjacent elements of 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 differ by either 
\(-\lambda \)
 or at least 
\(1-\lambda \)
.

              
                
Proof

                
The quadratic polynomial 
\(p(x):= x^2 + mx - n \in \mathbb {Z}[x]\)
 has roots 
\((-m\pm \sqrt{m^2+4n})/2\)
. If 
\(\lambda \)
 and 
\(\mu \)
 are the roots of 
p
(
x
) as above, then the inequality 
\(0 < n \le m\)
 is equivalent to 
\(0< \mu < 1\)
, and it guarantees that 
p
(
x
) is irreducible (and so 
\(\lambda \)
 and 
\(\mu \)
 are conjugates). We can thus apply Theorem 
4.12
 with 
\((\mu _0,\mu _1):= (\mu ,\lambda )\)
, which says that 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is uniformly discrete. Applying Eq. (
7
) in Fact 
4.19
 with 
\(d:=2\)
, 
\(k:=1\)
, and 
\(\mu _0:= \mu \)
, we have (using (
a
, 
b
) instead of 
\((a_0,a_1)\)
 as the index)
$$ \begin{aligned} Q_\lambda \subseteq \left\{ \left. a + b\lambda \;\right| \; a,b\in \mathbb {Z}\;\mathrel { \& }\;0 \le a + b\mu \le 1 \right\} \;. \end{aligned}$$
By switching 
b
 with 
\(-b\)
, this set inclusion is seen to be equivalent to the first inclusion of (
8
). The subsequent set equality in (
8
) follows from the fact that 
\(\mu \)
 is irrational.

                
It is clear that 
\(\lambda < -1\)
. The quantity 
\({\lceil {b\mu }\rceil } - b\lambda \)
 increases strictly monotonically in 
b
, so adjacent points of 
\(Q_\lambda \setminus \{1\}\)
 correspond (at least) to adjacent integer values of 
b
. When 
b
 increases by 1, 
\({\lceil {b\mu }\rceil }\)
 increases by 0 or 1, giving a difference of either 
\(-\lambda \)
 or 
\(1-\lambda \)
. Finally, the closest point to 1 in 
\(Q_\lambda \)
, other than 0, is 
\({\lceil {\mu }\rceil } - \lambda = 1 - \lambda \)
, which is 
\(-\lambda \)
 away from 1. 
\(\square \)

              
Here are a few simple cases:

                

                  
\(m=n=1\)
: 
\(\lambda = -\varphi \)
 and 
\(Q_\lambda = Q_{-\varphi } = Q_{1+\varphi }\)
.

                

                

                  
\(m=2\)
 and 
\(n=1\)
: 
\(\lambda = -1-\sqrt{2}\)
 and 
\(Q_\lambda = Q_{1-\lambda } = Q_{2+\sqrt{2}}\)
.

                

                

                  
\(m=2\)
 and 
\(n=2\)
: 
\(\lambda = -1-\sqrt{3}\)
 and 
\(Q_\lambda = Q_{1-\lambda } = Q_{2+\sqrt{3}}\)
.

                

                

                  
\(m=3\)
 and 
\(n=1\)
: 
\(\lambda = -(3+\sqrt{13})/2\)
 and 
\(Q_\lambda = Q_{1-\lambda } = Q_{(5+\sqrt{13})/2}\)
.

                

              
The next corollary characterizes the non-real sPV numbers of degree 3. It applies the Discreteness Theorem with 
\(d=3\)
 and 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {C}\setminus \mathbb {R}\)
.

                
Corollary 5.2

                
A non-real number 
\(\lambda \)
 is sPV of degree 3 if and only if 
\(\lambda \)
 is a root of a polynomial 
\(p(x):= x^3 + ax^2 + bx + c\)
 for some 
\(a,b,c\in \mathbb {Z}\)
 such that: (1) 
\(c<0\)
; (2) 
\(a+b+c \ge 0\)
; and (3) the discriminant 
\(\Delta < 0\)
, where 
\(\Delta := a^2b^2 - 4b^3 - 4a^3c - 27c^2 + 18abc\)
. If this is the case, then letting 
\(\mu \)
 be the (unique) root of 
p
 in 
\({\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
, and 
\(\lambda \)
 one of the non-real roots of 
p
,
$$\begin{aligned} Q_\lambda \subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P) = \left\{ 1\right\} \cup \left\{ m\lambda + n\lambda ^2 - {\lfloor {m\mu +n\mu ^2}\rfloor } \mid m,n\in \mathbb {Z}\right\} \;. \end{aligned}$$

                    (9)
                

              
                
Proof sketch

                
The first two conditions give 
\(p(0) = c < 0\)
 and 
\(p(1) = 1+a+b+c > 0\)
, respectively, and together they force 
p
 to have a root in 
\({\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
. The negative discriminant implies that the other two roots are non-real. (The only other way to force a single real root in 
\({\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
 is for 
\(p(0)>0\)
 and 
\(p(1)<0\)
, but this makes 
p
 have three real roots.) Thus the hypothesis of Theorem 
4.12
 is satisfied if and only if the conditions of the corollary are satisfied. If this is the case, we apply Fact 
4.19
, Eq. (
7
) with 
\(d:= 3\)
, with 
\(k:=1\)
, and with 
\(\mu _0:= \mu \)
 to get the first inclusion. The equality follows from the fact that 
p
 must be irreducible, and thus the set 
\(\{1,\mu ,\mu ^2\}\)
 is linearly independent over 
\(\mathbb {Q}\)
, whence 
\(m\mu + n\mu ^2\notin \mathbb {Z}\)
 unless 
\(m=n=0\)
. 
\(\square \)

              
The next corollary covers the case of sPV numbers of degree 3 which are real. It considers only negative 
\(\lambda \)
; the positive numbers are then all of the form 
\(1-\lambda \)
.

                
Corollary 5.3

                
A negative real number 
\(\lambda \)
 is sPV of degree 3 if and only if 
\(\lambda \)
 is the least root of a polynomial 
\(p(x):= x^3 + ax^2 + bx + c\)
 for some 
\(a,b,c\in \mathbb {Z}\)
 such that: (1) 
\(c>0\)
; (2) 
\(a+b+c \ge 0\)
; (3) 
\(-2a-3< b < 0\)
; and (4) the discriminant 
\(\Delta > 0\)
, where 
\(\Delta := a^2b^2 - 4b^3 - 4a^3c - 27c^2 + 18abc\)
. If this is the case, then 
\(a>0\)
 as well, and letting 
\(\mu < \nu \)
 be the two roots of 
p
 in 
\({\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
 and letting 
\(v_max (a_1,a_2)\)
 and 
\(v_min (a_1,a_2)\)
 be 
\(\max \{-a_1\mu -a_2\mu ^2, -a_1\nu -a_2\nu ^2\}\)
 and 
\( \min \{-a_1\mu - a_2\mu ^2, -a_1\nu - a_2\nu ^2\}\)
, respectively, we have
$$     \begin{aligned} Q_\lambda&\subseteq \left\{ a_0 + a_1\lambda + a_2\lambda ^2 \mid a_0,a_1,a_2\in \mathbb {Z}\;\mathrel { \& }\;a_0 + a_1\mu + a_2\mu ^2 \in {\left[ {0,1}\right] } \;\mathrel { \& }\;a_0 + a_1\nu + a_2\nu ^2 \in {\left[ {0,1}\right] } \right\} \\&= \left\{ a_0 +a_1\lambda + a_2\lambda ^2 \mid a_0,a_1,a_2\in \mathbb {Z}\;\mathrel { \& }\;v_max (a_1,a_2) \le a_0 \le v_min (a_1,a_2) + 1 \right\} \\&= \left\{ 1\right\} \cup \left\{ m\lambda + n\lambda ^2 + {\lceil {v_max (m,n)}\rceil } \mid m,n\in \mathbb {Z}\;\mathrel { \& }\;{\lceil {v_max (m,n)}\rceil } = {\lceil {v_min (m,n)}\rceil } \right\} \\&= \left\{ 1\right\} \cup \left\{ m\lambda + n\lambda ^2 - {\lfloor {m\mu +n\mu ^2}\rfloor } \mid m,n\in \mathbb {Z}\;\mathrel { \& }\;{\lfloor {m\mu +n\mu ^2}\rfloor } = {\lfloor {m\nu + n\nu ^2}\rfloor } \right\} \;. \end{aligned}$$

              
                
Proof sketch

                
The discriminant 
\(\Delta \)
 is positive if and only if 
p
 has three distinct real roots: 
\(\lambda <0\)
 and its conjugates 
\(\mu \)
 and 
\(\nu \)
 such that 
\(\mu <\nu \)
. As in Corollary 
5.2
, conditions 1 and 2 are equivalent to 
\(p(0),p(1) > 0\)
. The derivative of 
p
(
x
) is 
\(p'(x) = 3x^2 +2ax + b\)
 and has roots 
\(x_\pm = (-a \pm \sqrt{a^2-3b})/3\)
. Condition 4 implies 
\(x_+,x_-\in \mathbb {R}\)
 and 
\(p(x_+)<0\)
. 
(
\(\Longrightarrow \)
)::

                      
Suppose 
\(\lambda \)
 is sPV of degree 3 with minimal polynomial 
p
 as above (and thus 
\(\lambda< 0< \mu< \nu < 1\)
). Then 
\(\Delta > 0\)
 (Condition 4), and 
\(\lambda \)
 is the least root of 
p
. Additionally, 
\(0<\mu< x_+< \nu < 1\)
 and 
\(p(0),p(1)>0\)
, the latter implying Conditions 1 and 2. From 
\(0<x_+<1\)
 we get 
\(a< \sqrt{a^2-3b} < a+3\)
 (and in particular, 
\(a > -3\)
 and 
\(b\le a^2/3\)
). Squaring both sides of the inequality 
\(\sqrt{a^2-3b} < a+3\)
 yields 
\(-2a-3<b\)
. If 
\(a < 0\)
, there are only two possible cases: 
\(a = -1\)
 (whence 
\(b=0\)
, because 
\(-2a-3<b\le a^2/3\)
) and 
\(a=-2\)
. We cannot have 
\(a=-2\)
, because this violates 
\(-2a-3<b\le a^2/3\)
 for any integer 
b
. If 
\(a=-1\)
 and 
\(b=0\)
, however, then 
\(\Delta = 4c-27c^2 < 0\)
 for all integers 
\(c > 0\)
 (cf. Condition 1), so this case cannot happen either. Hence 
\(a\ge 0\)
. Squaring both sides of the inequality 
\(a < \sqrt{a^2-3b}\)
 gives 
\(b<0\)
, which implies Condition 3. Finally, we also have 
\(a > 0\)
, for if 
\(a=0\)
, then 
\(b\in \{-2,-1\}\)
 by Condition 3, and in either case, 
\(\Delta = -4b^3-27c^2 < 0\)
 for any integer 
\(c\ge -b\)
 (cf. Condition 2).

                    
(
\(\Longleftarrow \)
)::

                      
Suppose 
\(\lambda \)
 is the least root of 
p
 as above satisfying Conditions 1–4, with the other real roots 
\(\mu <\nu \)
 (by Condition 4). We have 
\(p(0),p(1)>0\)
 by Conditions 1 and 2, and 
\(0<x_+<1\)
 by Condition 3. By Condition 4, 
\(p(x_+)<0\)
, and thus there are two roots of 
p
 strictly between 0 and 1. These are 
\(\mu \)
 and 
\(\nu \)
, because 
\(\lambda < 0\)
. Finally, 
p
 is irreducible (over 
\(\mathbb {Q}\)
), for otherwise, 
p
 has an integral root (which must be 
\(\lambda \)
), which makes 
\(\mu \)
 and 
\(\nu \)
 conjugate roots of some monic quadratic polynomial in 
\(\mathbb {Z}[x]\)
, but this is impossible. Thus 
\(\lambda \)
 is sPV of degree 3.

                    
 The rest of the corollary follows from Eq. (
7
) of Fact 
4.19
 where 
\(d=3\)
 and 
\(k=2\)
. 
\(\square \)

              
Notice that the condition 
\({\lfloor {m\mu +n\mu ^2}\rfloor } = {\lfloor {m\nu + n\nu ^2}\rfloor }\)
 in the corollary implies 
\(|m\nu + n\nu ^2-m\mu -n\mu ^2|<1\)
, which implies 
\(|m+n(\nu +\mu )| < (\nu -\mu )^{-1}\)
. Thus given 
\(m\in \mathbb {Z}\)
, there are only finitely many 
\(n\in \mathbb {Z}\)
 such that the condition is satisfied, and vice versa.
6 
Relative Density of Discrete 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)

                     
Theorem 
4.12
 says that 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 is a subset of the model set of a certain cut-and-project scheme, where 
\(\lambda \)
 is nontrivial sPV and 
\(S\subseteq \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
 is finite. One of our chief conjectures is that these 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 are all Meyer sets. To establish the conjecture it suffices by Fact 
4.9
 to show that 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 is relatively dense (in either 
\(\mathbb {R}\)
 or 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
 as appropriate). While we have not succeeded in finding a general proof, we have a number of partial results, as well as a sufficient condition for 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 to be Meyer: 
\(\lambda \)
 is nontrivial sPV and 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 contains a unit of 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
 other than 1 (see Corollary 
6.23
).
Footnote 
11
We begin with the case of real quadratic sPV numbers (Section 
6.1
), and, generalizing techniques of Berman and Moody [
8
] and Masáková et al. [
30
], characterize all such numbers for which 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 
equals
 a naturally corresponding model set (which is stronger than the Meyer property). As model sets are relatively dense, relative density is immediate. We find that the model set property can only hold for four values of 
\(\lambda \)
 (or the corresponding 
\(1-\lambda \)
): 
\(-\varphi \)
, 
\(-1-\sqrt{2}\)
, 
\(-1-\sqrt{3}\)
, and 
\(\frac{-3-\sqrt{17}}{2}\)
. We then prove it holds for each of these values; the proof for 
\(\frac{-3-\sqrt{17}}{2}\)
 is somewhat more difficult and appears separately, as outlined in the next paragraph. We thus obtain a characterization that strictly strengthens a previous result of Masáková et al. [
30
], which gave a similar characterization for quadratic unitary Pisot numbers (a subset of quadratic sPV numbers).
Certain aspects of Berman and Moody’s technique, which we call the 
covering method
, are then progressively strengthened in Section 
6.2
, where we complete the proof of our characterization of Section 
6.1
 for the remaining case of 
\(\frac{-3-\sqrt{17}}{2}\)
. Sufficient conditions for obtaining a model set are further extended to higher degree 
\(\lambda \)
.
Another notion, which we call 
affine embedding
, provides a flexible tool for proving both relative density 
and
 uniform discreteness (Section 
6.3
). Affine embeddings combined with the covering method lead to the strongest sufficient condition we have for 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 to be a Meyer set (Corollary 
6.23
, as mentioned above).
In part II, we will see, in Section  
8
, that the covering method allows us to conclude that 
\(Q_{\lambda }\)
 is relatively dense for three cubic 
\(\lambda \)
’s: two non-real values, and 
\(\lambda _7\)
, the latter being the appropriate parameter for generating the set based on the regular heptagon (as explained in Section 
7
; also see Figures 
6
 and 
9
). These proofs explicitly show that 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 
contains
 (but does not necessarily equal) a model set. Affine embedding yields a method to determine if certain elements of 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 are 
not
 contained in 
\(Q_\lambda \)
, for certain 
\(\lambda \)
 (Section 
9.1
). Finally, in Section 
10
 we use the 1-dimensional (real) results outlined above to prove relative density results in higher dimensions. Thus, for example, the relative density of 
\(Q_{\lambda _7}\)
 implies the relative density of the set of Figure 
6
 derived from the heptagon.
Since throughout this section we are concerned, for any 
\(x \in Q_\lambda \)
, with the individual components of 
\(x^*\)
, we make use of the explicit form given for 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 in Eq. 
7
 of Fact 
4.19
.
6.1 
Relative Density in 
\(\mathbb {R}\)
 of 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 for Real Quadratic sPV Numbers 
\(\lambda \)

                        
We assume throughout this subsection that 
\(\lambda \)
 is a real quadratic sPV number. These numbers are characterized in Corollary 
5.1
.
In Theorem 
4.12
, we showed that 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is a subset of a cut-and-project, or model, set. In the quadratic case, according to Eq. (
8
) in Corollary 
5.1
 (for 
\(\lambda < 0\)
 without loss of generality), we can write this relation as follows:
$$\begin{aligned} Q_\lambda \subseteq \{ a - b\lambda \mid a,b\in \mathbb {Z} \text{ and } a-b\mu \in [0,1]\} \end{aligned}$$
where 
\(\mu = \lambda '\)
 is the Galois conjugate of 
\(\lambda \)
. Following the notation of Definition 
4.13
, we denote 
\(P:= [0,1]\)
 and 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P):= \{ x \mid x \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \text{ and } x' \in [0,1]\}\)
. Then the above containment is stated simply as,
$$\begin{aligned} Q_\lambda \subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\;. \end{aligned}$$
Our goal in this section is to determine for which 
\(\lambda \)
 the above containment is an equality. This we do in Theorem 
6.9
. When equality holds, given that 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 is a model set, it then follows that 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is relatively dense. This is one of only two ways we have been able to prove that 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is relatively dense; the other is used for Corollary 
9.3
 later on.
The development closely follows prior work of Berman & Moody [
8
] and Masáková et al. [
30
].
Recall the concept of the 
field norm
 
\(N(x) = xx'\)
, for 
\(x \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
.
Footnote 
12
 More concretely, if 
\(x = a + b\lambda \)
, then 
\(N(x) = (a + b\lambda )(a + b\mu )\)
. It is known that 
N
 is multiplicative, and that 
x
 is a unit (has an inverse) in 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
 iff 
\(N(x) = \pm 1\)
.

                  
Definition 6.1

                  
An algebraic integer 
\(\alpha \)
 is 
unitary
 iff 
\(\alpha \)
 is a unit of 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\alpha ]\)
. A quadratic integer 
\(\alpha \in \mathbb {R}\)
 is 
unitary Pisot
 iff it is a PV number which is a unit in 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\alpha ]\)
.

                
Most quadratic sPV numbers are not unitary Pisot (as they can be irrationals with non-unit field norm, cf. Corollary 
5.1
 for 
\(n > 1\)
). In [
30
], Masáková et al. characterize the real quadratic unitary Pisot numbers that lead to model sets. By a result of Pinch (in [
40
, Thm. 15]) for real quadratic integer 
\(\alpha \)
, 
\(Q_\alpha \)
 is discrete iff 
\(\alpha \)
 is sPV. Thus real quadratic unitary Pisots can lead to model sets only if they are also sPV. Our characterization below (Theorem 
6.9
) thus strengthens that of [
30
], as it deals with a strict superset of those unitary Pisots that may result in model sets.
Let 
\(Q_{\lambda }'\)
 denote the set of all conjugates of elements in 
\(Q_{\lambda }\)
. It is clear that 
\(Q_{\lambda }' = Q_{\lambda '} = Q_\mu \)
. Similarly , 
\( \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)' = \{x' \mid x \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \;\mathrel { \& }\;x' \in P\}\)
. Since 
\(\mu \)
 generates the same ring as 
\(\lambda \)
, 
\(x \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
 iff 
\(x' \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
, so it is plain that 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)' = \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \cap P\)
. Because the algebraic structures are the same, that implies 
\(Q_{\lambda } \subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 iff 
\(Q_\mu \subseteq \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\cap P\)
. Further, 
\(Q_\lambda = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 iff 
\(Q_\mu = \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\cap P =\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)'\)
.
The following result and proof is identical to an analogous one in [
30
]. The only observation we make here is that it’s not necessary to assume that 
\(\mu \)
 (or, equivalently, 
\(\lambda \)
) is unitary Pisot. We also find it convenient to use the notation of congruence. If 
R
 is a ring, and 
\(x, y, z \in R\)
, we write 
\({x} \equiv {y} \pmod {{z}}\)
 to mean 
\(x - y \in z R\)
, or, equivalently, that 
\(z \mid x - y\)
, where divisibility is with respect to 
R
.

                  
Lemma 6.2

                  
For any 
\(y \in Q_\mu \)
, both of the following hold: 

                    

                      
(i)

                      
                        
\({y} \equiv {0} \pmod {{\mu }}\)
 or 
\({y} \equiv {1} \pmod {{\mu }}\)
.

                      
                    

                    

                      
(ii)

                      
                        
\(y \equiv 0 \pmod {(1-\mu )}\)
 or 
\(y \equiv 1 \pmod {(1-\mu )}\)
.

                      
                    

                  

                
                  
Proof

                  
If 
\(y \in Q_\mu \)
, by Lemma 
3.5
, then 
\(y = \sum _{i=0}^n b_i \mu ^i(1-\mu )^{n-i}\)
 for some 
\(n \ge 0\)
 and 
\(0 \le b_i \le {n \atopwithdelims ()i}\)
. If 
\(b_0 = 0\)
, then clearly 
\(\mu \mid y\)
. If 
\(b_0 = 1\)
, then 
\(y = 1 + (\text{ terms } \text{ divisible } \text{ by } {\mu })\)
, so 
\(y \equiv 1\pmod {\mu }\)
, which yields item (i). For item (ii), the proof is the same, but based on the 
\(i= n\)
 term in the sum. 
\(\square \)

                
Remark:
 By conjugation, Lemma 
6.2
 is equivalent to the fact that for all 
\(y \in Q_\lambda \)
, 
\(y \equiv 0\pmod {\lambda }\)
 or 
\(y \equiv 1\pmod {\lambda }\)
, etc.
We now recall some well-known facts about rings of real quadratic integers (i.e., rings of algebraic integers in real quadratic fields) that are of the form 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\sqrt{d}]\)
, where 
\(d > 0 \)
 is square-free. See [
15
] and [
18
] for further details and proofs. All such rings have minimal units greater than 1, known as 
fundamental units
. All units in 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\sqrt{d}]\)
 are positive or negative powers 
\(\pm u_d^k\)
 (where 
\(k \in \mathbb {Z}\)
) of the fundamental unit 
\(u_d\)
. The following lemma, which follows easily, is used in the subsequent proof. For any ring 
R
, we let 
U
(
R
) denote the set of units of 
R
.

                  
Lemma 6.3

                  
Let 
\(\lambda \)
 be quadratic sPV. Then 
\(U(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ])\)
 contains an unbounded set of elements which are positive or negative powers of a fundamental unit of some ring 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\sqrt{d}]\)
 where 
d
 is square-free.

                
                  
Proof

                  
By Corollary 
5.1
, we may consider the negative case 
\(\lambda = \frac{-m-\sqrt{D}}{2}\)
 where 
\(D = m^2+4n\)
 (the proof in the positive case is similar); note that here 
D
 is not a perfect square. Clearly, 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\sqrt{D}] \subseteq \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
, and any units in 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\sqrt{D}]\)
 are also units in 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
. An element 
\(a+b\sqrt{D} \in \mathbb {Z}[\sqrt{D}]\)
 is a unit iff 
\(N(a+b\sqrt{D}) = (a+b\sqrt{D})(a-b\sqrt{D}) = a^2 - Db^2 = 1\)
, i.e., iff 
a
 and 
b
 satisfy Pell’s equation, known by a theorem of Lagrange to have infinitely many integer solutions when 
D
 is not a perfect square. The solution 
\(a_0, b_0\in \mathbb {Z}\)
 with the smallest value 
\(a_0 + b_0\sqrt{D} > 1\)
 corresponds to the fundamental unit 
\(u_D\)
 in 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\sqrt{D}]\)
. Suppose 
\(D = s^2d\)
 where 
d
 is square-free. Since 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\sqrt{D}] = \mathbb {Z}[s\sqrt{d}] \subseteq \mathbb {Z}[\sqrt{d}]\)
, and all units in 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\sqrt{d}]\)
 are powers of a fundamental unit 
\(u_d \in \mathbb {Z}[\sqrt{d}]\)
, it follows that the fundamental unit 
\(u_D\)
 of 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\sqrt{D}]\)
 is a power of 
\(u_d > 1\)
: for some 
\(k>0\)
, 
\(u_D = u_d^k\)
, and as the units of 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\sqrt{D}]\)
 are arbitrary integer powers of 
\(u_D\)
, 
\(U(\mathbb {Z}[\sqrt{D}])\)
 is unbounded. 
\(\square \)

                
The next lemma only relies on the existence of a unit in 
\(U(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ])\)
 of any size (guaranteed by Lemma 
6.3
). In general, 
\(\lambda , 1-\lambda \not \in U(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ])\)
, so that 
\(\lambda \)
 and 
\(1-\lambda \)
 need not be unitary, unlike the analogous lemma in [
30
].

                  
Lemma 6.4

                  
Let 
\(\lambda \)
 be a real quadratic sPV number. Suppose that 
\(Q_\mu = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)'\cap \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
. Then 
\({2} \equiv {0} \pmod {{\mu }}\)
 and 
\({2} \equiv {0} \pmod {{(1-\mu )}}\)
, or, equivalently, 
\({2} \equiv {0} \pmod {{\lambda }}\)
 and 
\({2} \equiv {0} \pmod {{(1-\lambda )}}\)
.

                
                  
Proof

                  
Choosing 
d
 as in Lemma 
6.3
, let 
\(z \in U(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]) \cap \mathbb {Z}[\sqrt{d}]\)
 be sufficiently large and positive so that 
\(z^{-1} \in {\left( {0,1/2}\right) }\)
. Let 
\(u = z^{-1}\)
 denote this unit. Thus 
\(u, 2u \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \cap P = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)' = Q_\mu \)
, and Lemma 
6.2
(i) can be applied to 
\(y = u\)
 and 
\(y = 2u\)
. If 
\(\mu \)
 is a unit, then 
\({2} \equiv {0} \pmod {{\mu }}\)
 and we are done. If 
\(\mu \)
 is not a unit, we have 
\({u} \not \equiv {0} \pmod {{\mu }}\)
 and hence, by Lemma 
6.2
, 
\({u} \equiv {1} \pmod {{\mu }}\)
. If 
\({2u} \equiv {0} \pmod {{\mu }}\)
, then 
\({2} \equiv {0} \pmod {{\mu }}\)
, and again we are done, so suppose 
\({2u} \equiv {1} \pmod {{\mu }}\)
. Subtracting 
\({u} \equiv {1} \pmod {{\mu }}\)
 from 
\({2u} \equiv {1} \pmod {{\mu }}\)
 yields 
\({u} \equiv {0} \pmod {{\mu }}\)
, contradicting 
\({u} \equiv {1} \pmod {{\mu }}\)
. Thus 
\({2u} \equiv {0} \pmod {{\mu }}\)
, and hence 
\({2} \equiv {0} \pmod {{\mu }}\)
.

                  
The exact same argument applies to 
\(1-\mu \)
, appealing to part (ii) of Lemma 
6.2
. 
\(\square \)

                
The following lemma is useful in the proof of Theorem 
6.8
.

                  
Lemma 6.5

                  
If 
\(\lambda \not \in U(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ])\)
 and 
\({\lambda } \equiv {0} \pmod {{2}}\)
, then 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]/\lambda \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \cong \mathbb {Z}_2\)
. The analogous statement holds with 
\(\lambda \)
 replaced by 
\(1-\lambda \)
, 
\(\mu \)
, or 
\(1-\mu \)
.

                
                  
Proof

                  
For any 
\(x = a + b\lambda \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
, we have 
\(x = a + b\lambda \equiv a~\pmod {\lambda }\)
 where 
\(a, b \in \mathbb {Z}\)
. Hence, 
\(x \equiv a~\pmod {\lambda }\)
 where 
\(a \in \mathbb {Z}\)
. However, because 
\(\lambda \mid 2\)
, we find that 
\(2 \equiv 0~\pmod {\lambda }\)
. Hence for any 
\(x \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
, we have either 
\(x \equiv 0~\pmod {\lambda }\)
 or 
\(x \equiv 1~\pmod {\lambda }\)
. Hence the ideal 
\(\lambda \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
 partitions 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
 into two classes, one equivalent to 0 mod 
\(\lambda \)
, one equivalent to 1 mod 
\(\lambda \)
, and preserving addition and multiplication. Thus 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]/\lambda \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \cong \mathbb {Z}_2\)
. 
\(\square \)

                
                  
Lemma 6.6

                  
For any real quadratic sPV number 
\(\lambda \)
 such that in the ring 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
, 
\(\mu \mid 2\)
 and 
\((1-\mu ) \mid 2\)
 (equivalently, 
\(\lambda \mid 2\)
 and 
\((1-\lambda ) \mid 2\)
), one of the following must hold: 

                    

                      
(a)

                      
                        
\(\lambda = -\varphi \)
 or 
\(\lambda = 1+\varphi \)

                      
                    

                    

                      
(b)

                      
                        
\(\lambda = -1-\sqrt{2}\)
 or 
\(\lambda = 2+\sqrt{2}\)

                      
                    

                    

                      
(c)

                      
                        
\(\lambda = -1-\sqrt{3}\)
 or 
\(\lambda = 2+\sqrt{3}\)

                      
                    

                    

                      
(d)

                      
                        
\(\lambda = \frac{-3-\sqrt{17}}{2}\)
 or 
\(\lambda = \frac{5+\sqrt{17}}{2}\)

                      
                    

                  

                
                  
Proof

                  
We consider 
\(\lambda < 0\)
; the postive case is similar. Then by Corollary 
5.1
, 
\(\lambda \)
 and 
\(\mu \)
 have the minimal polynomial 
\(x^2 + mx - n\)
, for 
\(0 < n \le m\)
. Since 
\(\mu \mid 2\)
, there are 
\(a, b \in \mathbb {Z}\)
 such that 
\((a + b\mu ) \mu = 2\)
. Given that 
\(\mu ^2 + m\mu - n = 0\)
, a little calculation gives the constraint 
\(nb = 2\)
. This is only possible if 
\(n = 1\)
 or 
\(n = 2\)
.

                  
Since we also have 
\((1-\mu ) \mid 2\)
, there are 
\(c, d \in \mathbb {Z}\)
 such that 
\((c + d\mu ) (1-\mu ) = 2\)
. Again, a little calculation yields 
\(d = \frac{2}{m-n+1}\)
. Since 
\(d \in \mathbb {Z}\)
, this can only hold if 
\(m-n = 0\)
 or 1.

                  
If 
\(n = 1\)
, we have the unitary Pisot case, which is essentially treated in [
30
]. For the sake of completeness, we give the argument here: From 
\(n=1\)
 and the constraint 
\(m-n = 0\)
 or 1, we conclude that 
\(m = 1\)
 or 
\(m=2\)
. In the first case we obtain 
\(\lambda = -\varphi \)
 (item (a)), and in the second, 
\(\lambda =-1-\sqrt{2}\)
 (item (b)).

                  
Now suppose 
\(n = 2\)
. Then 
\(m = 2\)
 or 
\(m = 3\)
. The former case corresponds to 
\(\lambda = -1-\sqrt{3}\)
, item (c). The latter case corresponds to 
\(\lambda = \frac{-3-\sqrt{17}}{2}\)
, item (d). 
\(\square \)

                
From Lemmas 
6.4
 and 
6.6
 we immediately get the following:

                  
Lemma 6.7

                  
For any real quadratic sPV number 
\(\lambda \)
 such that 
\(Q_\mu = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)'\)
 (equivalently, 
\(Q_\lambda = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
), one of the items (a), (b), (c), (d) of Lemma 
6.6
 must hold.

                
In each of the items (a), (b), and (c) in Lemma 
6.6
, at least one of the two alternative values of 
\(\lambda \)
 is unitary Pisot. We next prove that 
\(Q_\lambda = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 for these values of 
\(\lambda \)
. This was first proved in [
30
] using the notion of 
\(\beta \)
-expansions due to Rényi [
41
]. Here we include a self-contained proof that does not use 
\(\beta \)
-expansions. The original method of Berman & Moody [
8
] can only be applied directly to 
\(\lambda = -\varphi \)
 or 
\(1+\varphi \)
. We extend the technique for the other two values.

                  
Theorem 6.8

                  
(Masáková et al. [
30
]) If 
\(\lambda \)
 or 
\(1-\lambda \)
 is as in Lemma 
6.6
 items (a), (b), or (c), then 
\(Q_\lambda = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
.

                
                  
Proof

                  
Since 
\(Q_\lambda = Q_{1-\lambda }\)
, without loss of generality we choose the extrapolation parameter to be either 
\(\lambda \)
 or 
\(1-\lambda \)
 so that 
\(\mu > 1/2\)
. For the relevant 
\(\lambda \)
’s (Lemma 
6.6
 (a), (b), or (c)), it then turns out that 
\(1-\lambda \)
 is a unit. Concretely, this dictates that we focus on 
\(\lambda = 2+\sqrt{2}\)
 or 
\(\lambda = -1-\sqrt{3}\)
. In those cases, 
\(\lambda \)
 is 
not
 a unit. Only when 
\(\lambda = 1+\varphi \)
 does it hold that 
both
 
\(\lambda \)
 and 
\(1-\lambda \)
 are units which (as we will see) leads to the simpler technique of Berman and Moody.

                  
For any set 
A
, define 
\(x{\mathop {\star }}A = \{x{\mathop {\star }}a \mid a \in A\}\)
, and similarly 
\(A{\mathop {\star }}x = \{a {\mathop {\star }}x \mid a \in A\}\)
.

                  
Our primary task is to prove that
$$\begin{aligned} \mathord {\curlywedge }(P) = 0{\mathop {\star }}\mathord {\curlywedge }(P) \cup 1{\mathop {\star }}\mathord {\curlywedge }(P) \cup \mathord {\curlywedge }(P){\mathop {\star }}0\cup \mathord {\curlywedge }(P) {\mathop {\star }}1. \end{aligned}$$

                    (10)
                
This is a generalization of what Berman and Moody call the “replication" property. The main result then follows by induction, as explained below.

                  
We compute 
\(0{\mathop {\star }}\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
, 
\(1{\mathop {\star }}\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
, 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P){\mathop {\star }}0\)
, and 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P) {\mathop {\star }}1\)
 in turn, starting with 
\(0{\mathop {\star }}\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
:
$$   \begin{aligned} 0{\mathop {\star }}\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)= &   \{0 {\mathop {\star }}x \mid x \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \;\mathrel { \& }\;x' \in P\}\\= &   \{\lambda x \mid x \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \;\mathrel { \& }\;x' \in P\}\\= &   \{\lambda x \mid \lambda x \in \lambda \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \;\mathrel { \& }\;(\lambda x)' \in \mu P\} \end{aligned}$$
Note at this point that, because 
\(\lambda \)
 is not necessarily a unit (unless 
\(\lambda = -\varphi \)
 or 
\(1+\varphi \)
), the ideal 
\(\lambda \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
 is not equal to the ring 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
. We address this issue later. After changing variables 
\(\lambda x \mapsto x\)
,
$$  \begin{aligned} 0{\mathop {\star }}\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)= &   \{x \mid x \in \lambda \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \;\mathrel { \& }\;x' \in \mu P\} = \{x \mid x \in \lambda \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \;\mathrel { \& }\;x' \in [0, \mu ]\}.\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$

                    (11)
                
Proceeding similarly,
$$ \begin{aligned} 1{\mathop {\star }}\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)= &   \{1 {\mathop {\star }}x \mid x \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \;\mathrel { \& }\;x' \in P\} \end{aligned}$$

                    (12)
                
$$ \begin{aligned}= &   \{1-\lambda + \lambda x \mid x \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \;\mathrel { \& }\;x' \in P\} \end{aligned}$$

                    (13)
                
$$ \begin{aligned}= &   \{x \mid x \in 1-\lambda + \lambda \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \;\mathrel { \& }\;x' \in [1-\mu ,1]\}. \end{aligned}$$

                    (14)
                
It is crucial that the intervals 
\(\mu P\)
 and 
\(1-\mu + \mu P\)
 cover 
P
.

                  
Next we extrapolate 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 on the 
right
 with 0:
$$   \begin{aligned} \mathord {\curlywedge }(P) {\mathop {\star }}0= &   \{ x {\mathop {\star }}0 \mid x \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \;\mathrel { \& }\;x' \in P\}\\= &   \{ (1-\lambda ) x \mid x \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \;\mathrel { \& }\;x' \in P\}\\= &   \{ (1-\lambda ) x \mid (1-\lambda ) x \in (1-\lambda )\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \;\mathrel { \& }\;((1-\lambda ) x)' \in (1-\mu ) P\}. \end{aligned}$$
Because 
\(1-\lambda \)
 
is
 a unit, we have 
\((1-\lambda )\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] = \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
. Therefore,
$$  \begin{aligned} \mathord {\curlywedge }(P) {\mathop {\star }}0 {=} \{ x \mid x \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\!\! \;\mathrel { \& }\;x' \in (1-\mu ) P\} {=} \{ x \mid x \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \;\mathrel { \& }\;x' \in [0,1-\mu ]\}.\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$

                    (15)
                
Finally proceeding similarly to the previous case, we extrapolate 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 on the right with 1:
$$  \begin{aligned} \mathord {\curlywedge }(P) {\mathop {\star }}1= &   \{ x {\mathop {\star }}1 \mid x \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \;\mathrel { \& }\;x' \in P\}\\= &   \{\lambda + (1-\lambda ) x \mid \lambda + (1-\lambda ) x \in \lambda + (1-\lambda )\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \;\mathrel { \& }\;(\lambda + (1-\lambda ) x)' \in \mu + (1-\mu ) P\}. \end{aligned}$$
Using the fact that 
\(\lambda + (1-\lambda )\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] = \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
,
$$  \begin{aligned} \mathord {\curlywedge }(P) {\mathop {\star }}1 = \{ x \mid x \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \;\mathrel { \& }\;x' \in \mu + (1-\mu ) P\} = \{ x \mid x \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \;\mathrel { \& }\;x' \in [\mu , 1]\}.\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$

                    (16)
                
Observe that, because 
\(\mu > 1/2\)
, 
\((1-\mu )P\)
 and 
\(\mu P\)
 fail to cover 
P
:
$$\begin{aligned} (1-\mu ) P \cup (\mu + (1-\mu ) P) = {\left[ {0, 1-\mu }\right] } \cup {\left[ {\mu , 1}\right] } = P\setminus {\left( {1-\mu ,\mu }\right) }. \end{aligned}$$
Now for 
\(\lambda = 2+\sqrt{2}\)
, 
\(N(\lambda ) = 2\)
, and for 
\(\lambda = -1-\sqrt{3}\)
, 
\(N(\lambda ) = -2\)
. Thus in these cases 
\(\lambda \not \in U(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ])\)
, and 
\({\lambda } \equiv {0} \pmod {{2}}\)
, so the hypothesis of Lemma 
6.5
 is satisfied, and hence 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]/\lambda \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \cong \mathbb {Z}_2\)
. Thus for 
\(y \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
, either 
\({y} \equiv {0} \pmod {{\lambda }}\)
 or 
\({y} \equiv {1} \pmod {{\lambda }}\)
. When 
\(\lambda = 1+\varphi \)
 or 
\(-\varphi \)
, then 
\(\lambda , 1-\lambda \in U(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ])\)
, so 
\(\lambda \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] = (1-\lambda )\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] = \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
, and the induction is simpler (see below).

                  
The fact that 
\(\lambda \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
 induces this partitioning (for 
\(\lambda \not = -\varphi \)
 or 
\(1+\varphi \)
) leads to the necessity of extrapolating on the 
right
 as well as the left.

                  
We now establish Eq. (
10
). First, because each is a subset of 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
, and the relevant convex sets (e.g., 
P
, 
\(\mu P\)
, etc.) are contained in 
P
, it is clear that each of 
\(0{\mathop {\star }}\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
, 
\(1{\mathop {\star }}\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
, 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P){\mathop {\star }}0\)
, 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P){\mathop {\star }}1\)
 are contained in 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
. For the reverse containment, consider any 
\(x \in \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
. There are three cases: 

                    

                      
(i)

                      
                        
\(x' \in {\left[ {0, 1-\mu }\right] }\)
: By Eq. (
15
), 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P){\mathop {\star }}0\)
 contains all 
\( x \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
 such that 
\( x' \in {\left[ {0,1-\mu }\right] }\)
. Thus this implies that 
\(x \in \mathord {\curlywedge }(P){\mathop {\star }}0\)
.

                      
                    

                    

                      
(ii)

                      
                        
\(x' \in {\left[ {\mu , 1}\right] }\)
: By Eq. (
16
), 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P){\mathop {\star }}1\)
 contains all 
\( x \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
 such that 
\( x' \in {\left[ {\mu ,1}\right] }\)
. Thus this implies that 
\(x \in \mathord {\curlywedge }(P){\mathop {\star }}1\)
.

                      
                    

                    

                      
(iii)

                      
                        
\(x' \in {\left( {1-\mu , \mu }\right) }\)
: We know that either 
\(x \equiv 0~\pmod {\lambda }\)
 or 
\(x \equiv 1~\pmod {\lambda }\)
. If the former, then 
\(x\in \lambda \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
, and hence (since 
\(x' \in {\left[ {0,\mu }\right] }\)
), by Eq. (
11
), we have 
\(x\in 0{\mathop {\star }}\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
. If the latter, then 
\(x \in 1-\lambda + \lambda \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
, and hence (since 
\(x' \in {\left[ {1-\mu , 1}\right] }\)
), by Eq. (
14
), we have 
\(x \in 1{\mathop {\star }}\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
.

                      
                    

                  
This establishes Eq. (
10
). If 
\(\lambda = 1+\varphi \)
, we can take 
\(\mu < 1/2\)
, and we would only have cases (i) and (ii) above. That is, for 
\(\lambda = 1+\varphi \)
, we find 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P) = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P){\mathop {\star }}0 \cup \mathord {\curlywedge }(P) {\mathop {\star }}1\)
.

                  
The proof is completed by an induction to show that 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P) \subseteq Q_\lambda \)
. We first consider 
\(\lambda = 2+\sqrt{2}\)
 and 
\(-1-\sqrt{3}\)
. For these values, 
\(|\lambda |, |1-\lambda | > 2\)
. Let 
x
 be any element of 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
. By Eq. (
10
), there exists 
\(y\in \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 such that 
\(x\in \{0{\mathop {\star }}y, 1{\mathop {\star }}y, y{\mathop {\star }}0, y{\mathop {\star }}1\}\)
. Suppose for the moment that 
\(|x| > \max (|\lambda |,|1-\lambda |)\)
. There are four cases: 

                    

                      
1.

                      
                        
If 
\(x = 0{\mathop {\star }}y\)
, then 
\(x = \lambda y\)
 and 
\(|\lambda |\cdot |y| = |x|\)
, hence 
\(|y| = \frac{|x|}{|\lambda |} < |x|\)
.

                      
                    

                    

                      
2.

                      
                        
If 
\(x = 1{\mathop {\star }}y\)
, then 
\(x = 1-\lambda +\lambda y\)
, so 
\(\lambda y = x - (1-\lambda )\)
, and 
\(|\lambda |\cdot |y| \le |x| + |1-\lambda | < 2|x|\)
. Thus 
\(|y|< \frac{2}{|\lambda |}|x| < |x|\)
.

                      
                    

                    

                      
3.

                      
                        
If 
\(x = y{\mathop {\star }}0\)
 then 
\(x = (1-\lambda )y\)
, and 
\(|y| = \frac{|x|}{|1-\lambda |} < |x|\)
.

                      
                    

                    

                      
4.

                      
                        
If 
\(x = y{\mathop {\star }}1\)
, then 
\(x = (1-\lambda )y + \lambda \)
. This implies 
\((1-\lambda ) y = x - \lambda \)
, and hence 
\(|y| \le \frac{|x| + |\lambda |}{|1-\lambda |}< \frac{2|x|}{|1-\lambda |} < |x|\)
.

                      
                    

                  
Thus for any 
\(x\in \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 with 
\(|x| > \max (|\lambda |,|1-\lambda |)\)
, we obtain a 
\(y\in \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 with norm less than |
x
| which yields 
x
 under extrapolation with 0 or 1 on the left or right (and thus 
\(y\in Q_\lambda \)
 implies 
\(x\in Q_\lambda \)
). Since 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 is uniformly discrete, we can repeat this process a finite number of times to reduce to a 
\(y\in \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 with 
\(|y| \le \max (|\lambda |,|1-\lambda |)\)
. It only remains to verify that the finite number of 
\(y \in \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 satisfying 
\(|y| \le \max (|\lambda |,|1-\lambda |)\)
 are in 
\(Q_{\lambda }\)
. But these are exactly the points 0, 1, 
\(1-\lambda \)
, and 
\(\lambda \)
 itself, all of which are in 
\(Q_{\lambda }\)
.

                  
For 
\(\lambda = 1+\varphi \)
, while 
\(|\lambda | = 1+ \varphi > 2\)
, we have 
\(1< |1-\lambda |= \varphi < 2\)
. Thus the above argument doesn’t work. This case is still simpler, however, since we only need cases (i) and (ii) above. This is Berman and Moody’s argument, which we review here. We consider 
\(x\in \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 with 
\(|x| > |1-\lambda |^3 =\varphi ^3\)
. If 
\(x = y{\mathop {\star }}0\)
, then 
\(x = (1-\lambda )y\)
, so 
\(|y| = \frac{|x|}{|1-\lambda |} < |x|\)
. If 
\(x = y{\mathop {\star }}1\)
, then 
\(x = (1-\lambda )y + \lambda \)
 and 
\(|y| \le \frac{|x| + |\lambda |}{|1-\lambda |} = \frac{|x| + 1+\varphi }{\varphi } < \frac{1}{\varphi }\left( 1+\frac{1+\varphi }{\varphi ^3}\right) |x|\)
. Now observe that,
$$\begin{aligned} \frac{1}{\varphi }\left( 1+\frac{1+\varphi }{\varphi ^3}\right) = \frac{\varphi ^3+\varphi +1}{\varphi ^4} = \frac{\varphi ^3+\varphi ^2}{\varphi ^2(\varphi +1)}= \frac{\varphi ^2(\varphi +1)}{\varphi ^2(\varphi +1)} = 1, \end{aligned}$$
so 
\(|y| < |x|\)
. As before, we reduce to a 
\(y\in \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 with norm 
\(\le \varphi ^3 = 1+2\varphi \)
. The relevant points in 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 are 
\(0, 1, \lambda , 1-\lambda \)
 and 
\(-1-\lambda = \lambda {\mathop {\star }}0\)
, all of which are in 
\(Q_\lambda \)
. 
\(\square \)

                
The foregoing proof requires either 
\(\lambda \)
 or 
\(1-\lambda \)
 to be a unit. This does not hold for 
\(\lambda = \frac{-3-\sqrt{17}}{2}\)
, and hence this requires a different technique (although still not relying on 
\(\beta \)
-expansions), developed in Section 
6.2
. It turns out that 
\(Q_\lambda = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 for 
\(\lambda = \frac{-3-\sqrt{17}}{2}\)
 as well (see Proposition 
8.4
 in Section 
8.3
 below). Thus from Lemma 
6.7
, Theorem 
6.8
, and Proposition 
8.4
, the following is immediate:

                  
Theorem 6.9

                  
If 
\(\lambda \)
 is a real quadratic sPV number, then 
\(Q_\lambda = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 if and only if 
\(\lambda \)
 is one of the cases (a), (b), (c), or (d) of Lemma 
6.6
.

                
This can be further strengthened. In [
40
, Theorem 15], Pinch shows that if 
\(\lambda \)
 is real and not an integer, and 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is discrete, then 
\(\lambda \)
 has a conjugate in (0, 1). Hence if 
\(\lambda \)
 is real and quadratic and 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is discrete, we can conclude that 
\(\lambda \)
 is sPV. Conversely, we know that if 
\(\lambda \)
 is sPV, then 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is discrete. Thus for real, quadratic 
\(\lambda \)
, 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is discrete iff 
\(\lambda \)
 is sPV. We thus have the following corollary of Theorem 
6.9
 (recall that if 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {R}\)
 and 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is discrete, then 
\(\lambda \)
 is an algebraic integer by a Theorem of Pinch [
40
, Theorem 8]).

                  
Corollary 6.10

                  
If 
\(\lambda \)
 is real and quadratic, and 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is discrete, then 
\(Q_\lambda = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 if and only if 
\(\lambda \)
 is one of the cases (a), (b), (c), or (d) of Lemma 
6.6
.

                
As per the discussion just after Definition 
6.1
, Theorem 
6.9
 also implies the following immediately:

                  
Corollary 6.11

                  
(Masáková et al. [
30
]) If 
\(\lambda \)
 is unitary Pisot, then 
\(Q_\lambda = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 iff 
\(\lambda \)
 is one of the cases (a), (b), and (c) of Lemma 
6.6
.

                
6.2 
The Covering Method
The underlying strategy of the proof of Theorem 
6.8
 is to find a finite cover of the interval 
\(P = [0,1]\)
 (in that case, simply the intervals 
\([0,1-\mu ]\)
, 
\([1-\mu ,\mu ]\)
, 
\([\mu , 1]\)
), such that for any 
\(x \in \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
, depending on which element of the cover 
\(x'\)
 is contained in, we can write 
x
 as 
\(b\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}y\)
 or 
\(y \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}b\)
, for 
\(b \in \{0,1\}\)
. Since, in all of these four cases, 
\(|y| < |x|\)
, an inductive proof then suffices to show that 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P) \subseteq Q_\lambda \)
.
In this section we generalize that proof, which yields further relative density results, including for case (d) of Theorem 
6.9
, 
\(\lambda = \frac{-3-\sqrt{17}}{2}\)
. That case is covered by Proposition 
8.4
 in Part II.
In particular, the technique allows us to use a fixed set of “seed” points other than 
\(\{0,1\}\)
. Indeed, it turns out that when 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 has a unit, Theorem 
6.12
 below shows that it is always possible to adjoin finitely many points 
Y
 in 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 to 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 such that it equals 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
, i.e., for some finite 
\(Y \subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
, 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P) = Q_\lambda (Y)\)
. Thus, if we can explicitly find such a set 
Y
 that is a subset of 
\(Q_\lambda \)
, then 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 itself is cut and project in this way: 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P) = Q_\lambda \)
. However, the result yields more than relative density results. As we will see later on, even when 
\(Y \not \subseteq Q_\lambda \)
, the results of this section enable us to prove that certain elements of 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 are 
not
 in 
\(Q_\lambda \)
, again in certain cases.
The proof of Theorem 
6.12
 extends that of Theorem 
6.8
 in two ways. First, the size of the cover of 
P
 is larger, but still finite (depending on the size of the fundamental unit in 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
). Secondly, the proof that 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P) \subseteq Q_\lambda \)
 proceeds by inducting on |
z
| as before, but now a larger finite set 
Y
 of seed points plays the role that 
\(\{0,1\}\)
 played in the proof of Theorem 
6.8
. In one respect, the induction is simpler, since we find 
z
 can be written as 
\(x\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}y\)
 where 
\(x \in Y\)
 and hence (as shown below) 
\(|z| > |y|\)
. That is, 
z
 is the extrapolant of one of a finite number of seed points on the 
left only
 with a smaller point on the 
right
.
6.2.1 
Quadratics and Non-Real Cubics
We begin by considering real quadratic sPV numbers and non-real cubic (degree 3) sPV numbers. In either case, 
\(\lambda \)
 has a unique conjugate 
\(\mu \in {\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
. It also has 
\(\overline{\lambda }\)
 as its other conjugate. In this section, we again let 
\(x \mapsto x'\)
 be the unique field isomorphism
Footnote 
13
\(\mathbb {Q}(\lambda )\rightarrow \mathbb {Q}(\mu )\)
 that maps 
\(\lambda \)
 to 
\(\mu \)
, and we define 
\(P:= {\left[ {0,1}\right] }\)
 and 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P) = \mathord {\curlywedge }({\left[ {0,1}\right] }):= \{x\in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \mid x'\in {\left[ {0,1}\right] }\}\)
 as before. So if 
\(\lambda \notin \mathbb {R}\)
, we have
$$ \begin{aligned} \mathord {\curlywedge }(P) = \{a+b\lambda +c\lambda ^2 \mid a,b,c\in \mathbb {Z}\;\mathrel { \& }\;0 \le a+b\mu +c\mu ^2 \le 1\}\;. \end{aligned}$$

                    (17)
                
Since 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 is a cut-and-project (model) set, it is relatively dense in either 
\(\mathbb {R}\)
 or 
\(\mathbb {C}\cong \mathbb {R}^2\)
.
The next theorem shows that, for any sPV 
\(\lambda \)
 with a unique conjugate in 
\({\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
, if there exists an 
\(\alpha \in \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 that is a unit of 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
 other than 1, then there exists a finite set 
\(Y\subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 such that 
\(Q_\alpha (Y) = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
. If, in addition, 
\(\alpha \in Q_\lambda \)
, then 
\(Q_\lambda (Y) = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
. The theorem also gives sufficient conditions on 
Y
 such that 
\(Q_\alpha (Y) = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
. These conditions are sometimes not completely necessary, however, as one can often get by with a smaller “seed” set 
Y
. This will be true, for example, in the case where 
\(\lambda = -(3+\sqrt{13})/2\)
 (Proposition 
9.1
 in Part II). This theorem is our main tool for proving relative density of 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 for such 
\(\lambda \)
.

                    
Theorem 6.12

                    
Let 
\(\lambda \)
 be a strong PV number with a unique conjugate in 
\({\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
 (which implies 
\(\lambda \)
 is either real quadratic or non-real cubic). Let 
\(P:= {\left[ {0,1}\right] }\)
 and 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P):= \{x\in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \mid x'\in {\left[ {0,1}\right] }\}\)
, where the map 
\(x\mapsto x'\)
 is as defined above. Suppose 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 contains a unit 
\(\alpha \)
 of 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
 other than
Footnote 
14
 1. Let 
\(\beta := \alpha ' \in P\)
. Then 
\(|\alpha |>1\)
, 
\(0<\beta <1\)
, and there exists a finite set 
\(X\subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 such that
$$\begin{aligned} {\left[ {0,1}\right] } \subseteq \bigcup _{x\in X} {\left[ {(1-\beta )x',(1-\beta )x' + \beta }\right] }\;. \end{aligned}$$

                    (18)
                
Moreover, for any such 
X
, letting
$$\begin{aligned} M := \left( \frac{|\alpha -1|}{|\alpha |-1}\right) \max _{x\in X} |x|\;, \end{aligned}$$

                    (19)
                
the set
$$\begin{aligned} Y := \mathord {\curlywedge }(P) \cap {\left[ {-M,M}\right] } \end{aligned}$$

                    (20)
                
is finite, and 
\(Q_\alpha (Y) = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
. If, in addition, 
\(\alpha \in Q_\lambda \)
, then 
\(Q_\lambda (Y) = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
.

                  
                    
Proof

                    
Observe that 
\(\beta \in {\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
, since 
\(\alpha \in \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 and 
\(\alpha \notin \{0,1\}\)
. This in turn implies 
\(\alpha \notin \mathbb {Z}\)
, and thus, since 
\(\lambda \)
 has prime degree and 
\(\alpha \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
, it must be that 
\(\alpha \)
 has the same degree as 
\(\lambda \)
, and by standard facts of algebra, the conjugates of 
\(\alpha \)
 are 
\(\alpha \)
, 
\(\beta \)
, and 
\(\overline{\alpha }\)
. If 
\(\lambda \)
 is real quadratic, then 
\(\alpha \in \mathbb {R}\)
 and so 
\(\alpha = \overline{\alpha }\)
 making 
\(\alpha \)
 real quadratic. If 
\(\lambda \)
 is non-real qubic, then 
\(\alpha \)
 is also qubic, whence 
\(\alpha \ne \overline{\alpha }\)
 and so 
\(\alpha \notin \mathbb {R}\)
. Since 
\(\alpha \)
 is a unit, we have 
\(N(\alpha ) = \pm 1\)
. If 
\(\alpha \in \mathbb {R}\)
, then 
\(N(\alpha ) = \alpha \beta \)
, and 
\(N(\alpha ) = \alpha \overline{\alpha }\beta \)
 otherwise. Thus 
\(\beta = 1/|\alpha |\)
 if 
\(\alpha \in \mathbb {R}\)
 and 
\(\beta = 1/|\alpha |^2\)
 otherwise. In either case, we have
Footnote 
15
\(|\alpha | > 1\)
.

                    
Also observe that 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 is 
\(\alpha \)
-convex: for any 
\(u,v\in \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
, 
 and 
 (because 
\(u',v',\beta \in P\)
). Then since 
\(Y\subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
, we have 
\(Q_\alpha (Y)\subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
. It therefore suffices for the first part to show that 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\subseteq Q_\alpha (Y)\)
.

                    
There exists a finite 
X
 satisfying Eq. (
18
), because 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)'\)
 is dense in 
\({\left[ {0,1}\right] }\)
.
Footnote 
16
 (Notice, however, that 
X
 must contain both 0 and 1.) The quantity 
M
 is well-defined (since 
\(|\alpha | >1\)
), and in addition, 
\(X\subseteq Y\)
 because 
\(|\alpha -1|/(|\alpha |-1) \ge 1\)
.

                    
We show for any 
\(z\in \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 that 
z
 is in 
\(Q_\alpha (Y)\)
. We do this by induction on |
z
|, which is allowed, because 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 is uniformly discrete. If 
\(|z| \le M\)
, then 
\(z\in Y\)
 and we are done, so suppose 
\(|z|>M\)
. Choose 
\(x\in X\)
 such that 
\(z' \in {\left[ {(1-\beta )x',(1-\beta )x' + \beta }\right] }\)
, and let 
\(y:= x \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{1/\alpha }}z\)
. One can quickly check that 
. Moreover, 
\(y\in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
, since 
\(\alpha \)
 is a unit in 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
. We next show that 
\(|y| < |z|\)
. The condition 
\(|y| < |z|\)
 is equivalent to
$$\begin{aligned} {\left| {\left( 1-\frac{1}{\alpha }\right) x + \frac{1}{\alpha }z}\right| } < |z|\;, \end{aligned}$$
This holds provided 
\(|(1-1/\alpha )x| + |z/\alpha | < |z|\)
, or equivalently, 
\(|\alpha -1|\cdot |x| + |z| < |\alpha |\cdot |z|\)
. Since 
\(x\in X\)
, we have 
\(|x| \le M(|\alpha |-1)/|\alpha -1|\)
, and so the inequality is satisfied.

                    
It remains to show that 
\(0 \le y' \le 1\)
, thus putting 
y
 in 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
. This suffices: applying the inductive hypothesis to 
y
 to get 
\(y\in Q_\alpha (Y)\)
, we get 
 as desired. We have
$$\begin{aligned} y' = (x\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{1/\alpha }}z)' = x' \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{1/\beta }}z' = \frac{1}{\beta }\left( (\beta -1)x'+z'\right) \;. \end{aligned}$$
Since 
\(z' \in {\left[ {(1-\beta )x',(1-\beta )x' + \beta }\right] }\)
, we then have
$$\begin{aligned} \frac{1}{\beta }\left( (\beta -1)x' + (1-\beta )x'\right) \le y' \le \frac{1}{\beta }\left( (\beta -1)x'+(1-\beta )x' + \beta \right) \;; \end{aligned}$$
that is, 
\(0\le y' \le 1\)
. Thus by induction, we get 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\subseteq Q_\alpha (Y)\)
, and hence, 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P) = Q_\alpha (Y)\)
.

                    
If 
\(\alpha \in Q_\lambda \)
, then by Lemma 
2.17
 and the fact that 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 is 
\(\lambda \)
-convex,
$$\begin{aligned} \mathord {\curlywedge }(P) = Q_\alpha (Y) \subseteq Q_\lambda (Y) \subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\;, \end{aligned}$$
and thus all sets above are equal. 
\(\square \)

                  
                    
Remark

                    
We have proved something stronger. Define 
\(Y^{(0)}, Y^{(1)}, Y^{(2)}, \ldots \)
 inductively as follows: 
\(Y^{(0)} := Y\)
 and 
 for 
\(n\ge 0\)
. Then the proof shows that 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P) = \bigcup _{n=0}^\infty Y^{(n)}\)
. This was shown for the case where 
\(\lambda = \alpha = -\varphi \)
 by Berman & Moody [
8
]. 
\(\square \)

                  
If 
\(\lambda \)
 is real quadratic, we can get sufficient bounds on the size of 
X
 in Theorem 
6.12
 based on results in the theory of uniformly distributed distributions. See Section 
8.1
.
6.2.2 
Higher Degrees
Theorem 
6.12
 is specific to quadratic sPV’s or non-real sPV’s of degree 3. In this section we turn to nontrivial sPV’s of arbitrary degree, real or complex. Theorem 
6.13
 below will be used to show relative density in these cases. Relative density under the same hypotheses also holds for trivial sPV numbers as well, but via a different proof [
17
, Proposition 15.43].
For any subset 
R
 of a topological space, we let 
\({{R}^int }\)
 denote the interior of 
R
.
Fix a nontrivial strong PV number 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {C}\)
 with 
\(k\ge 1\)
 many conjugates. See Definition 
4.13
 for notation; Thus, for any 
\(x\in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
 and 
\(0\le i<k\)
, let 
\(x'_i\)
 denote the conjugate of 
x
 that is the image of 
x
 under the unique ring homomorphism 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\rightarrow \mathbb {Z}[\mu _i]\)
 mapping 
\(\lambda \)
 to 
\(\mu _i\)
, and hence 
\(x^{\mathop {*}}= (x'_0,x'_1,\ldots ,x'_{k-1})\)
, viewed as a column vector. We also let 
\(\Lambda _x:= diag (x'_0,x'_1,\ldots ,x'_{k-1})\)
, the diagonal matrix with the 
\(x'_i\)
’s along the diagonal. Recall Definition 
2.13
, according to which we may used 
\(\Lambda _x\)
 in place of 
\(\lambda \)
, and define the extrapolation operation 
\({\mathop {\star }}_{\Lambda }\)
 in those terms. Set 
\(P:= {\left[ {0,1}\right] }^{\times k}\)
 and for any 
\(S\subseteq \mathbb {R}^k\)
 let 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(S)\)
 denote 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }^{(\lambda )}(S)\)
, recalling that this is 
\(\{x\in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \mid x^{\mathop {*}}\in S\}\)
.
The following generalizes Theorem 
6.12
, but with any sub-box 
S
 of 
P
 that can be arbitrarily close to, but never equals, 
P
.

                    
Theorem 6.13

                    
Suppose 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 contains an 
\(\alpha \ne 1\)
 that is a unit of 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
. Fix any 
\(0<\varepsilon <1/2\)
, and define 
\(S:= {\left[ {\varepsilon ,1-\varepsilon }\right] }^{\times k}\)
. There exists a finite set 
\(Y\subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 such that 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(S) \subseteq Q_\alpha (Y)\)
. If, in addition, 
\(\alpha \in Q_\lambda \)
, then 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(S) \subseteq Q_\lambda (Y)\)
.

                  
                    
Proof

                    
This proof follows the general approach of that of Theorem 
6.12
. Since 
\(\alpha \in \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
, we have 
\(0<\alpha '_i<1\)
 for all 
\(0\le i<k\)
. Since 
\(\alpha \)
 is a unit, 
\(1/\alpha \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
, and 
\((1/\alpha )' = (1/\alpha '_0,1/\alpha '_1,\ldots ,1/\alpha '_{k-1})\)
. Set 
\(\Lambda := \Lambda _{\alpha } = diag (\alpha '_0,\ldots ,\alpha '_{k-1})\)
. For any 
\(\textbf{x}\in \mathbb {R}^k\)
, define
$$\begin{aligned} J_{\textbf{x}} := \textbf{x}\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\Lambda }}S = \{\textbf{z}\in \mathbb {R}^k \mid (\exists \textbf{y}\in S)\;\textbf{z} = \textbf{x}\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\Lambda }}\textbf{y}\} = \{\textbf{z}\in \mathbb {R}^k \mid \textbf{x}\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\Lambda ^{-1}}} \textbf{z} \in S\}\;. \end{aligned}$$
For the last equality, one can readily check that for all 
\(\textbf{x},\textbf{y},\textbf{z}\in \mathbb {R}^k\)
, if 
\(\textbf{x}\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\Lambda }}\textbf{y} = \textbf{z}\)
 then 
\(\textbf{y} = \textbf{x} \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\Lambda ^{-1}}} \textbf{z}\)
 and conversely.

                  
                    
Claim 6.14

                    
For all 
\(\textbf{x}\in \mathbb {R}^k\)
,
$$ J_{\textbf{x}} = {\left[ {x_0\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\alpha '_0}}\varepsilon ,\;x_0\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\alpha '_0}}(1-\varepsilon )}\right] } \times \cdots \times {\left[ {x_{k-1}\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\alpha '_{k-1}}}\varepsilon ,\;x_{k-1}\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\alpha '_{k-1}}}(1-\varepsilon )}\right] }.$$

                  
                    
Proof

                    
For 
\(\textbf{z}\in \mathbb {R}^k\)
, set 
\(\textbf{y}:= \textbf{x}\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\Lambda ^{-1}}} \textbf{z}\)
, so that 
\(\textbf{z} = \textbf{x}\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\Lambda }}\textbf{y}\)
. Now the linear map 
\(f_i(y):= x_i \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\alpha '_i}} y\)
 is monotone increasing in 
y
, and hence 
\(f_i([\varepsilon ,1-\varepsilon ]) = [x_i\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\alpha '_i}}\varepsilon , x_i\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\alpha '_i}}(1-\varepsilon )]\)
. The tensor product mapping 
\(f_0\otimes \dots \otimes f_{k-1}\)
 maps 
S
 to 
\((f_0([\varepsilon ,1-\varepsilon ]),\dots ,f_{k-1}([\varepsilon ,1-\varepsilon ]) = {\left[ {x_0\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\alpha '_0}}\varepsilon ,\;x_0\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\alpha '_0}}(1-\varepsilon )}\right] } \times \cdots \times {\left[ {x_{k-1}\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\alpha '_{k-1}}}\varepsilon ,\;x_{k-1}\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\alpha '_{k-1}}}(1-\varepsilon )}\right] } = J_{\textbf{x}} \)
. 
\(\square \)

                  
Note then that
$${{J_{\textbf{x}}}^int } = {\left( {x_0\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\alpha '_0}}\varepsilon ,\;x_0\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\alpha '_0}}(1-\varepsilon )}\right) } \times \cdots \times {\left( {x_{k-1}\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\alpha '_{k-1}}}\varepsilon ,\;x_{k-1}\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\alpha '_{k-1}}}(1-\varepsilon )}\right) }.$$
For 
\(\textbf{z}\in \mathbb {R}^k\)
, define
$$\begin{aligned} J^{-1}_{\textbf{z}} := \{ \textbf{x}\in \mathbb {R}^k \mid \textbf{z}\in {{J_{\textbf{x}}}^int } \}\;. \end{aligned}$$
By Claim 
6.14
, with 
\(\Lambda \)
 replaced by 
\(\Lambda ^{-1}\)
 and open rather than closed intervals, we also obtain
$$\begin{aligned} J^{-1}_z&= {\left( {(1-\varepsilon )\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{1/(1-\alpha '_0)}} z_0,\;\varepsilon \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{1/(1-\alpha '_0)}} z_0}\right) }\times \cdots \\  &\quad \times {\left( {(1-\varepsilon )\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{1/(1-\alpha '_{k-1})}} z_{k-1},\;\varepsilon \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{1/(1-\alpha '_{k-1})}} z_{k-1}}\right) }\;.\end{aligned} $$

                    
Remark

                    
Letting 
\(\textbf{1}= (1,1,\ldots ,1)\in \mathbb {R}^k\)
, we see that 
\(J^{-1}_{\textbf{z}}\)
 is an open box with opposite corners 
\((1-\varepsilon )\textbf{1}\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{(I-\Lambda )^{-1}}} \textbf{z}\)
 and 
\(\varepsilon \textbf{1}\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{(I-\Lambda )^{-1}}} \textbf{z}\)
. 
\(\square \)

                  
                    
Claim 6.15

                    
\(J^{-1}_{\textbf{z}} \cap {{P}^int } \ne \emptyset \)
 for all 
\(\textbf{z}\in S\)
.

                  
                    
Proof

                    
It suffices to show for every 
\(0\le i<k\)
 that if 
\(\varepsilon \le z_i \le 1-\varepsilon \)
, then
$$\begin{aligned} (1-\varepsilon )\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{1/(1-\alpha '_i)}} z_i < 1\;\; \text{ and } \;\; \varepsilon \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{1/(1-\alpha '_i)}} z_i > 0\;. \end{aligned}$$
Fixing 
i
 and letting 
\(\beta := \alpha '_i\)
, we get the following chain of equivalences for the first inequality above:
$$\begin{aligned}&\left( 1-\frac{1}{1-\beta }\right) (1-\varepsilon ) + \frac{1}{1-\beta } z_i< 1\\&\quad \iff z_i - \beta (1-\varepsilon )< 1-\beta \iff z_i < 1-\beta \varepsilon \;, \end{aligned}$$
and the last inequality is certainly true if 
\(z_i \le 1-\varepsilon \)
 (because 
\(\beta < 1\)
). Similarly, for the second inequality:
$$\begin{aligned} \left( 1-\frac{1}{1-\beta }\right) \varepsilon + \frac{1}{1-\beta } z_i&> 0\iff &    &z_i - \beta \varepsilon&> 0\iff &    &z_i&> \beta \varepsilon \;, \end{aligned}$$
which is again true if 
\(z_i\ge \varepsilon \)
. 
\(\square \)

                  
We know that 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)'\)
 is dense in 
P
 by Fact 
4.19
. For every 
\(\textbf{z}\in S\)
, choose an element 
\(\textbf{x}_{\textbf{z}}\)
 of 
\(J^{-1}_{\textbf{z}} \cap \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)^{\mathop {*}}\)
 in some standard way. Such an element exists by density and because 
\(J^{-1}_{\textbf{z}} \cap {{P}^int }\)
 is open and nonempty by Claim 
6.15
. Then 
\(\textbf{z}\in {{J_{\textbf{x}_{\textbf{z}}}}^int }\)
 for all 
\(\textbf{z}\in S\)
, meaning that the family 
\(\{{{J_{\textbf{x}_{\textbf{z}}}}^int } \mid \textbf{z}\in S\}\)
 is an open cover of 
S
. Since 
S
 is compact, we may choose a finite subcover, indexed by a finite set 
\(X\subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 so that 
\(\{{{J_{x^{\mathop {*}}}}^int } \mid x\in X\}\)
 covers 
S
.

                    
Remark

                    
It is only necessary that 
S
 be covered by the 
\(J_{x^{\mathop {*}}}\)
 themselves, not their interiors. The interiors were only used to apply the compactness argument. Thus one may be able to find a smaller set 
X
 such that the 
\(J_{x^{\mathop {*}}}\)
 cover 
S
. 
\(\square \)

                  
The rest of the proof is very close to that of Theorem 
6.12
, with only some subtle differences. An argument similar to the one used in the proof of that theorem shows that 
\(|\alpha | > 1\)
. Let
$$\begin{aligned} Y := X \cup \{y\in \mathord {\curlywedge }(S) : |y| \le M\}\;, \end{aligned}$$
where
$$\begin{aligned} M := \left( \frac{|\alpha -1|}{|\alpha |-1}\right) \max _{x\in X} |x|\;. \end{aligned}$$
Y
 is certainly finite, because 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 is uniformly discrete. We now apply Lemma 
6.16
, given below, to obtain the first part of the theorem.
If 
\(\alpha \in Q_\lambda \)
, then 
\(Q_\alpha (Y) \subseteq Q_\lambda (Y)\)
 by Lemma 
2.17
, and the rest of the theorem follows. 
\(\square \)

                    
Remark

                    
There is a possible trade-off between the choice of 
X
 versus 
Y
. It is not necessary that 
\(X\subseteq Y\)
, only that 
\(X\subseteq Q_\alpha (Y)\)
. So, e.g., if we choose 
X
 so that 
\(X\subseteq Q_\alpha \)
 (which is always possible), then we can just let 
\(Y:= \{y\in \mathord {\curlywedge }(S): |y| \le M\}\)
 (albeit with a possibly bigger value of 
M
). 
\(\square \)

                  
                    
Remark

                    
Theorems 
6.8
 and 
6.12
 use essentially the same technique to induct on |
z
|. We abstract that process in the following Lemma, which we applied in Theorem 
6.13
, and will also be used in subsequent case studies. 
\(\square \)

                  
                    
Lemma 6.16

                    
Let 
\(\lambda \)
, 
P
, 
S
, 
\(\alpha \)
, and 
k
 be as in Theorem 
6.13
. Suppose there exists a finite set 
\(X \subseteq \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
 such that, setting 
\(\Lambda := diag (\alpha '_0,\ldots ,\alpha '_{k-1})\)
 and 
\(J_{x^{\mathop {*}}}:= x^{\mathop {*}}\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\Lambda }}S\)
 for each 
\(x \in X\)
, it holds that 
\(S \subseteq \bigcup \limits _{x \in X} J_{x^{\mathop {*}}}\)
. Let 
\(Y = X \cup \{y \in \mathord {\curlywedge }(S) : |y| \le M\}\)
, where 
\(M = \frac{|\alpha -1|}{|\alpha |-1} \max _{x\in X} |x|\)
. Then 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(S) \subseteq Q_\alpha (Y)\)
.

                  
                    
Proof

                    
We show for any 
\(z\in \mathord {\curlywedge }(S)\)
 that 
z
 is in 
\(Q_\alpha (Y)\)
. We do this by induction on |
z
| as in Theorem 
6.12
. The only difference here is that, due to the arbitrary degree of 
\(\lambda \)
, there are multiple conjugates. If 
\(|z| \le M\)
, then 
\(z\in Y \subseteq Q_\alpha (Y)\)
 and we are done, so suppose 
\(z \in \mathord {\curlywedge }(S)\)
 with 
\(|z|>M\)
. By definition of 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(S)\)
, 
\(z^{\mathop {*}}\in S\)
. Since 
\(\{J_{x^{\mathop {*}}} \mid x \in X\}\)
 covers 
S
, for some 
\(x\in X\)
 we have 
\(z^{\mathop {*}}\in J_{x^{\mathop {*}}}\)
. Let 
\(y := x \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{1/\alpha }}z\)
, so that 
. As before, since 
\(\alpha \)
 is a unit in 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
, we have 
\(y\in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
. We also have that 
\(|y| < |z|\)
 by an argument identical to the one for Theorem 
6.12
, repeated here in more detail:
$$\begin{aligned} |y|&= {\left| { \left( 1-\frac{1}{\alpha }\right) x + \frac{1}{\alpha }z}\right| } \le {\left| {1-\frac{1}{\alpha }}\right| } \cdot |x| + \frac{|z|}{|\alpha |} = \frac{1}{|\alpha |}\left( |\alpha -1|\cdot |x| + |z|\right) \\&\le \frac{1}{|\alpha |}\left( |\alpha -1|\cdot \frac{|\alpha |-1}{|\alpha -1|}M + |z|\right) = \frac{1}{|\alpha |}((|\alpha |-1)M + |z|) \\&\quad < \frac{1}{|\alpha |}((|\alpha | - 1)|z| + |z|) = |z|\;. \end{aligned}$$
It remains to show that 
\(y^{\mathop {*}}\in S\)
, thus putting 
y
 in 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(S)\)
. This suffices: applying the inductive hypothesis to 
y
 to get 
\(y\in Q_\alpha (Y)\)
, and given that 
\(x \in Y \subseteq Q_\alpha (Y)\)
, we get 
 as desired. We have
$$\begin{aligned} y^{\mathop {*}}= (x\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{1/\alpha }}z)^{\mathop {*}}= x^{\mathop {*}}\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\Lambda ^{-1}}} z^{\mathop {*}}\;. \end{aligned}$$
Since 
\(z^{\mathop {*}}\in J_{x^{\mathop {*}}}\)
, we then have 
\(y^{\mathop {*}}\in x^{\mathop {*}}\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\Lambda ^{-1}}} J_{x^{\mathop {*}}} = x^{\mathop {*}}\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\Lambda ^{-1}}} (x^{\mathop {*}}\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\Lambda }} S) = S\)
 by the definition of 
\(J_{x^{\mathop {*}}}\)
 and a simple calculation. We conclude by induction that 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(S) \subseteq Q_\alpha (Y)\)
. 
\(\square \)

                  
6.3 
Affine Embedding

                  
Definition 6.17

                  
Let 
R
 be a commutative ring and 
A
, 
B
 subsets of 
R
. If 
\(\rho _{a,b}(A) \subseteq B\)
 for some 
\(a,b\in R\)
 such that 
\((a-b)\)
 is not a zero divisor in 
R
, then we say 
A
 
affinely embeds
 in 
B
, and write 
\(A \mathrel {\hookrightarrow }B\)
. If 
\(A \mathrel {\hookrightarrow }B\)
 and 
\(B \mathrel {\hookrightarrow }A\)
, we say 
A
 and 
B
 are 
affine-equivalent
, and write 
\(A \mathrel {\leftrightharpoons }B\)
.

                
If 
R
 has no zero-divisors, then 
\(\rho _{a,b}\)
 is a one-to-one map just when 
\(a\ne b\)
. Thus the condition on 
a
 and 
b
 in Definition 
6.17
 is equivalent to 
\(\rho _{a,b}\)
 being one-to-one, hence the use of the term “embedding.” We will only apply Definition 
6.17
 when 
R
 has no zero-divisors.
The 
\(\mathrel {\hookrightarrow }\)
 relation is seen to be reflexive by setting 
\(a:=0\)
 and 
\(b:=1\)
. It is also transitive, making 
\(\mathrel {\leftrightharpoons }\)
 an equivalence relation. To see that 
\(\mathrel {\hookrightarrow }\)
 is transitive, one can first verify directly that for any 
\(a,b,c,d\in R\)
, 
\(\rho _{c,d}\circ \rho _{a,b} = \rho _{u,v}\)
, where 
\(u := \rho _{c,d}(a)\)
 and 
\(v := \rho _{c,d}(b)\)
. Thus for all 
\(A,B,C\subseteq R\)
, if 
\(\rho _{a,b}(A)\subseteq B\)
 and 
\(\rho _{c,d}(B)\subseteq C\)
, then 
\(\rho _{u,v}(A)\subseteq \rho _{c,d}(B)\subseteq C\)
. Furthermore, we have 
\(u-v = (a-b)(d-c)\)
, so if 
\(x(a-b) \ne 0\)
 for all nonzero 
\(x\in R\)
 and 
\(y(c-d)\ne 0\)
 for all nonzero 
\(y\in R\)
, then 
\(x(u-v)\ne 0\)
 for all nonzero 
\(x\in R\)
, satisfying the stated condition for 
u
 and 
v
.

                  
Fact 6.18

                  
Let 
F
 be either 
\(\mathbb {R}\)
 or 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
. Let 
A
 and 
B
 be subsets of 
F
 such that 
\(A\mathrel {\hookrightarrow }B\)
. 

                    

                      
1.

                      
                        
If 
B
 is uniformly discrete, then 
A
 is uniformly discrete.

                      
                    

                    

                      
2.

                      
                        
If 
A
 is relatively dense in 
F
, then 
B
 is relatively dense in 
F
.

                      
                    

                    

                      
3.

                      
                        
For any 
\(\lambda \in F\)
, 
\(Q_\lambda (A) \mathrel {\hookrightarrow }Q_\lambda (B)\)
 and 
\(K_\lambda (A) \mathrel {\hookrightarrow }K_\lambda (B)\)
. (This follows from Lemma 
2.12
.)

                      
                    

                  

                
                  
Lemma 6.19

                  
Let 
T
 be any finite subset of 
\(\mathbb {Q}[x]\)
. Then 
\(T \mathrel {\hookrightarrow }Q_{[x]}\)
. Furthermore, there exist distinct 
\(a,b\in \mathbb {Z}[x]\)
 such that 
\(\rho _{a,b}(T)\subseteq Q_{[x]}\)
 and the only roots of 
\(a-b\)
 are 0 and 1.

                
                  
Proof

                  
Suppose 
\(T = \{f_1,\ldots ,f_n\}\)
, where 
\(f_i \in \mathbb {Q}[x]\)
 for each 
\(1 \le i \le n\)
. Choose 
\(d,\ell , k \in \mathbb {Z}^+\)
 sufficiently large that for all 
\(1 \le i \le n\)
, 
\(df_i \in \mathbb {Z}[x]\)
 and 
\((df_i(\lambda )+\ell )\cdot \lambda ^k(1-\lambda )^k \in {\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
 for any 
\(\lambda \in {\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
. Then, writing 
\(h(x) := x^k(1-x)^k\)
 and 
\(g_i := (df_i+\ell )\cdot h\)
, we find that 
\(g_i\in \mathbb {Z}[x]\)
 and 
\(g_i(\lambda ) \in {\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
 for any 
\(\lambda \in {\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
. By the characterization given in Theorem 
3.6
, this implies 
\(g_i \in Q_{[x]}\)
. Now define the polynomials,
$$\begin{aligned} a&:= \ell h\;,&b&:= a+dh = (\ell +d) h\;. \end{aligned}$$
Then 
\(a,b\in \mathbb {Z}[x]\subseteq \mathbb {Q}[x]\)
 and 
\(a\ne b\)
