. In fact, 
\(b-a = dh\)
, whose only irreducible monic factors in 
\(\mathbb {Q}[x]\)
 are 
x
 and 
\(x-1\)
. Hence the only roots of 
\(b-a\)
 are 0 and 1. Moreover, for 
\(1\le i\le n\)
,
$$\begin{aligned} \rho _{a,b}(f_i) = (1-f_i)a + f_ib = a + f_i (b - a) = \ell h + df_i h = (df_i + \ell ) h = g_i\;. \end{aligned}$$
Thus 
\(\rho _{a,b}(T) \subseteq Q_{[x]}\)
, and hence 
\(T \mathrel {\hookrightarrow }Q_{[x]}\)
 via 
a
 and 
b
 satisfying the additional hypothesis. 
\(\square \)

                
                  
Remark

                  
Lemma 
6.19
 holds just as well for infinite 
T
, provided 
d
, 
\(\ell \)
, and 
k
 exist with the requisite properties, i.e., 
\(\bigcup _{f\in T}\{ |f(\lambda )|: \lambda \in {\left( {0,1}\right) }\}\)
 is bounded and all coefficients in elements of 
T
 share a common denominator. 
\(\square \)

                
                  
Lemma 6.20

                  
Let 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {C}\setminus \{0,1\}\)
 and 
S
 be any finite subset of 
\(\mathbb {Q}[\lambda ]\)
. Then 
\(S \mathrel {\hookrightarrow }Q_\lambda \)
.

                
                  
Proof

                  
Let 
\(f_1,\ldots ,f_n\in \mathbb {Q}[x]\)
 be such that 
\(S = \{f_1(\lambda ),\ldots ,f_n(\lambda )\}\)
. Set 
\(T:= \{f_1,\ldots ,f_n\}\)
. Then let 
\(a,b\in \mathbb {Z}[x]\)
 be as in Lemma 
6.19
. By that lemma, the only roots of 
\(a-b\)
 are 0 and 1, neither of which equal 
\(\lambda \)
. Thus 
\(a(\lambda ) \ne b(\lambda )\)
.

                  
Finally, we have 
\(\rho _{a,b}(T)\subseteq Q_{[x]}\)
 by Lemma 
6.19
. Evaluating both sides at 
\(\lambda \)
, we then get 
\(\rho _{c,d}(S) \subseteq Q_\lambda \)
 by Fact 
3.3
. Thus 
\(S \mathrel {\hookrightarrow }Q_\lambda \)
. 
\(\square \)

                
                  
Theorem 6.21

                  
For any nontrivial sPV 
\(\lambda \)
 such that 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 contains a unit of 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
 other than 1, and for any 
S
 defined as in Theorem 
6.13
, 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(S) \mathrel {\hookrightarrow }Q_\lambda \)
.

                
                  
Proof

                  
By Theorem 
6.13
, there exists a finite set 
\(Y \subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P) \subseteq \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
 such that 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(S) \subseteq Q_\lambda (Y)\)
. By Lemma 
6.20
, 
\(Y \mathrel {\hookrightarrow }Q_\lambda \)
. By Fact 
6.18
(3), 
\(Q_\lambda (Y) \mathrel {\hookrightarrow }Q_\lambda (Q_\lambda ) = Q_\lambda \)
. Hence 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(S) \mathrel {\hookrightarrow }Q_\lambda \)
. 
\(\square \)

                
The next two corollaries also hold for trivial sPV 
\(\lambda \)
 (see [
17
, Proposition 15.43]). By Fact 
6.18
,

                  
Corollary 6.22

                  
For any nontrivial sPV 
\(\lambda \)
 such that 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 contains a unit of 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
 other than 1, 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is relatively dense (in either 
\(\mathbb {R}\)
 or 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
, as 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {R}\)
 or 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {C}\setminus \mathbb {R}\)
, respectively).

                
Because 
\(Q_\lambda \subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
, and 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 is a Meyer set, we have

                  
Corollary 6.23

                  
For any nontrivial sPV 
\(\lambda \)
 such that 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 contains a unit of 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
 other than 1, 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is a Meyer set.

                
                  
Remark

                  
Alternatively, we note that if 
\(A \mathrel {\hookrightarrow }B \mathrel {\hookrightarrow }C\)
 and 
A
, 
C
 are Meyer sets, then 
B
 is also Meyer. In this case we have 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(S) \mathrel {\hookrightarrow }Q_\lambda \mathrel {\hookrightarrow }\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
, and hence 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is a Meyer set. 
\(\square \)

                
Part II: Applications
7 

                        
\(\lambda \)
-Convex Hulls of Some Regular Shapes
In this section, we consider the 
\(\lambda \)
-convex hulls of some point sets, specifically, regular polygons. We apply the Discreteness Theorem (Theorem 
4.12
) to find combinations of finite sets 
S
 and values 
\(\lambda \)
 that make 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 uniformly discrete. Throughout this section, 
\(\lambda \)
 will be a real number. A challenge for future research is to find familiar point sets in 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
 (besides 
\(\{0,1\}\)
) whose 
\(\lambda \)
-convex hulls are discrete for nonreal 
\(\lambda \)
.
To find discrete, nontrivially generated 
\(\lambda \)
-convex sets, we of course must have 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 discrete. We thus confine ourselves to considering sPV 
\(\lambda \)
.
7.1 
Regular Polygons
Here we will prove facts about discrete sets of the form 
\(Q_\lambda (P_n)\)
, where 
\(P_n\)
 is the set of vertices of a regular 
n
-gon for 
\(n \ge 3\)
, and 
\(\lambda \)
 is chosen appropriately, depending on 
n
. We will also show plots of some of these sets.
We first review the facts about the cyclotomic field extensions of 
\(\mathbb {Q}\)
 that we will need. Fix an integer 
\(n > 1\)
, and let
$$\begin{aligned} \zeta _n := e^{2\pi i/n} \end{aligned}$$

                    (21)
                
be the principal 
\({{n}^{\textit{th}}}\)
 root of unity. When 
n
 is understood, we write 
\(\zeta \)
. Thus 
\(\zeta \)
 is an algebraic integer of degree 
\(\phi (n)\)
 (where 
\(\phi \)
 is Euler’s totient function) whose minimum polynomial is the 
\({{n}^{\textit{th}}}\)
 cyclotomic polynomial 
\(\Phi _n\)
 and whose conjugates are the primitive 
\({{n}^{\textit{th}}}\)
 roots of 1. The field extension 
\(\mathbb {Q}(\zeta )\)
 of 
\(\mathbb {Q}\)
 is a Galois extension of degree 
\([\mathbb {Q}(\zeta ):\mathbb {Q}] = \phi (n)\)
 whose Galois group—isomorphic to 
\((\mathbb {Z}/n\mathbb {Z})^\times \)
—consists of those automorphisms 
\(\eta _a\)
 that map 
\(\zeta \)
 to 
\(\zeta ^a\)
 (and thus map 
\(\zeta ^j\)
 to 
\(\zeta ^{ja}\)
 for any 
\(j\in \mathbb {Z}\)
) for all 
\(0<a<n\)
 such that 
\(\gcd (a,n)=1\)
. Thus the 
\(\eta _a\)
 transitively permute the primitive 
\({{n}^{\textit{th}}}\)
 roots of 1.
We take the vertices of 
\(P_n\)
 to be the set 
\(\{0, 1, 1+\zeta , 1+\zeta +\zeta ^2,\dots ,1+\zeta +\dots \zeta ^{n-1}\} \subseteq \mathbb {C}\)
.
We will use the following lemma repeatedly to lift discreteness results on the real line up to the complex plane.

                  
Lemma 7.1

                  
Let 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {R}\)
 and 
\(S\subseteq \mathbb {C}\)
. Suppose that: (1) 
\(Q_\lambda (Re (S))\)
 is uniformly discrete; and (2) there exist 
\(a,b\in \mathbb {C}\)
 such that 
\(b-a\notin \mathbb {R}\)
 and 
\(S = \rho _{a,b}(S)\)
. Then 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 is uniformly discrete.

                
                  
Proof

                  
Suppose otherwise. Let 
\(d:= \max (|b-a|,1)\)
. For any 
\(\varepsilon > 0\)
, we show that there exist distinct 
\(u,v\in Q_\lambda (Re (S))\)
 such that 
\(|u-v| < \varepsilon \)
, contradicting the assumption that 
\(Q_\lambda (Re (S))\)
 is uniformly discrete. By assumption, there exist distinct 
\(x,y\in Q_\lambda (S)\)
 such that 
\(|x-y|<\varepsilon /d\)
. We have
$$\begin{aligned} \{Re (x),Re (y)\} \subseteq Re (Q_\lambda (S)) = Q_\lambda (Re (S))\;, \end{aligned}$$
the last equality by Lemma 
2.15
.
Footnote 
17
 If 
\(Re (x) \ne Re (y)\)
, then letting 
\(u := Re (x)\)
 and 
\(v := Re (y)\)
 gives the contradiction: 
\(0<|u-v| \le |x-y|<\varepsilon /d\le \varepsilon \)
. If 
\(Re (x) = Re (y)\)
, then 
\(x-y = ir\)
 for some nonzero 
\(r\in \mathbb {R}\)
 with 
\(|r| < \varepsilon /d\)
. Letting 
\(u := Re (\rho _{a,b}(x))\)
 and 
\(v := Re (\rho _{a,b}(y))\)
, we have
$$\begin{aligned} u-v = Re (\rho _{a,b}(x)-\rho _{a,b}(y)) \end{aligned}$$
$$\begin{aligned} = Re ((x-y)(b-a)) = Re (ir(b-a)) = -rIm (b-a) \ne 0\;, \end{aligned}$$
and so 
\(0< |u-v| = |r|\cdot |Im (b-a)| \le |r|\cdot |b-a| < (\varepsilon /d)|b-a| \le \varepsilon \)
, and
$$\begin{aligned} \{u,v\} \subseteq Re (\rho _{a,b}(Q_\lambda (S))) = Re (Q_\lambda (\rho _{a,b}(S))) = Re (Q_\lambda (S)) = Q_\lambda (Re (S))\;. \end{aligned}$$
Contradiction. The first equality is by Corollary 
2.12
; the last is by Lemma 
2.15
. 
\(\square \)

                
The primary question we ask in this section is: For which 
n
 and which 
\(\lambda \)
 is 
\(Q_\lambda (P_n)\)
 discrete, and if so, what does this set look like? We also wish to show that, if 
\(Q_\lambda (P_n)\)
 is discrete, then it is a Meyer set. For this it suffices to show uniform discreteness of 
\(Q_\lambda (P_n) - Q_\lambda (P_n)\)
 and relative density of 
\(Q_\lambda (P_n)\)
 in 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
 (see Fact 
4.9
). We show the former
Footnote 
18
 in this section and the latter in Section 
10
, specifically Lemma 
10.1
.
We will use the next lemma in conjunction with Lemma 
7.1
:

                  
Lemma 7.2

                  
For any 
\(a,b\in \mathbb {C}\)
 and 
\(S\subseteq \mathbb {C}\)
, if 
\(S = \rho _{a,b}(S)\)
, then 
\(S-S = \rho _{0,b-a}(S-S)\)
.

                
                  
Proof

                  
For all 
\(x,y\in \mathbb {C}\)
, we have 
\(\rho _{0,b-a}(x-y) = (x-y)(b-a) = \rho _{a,b}(x) - \rho _{a,b}(y)\)
. Therefore,
$$\begin{aligned} \rho _{0,b-a}(S-S) = \{\rho _{0,b-a}(x-y) \mid x,y\in S\} \end{aligned}$$
$$\begin{aligned} = \{\rho _{a,b}(x) - \rho _{a,b}(y) \mid x,y\in S\} = \rho _{a,b}(S) - \rho _{a,b}(S) = S-S\;. \end{aligned}$$
\(\square \)

                
It is most natural to define 
\(P_n\subseteq \mathbb {C}\)
 formally as the set of all 
\({{n}^{\textit{th}}}\)
 roots of 1, which orients the polygon on the unit circle. Purely for reasons of technical convenience, however (including some relating to the computer code generating the plots shown below), we instead orient 
\(P_n\)
 so that one of its sides coincides with the unit interval and the rest of the polygon lies in the upper halfplane:
$$\begin{aligned} P_n := \left\{ \sum _{j=0}^{\ell -1} \zeta ^j : 0\le \ell< n \right\} = \left\{ \frac{\zeta ^\ell -1}{\zeta -1} : 0\le \ell < n \right\} \;, \end{aligned}$$

                    (22)
                
for 
\(\zeta \)
 as in (
21
). This definition also has the advantage that 
\(\{0,1\}\subseteq P_n\)
.
We first prove a straightforward result for the equilateral triangle, square, and regular hexagon. Other shapes are considered in the next two sections.

                  
Fact 7.3

                  
For any 
n
, 
\(\rho _{1,1+\zeta _n}(P_n) = P_n\)
.

                
                  
Proposition 7.4

                  
If 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {R}\)
 is sPV, then 
\(Q_\lambda (S)-Q_\lambda (S)\)
 is uniformly discrete for all 
\(S\in \{P_3,P_4,P_6\}\)
.

                
                  
Proof

                  
Let 
\(n \in \{3,4,6\}\)
. Then by Fact 
7.3
, we have 
\(\rho _{1,1+\zeta _n}(S=P_n) = P_n\)
. It follows by Lemma 
7.2
 that 
\(\rho _{0,\zeta _n}(P_n-P_n) = P_n - P_n\)
. In all cases, 
\(Re (S-S) = Re (S)-Re (S) \subseteq \left\{ -2,-\frac{3}{2},-1,-\frac{1}{2},0,\frac{1}{2},1,\frac{3}{2},2\right\} \subseteq \mathbb {Q}\subseteq \mathbb {Q}(\lambda )\)
, and so by Theorem 
4.12
, 
\(Q_\lambda (Re (S-S))\)
 is uniformly discrete. It follows that 
\(Q_\lambda (S-S)\)
 is uniformly discrete by Lemma 
7.1
, and in addition, 
\(Q_\lambda (S-S) = Q_\lambda (S)-Q_\lambda (S)\)
 by Lemma 
2.9
. 
\(\square \)

                
7.2 
Constructions with Odd 
n

                        
We obtain most of the other values of 
\(\lambda \)
 that we consider here, corresponding to the various 
\(P_n\)
, by perhaps a somewhat arbitrary type of geometric construction: choose two nonparallel lines 
\(L_1\)
 and 
\(L_2\)
 passing through pairs of points of 
\(P_n\)
, then let 
\(\lambda \)
 be a certain ratio of distances on 
\(L_1\)
 between the two points on the polygon and the point of intersection of 
\(L_1\)
 and 
\(L_2\)
. For the moment we will choose pairs of adjacent points on the polygon, and so we can assume without loss of generality that 
\(L_1\)
 is the real axis.
Let 
\(n\ge 3\)
 be odd, and consider the construction below using 
\(P_n\)
. There we take 
\({L}_{1}\)
 to be the line through the base of 
\(P_n\)
 (in this case, the real axis), and 
\(L_2\)
 the line through the apex 
A
 and the point adjacent to it, 
B
. The two lines intersect at the point
$$\begin{aligned} \lambda _n := \frac{1}{2(1-\cos (\pi /n))} = \frac{1}{2+\zeta ^k+\zeta ^{-k}}\;, \end{aligned}$$

                    (23)
                
where 
\(k := (n-1)/2\)
 and 
\(\zeta \)
 is as in Eq. (
21
). Note that 
\(\gcd (n,k) = 1\)
. (One way to see that this formula for 
\(\lambda _n\)
 is correct is to drop a vertical line segment from 
A
 down to the base at the point 1/2, forming a right triangle 
T
 with the point 
\(\lambda _n\)
. The hypotenuse of 
T
 has length 
\(\lambda _n\)
 by symmetry, and the base of 
T
 has length 
\(\lambda _n - 1/2\)
, and finally, the acute angle at 
\(\lambda _n\)
 is 
\(\pi /n\)
, giving the first equality. To see the second equality, note that 
\((\zeta ^k + \zeta ^{-k})/2 = \cos (2k\pi /n) = \cos (\pi - \pi /n) = -\cos (\pi /n)\)
.) Of course, the first equality of (
23
) makes sense for all 
\(n\in {\mathbb {Z}^+}\)
, not necessarily odd, but throughout this section we assume that 
n
 is odd.
Now consider the set 
\(Q_{\lambda _n}(P_n)\)
. Since 
\(0,1\in P_n\)
, this set includes 
\(Q_{\lambda _n}\)
 as a subset, and so it can be discrete only if 
\(Q_{\lambda _n}\)
 is discrete. The next proposition comes close to a converse.

                  
Proposition 7.5

                  
For any odd 
\(n\ge 3\)
, let 
\(\lambda _n\)
 be given by Eq. (
23
). Suppose 
\(\lambda _n\)
 is sPV. Then 
\(Q_{\lambda _n}(P_n)-Q_{\lambda _n}(P_n)\)
 is uniformly discrete; in fact, 
\(Q_{\lambda _n}(P_{2n})-Q_{\lambda _n}(P_{2n})\)
 is uniformly discrete.

                
                  
Remark

                  
Note that if 
\(Q_{\lambda _n}(P_{2n})-Q_{\lambda _n}(P_{2n})\)
 is uniformly discrete, then so is 
\(Q_{\lambda _n}(P_n)-Q_{\lambda _n}(P_n)\)
: since 
\(P_n\)
 can be embedded into 
\(P_{2n}\)
 by a 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
-affine transformation, the same holds for 
\(P_n-P_n\)
 into 
\(P_{2n}-P_{2n}\)
, and thus for 
\(Q_{\lambda _n}(P_n)-Q_{\lambda _n}(P_n)\)
 into 
\(Q_{\lambda _n}(P_{2n})-Q_{\lambda _n}(P_{2n})\)
 by Lemma 
2.9
. 
\(\square \)

                
                  
Proof of Proposition 7.5

                  
Assume that 
\(n\ge 3\)
 is odd and that 
\(\lambda _n\)
 is sPV. By the previous remark, it suffices to show that 
\(Q_{\lambda _n}(P_{2n})-Q_{\lambda _n}(P_{2n})\)
 is uniformly discrete. By symmetry, we have 
\(\rho _{1,1+\eta }(P_{2n}) = P_{2n}\)
, where 
\(\eta := e^{2\pi i/(2n)}\)
 is the principal (2
n
)-th root of 1. Then by Lemmas 
2.9
, 
7.1
, and 
7.2
, it suffices to show that 
\(Q_{\lambda _n}(Re (P_{2n}-P_{2n}))\)
 is uniformly discrete.

                  
By Eq. (
22
),
$$\begin{aligned} Re (P_{2n}) = \left\{ \sum _{j=0}^{\ell -1} \cos (j\pi /n) : 0\le \ell < n \right\} \;. \end{aligned}$$
All the points in this finite set are elements of 
\(\mathbb {Q}(\lambda _n)\)
. This is because 
\(\cos (\pi /n) = 1 - 1/(2\lambda _n)\)
 is clearly in 
\(\mathbb {Q}(\lambda _n)\)
, and all the terms in the sum above are of the form 
\(\cos (j\pi /n)\)
 for integer 
j
, and these can in turn be expressed as (Chebychev) polynomials 
\(T_j(\cos (\pi /n))\)
 of 
\(\cos (\pi /n)\)
, where 
\(T_j(x)\in \mathbb {Z}[x]\)
. It follows that 
\(Re (P_{2n}-P_{2n}) = Re (P_{2n})-Re (P_{2n}) \subseteq \mathbb {Q}(\lambda _n)\)
 and is finite. Theorem 
4.12
 then says that 
\(Q_{\lambda _n}(Re (P_{2n}-P_{2n}))\)
 is uniformly discrete. 
\(\square \)

                
The “good” news regarding Proposition 
7.5
 is that there exist sVP 
\(\lambda _n\)
, which yield interesting sets 
\(Q_{\lambda _n}(P_n)\)
 and 
\(Q_{\lambda _n}(P_{2n})\)
. The “bad” news is that there are only finitely many such 
n
.

                  
Proposition 7.6

                  
Let 
\(n\ge 3\)
 be odd. Then 
\(\lambda _n\)
 is sPV if and only if 
\(n \in \{3,5,7,9,15\}\)
.

                
We prove Proposition 
7.6
 via the following three lemmas. Define 
\( \mathbb {Z}_n^*:= \{j\in \mathbb {Z}\mid 0<j<n \;\mathrel { \& }\;\gcd (j,n)=1\}\)
 as is customary.
To prove these lemmas, we rely on known properties of the minimal polynomial 
\(\Psi _n\)
 for the algebraic integer 
\(2\cos (\frac{2\pi }{n})\)
. For 
\(n \ge 3\)
, we know that 
\(\Psi _n\)
 has degree 
\(\phi (n)/2\)
, constant term 
\(\pm 1\)
, and also has roots 
\(2\cos (\frac{2j\pi }{n})\)
 for any 
j
 with 
\(\gcd (j,n) = 1\)
.

                  
Lemma 7.7

                  
Let 
\(n \ge 3\)
 be odd. Then 
\(\lambda _n\)
 (see Eq. (
23
)) is an algebraic integer of degree 
\(\phi (n)/2\)
, and its Galois conjugates are of the form
$$\begin{aligned} \frac{1}{2\left( 1+\cos (2a\pi /n)\right) } \end{aligned}$$
for all integers 
\(0< a < n/2\)
 such that 
\(\gcd (a,n)=1\)
.

                
                  
Proof

                  
From Eq. (
23
), letting 
\(k:= (n-1)/2\)
, we see that 
\(1/\lambda _n = 2+\zeta ^k+\zeta ^{-k}\)
 is an algebraic integer, since 
\(\zeta ^{-k} = \zeta ^{n-k}\)
 and the algebraic integers form a ring. Since 
n
 is odd, either 
k
 or 
\(k + n\)
 is even. Thus there is an 
m
 with 
\(\gcd (m,n)=1\)
 such that 
\(k = 2m\)
 or 
\(k + n = 2m\)
. For notational convenience, let 
\(\alpha _m:= \lambda _n^{-1}\)
. Then 
\(\alpha = 2 + \zeta ^{2m} + \zeta ^{-2m} = (\zeta ^m + \zeta ^{-m})^2 = (2\cos (\frac{2m\pi }{n}))^2\)
. Let 
\(\beta _m = 2\cos (\frac{2m\pi }{n})\)
, so 
\(\alpha _m = \beta _m^2\)
.

                  
As per the remarks preceding this lemma, 
\(\beta _{j}\)
 has minimal polynomial 
\(\Psi _n\)
, for any 
j
 with 
\(\gcd (n,j) = 1\)
. Since 
\(\gcd (2m,n) = 1\)
, 
\(\deg (\beta _{2m}) = \phi (n)/2\)
. Note that 
\(\alpha _m = 2 + 2\cos (\frac{4m\pi }{n}) = 2 + \beta _{2m}\)
. From this we infer that 
\(\deg (\alpha _m) = \deg (\beta _{2m})\)
, hence 
\(\deg (\alpha _m) = \phi (n)/2\)
. Now the norm 
\(N(\beta _m) = \pm 1\)
 because the constant term of 
\(\Psi _n\)
 is 
\(\pm 1\)
. Hence 
\(N(\alpha _m) = N(\beta _m^2) = N(\beta _m)^2 = 1\)
. It follows that the constant term of the minimal polynomial of 
\(\alpha _m\)
 is 1. Hence the coefficient of the leading term of the minimal polynomial for 
\(\lambda _n = \alpha _m^{-1}\)
 is 1, so 
\(\lambda _n\)
 is an algebraic integer of degree 
\(\phi (n)/2\)
.

                  
The conjugates of 
\(\alpha _m\)
 are 
\(\eta _a(\alpha _m) = (2\cos (\frac{2ma\pi }{n}))^2 = (2\cos (\frac{a\pi }{n}))^2\)
 for 
\(0< a < n/2\)
. Since 
\(\cos \)
 is monotonically decreasing in this interval, these are pairwise distinct, and hence there are exactly 
\(\phi (n)/2\)
 conjugates of 
\(\lambda _n^{-1}\)
. Since the constant term of the minimal polynomial is 1, it follows that the values 
\((2\cos (\frac{a\pi }{n}))^{-2} = (2 + 2\cos (\frac{2a\pi }{n}))^{-1}\)
 are the conjugates of 
\(\lambda _n\)
. 
\(\square \)

                
                  
Lemma 7.8

                  
Let 
\(n\ge 3\)
 be odd. Then 
\(\lambda _n\)
 is sPV if and only if there are no 
\(j\in \mathbb {Z}_n^*\)
 such that 
\(n/3 \le j \le 2n/3\)
, except for 
\((n\pm 1)/2\)
.

                
                  
Proof

                  
Set 
\(k:= (n-1)/2\)
. By the previous lemma, 
\(\lambda _n\)
 is an algebraic integer with conjugates 
\((2+2\cos (2j\pi /n))^{-1} = (2+\zeta ^j+\zeta ^{-j})^{-1}\)
 for 
\(j\in \mathbb {Z}_n^*\)
 with 
\(j<n/2\)
. We can drop the requirement that 
\(j<n/2\)
, because 
\(\cos (2j\pi /n) = \cos (2(n-j)\pi /n)\)
 for all 
j
. Since 
\(\lambda _n = (2+\zeta ^k+\zeta ^{-k})^{-1} = (2+\zeta ^{k+1}+\zeta ^{-(k+1)})^{-1}\)
, the conjugates of 
\(\lambda _n\)
 other than 
\(\lambda _n\)
 itself are of the form 
\((2+\zeta ^j+\zeta ^{-j})^{-1} = (2+2\cos (2j\pi /n))^{-1}\)
 for all 
\(j\in \mathbb {Z}_n^* \setminus \{k,k+1\}\)
. It follows by definition that 
\(\lambda _n\)
 is sPV if and only if 
\(0< (2+2\cos (2j\pi /n))^{-1} < 1\)
, or equivalently, 
\(\cos (2j\pi /n) > -1/2\)
 for all such 
j
. This latter inequality is equivalent to 
\(j < n/3\)
 or 
\(j > 2n/3\)
, for all 
\(j\in \mathbb {Z}_n^*\)
 other than 
k
 or 
\(k+1\)
. 
\(\square \)

                
                  
Lemma 7.9

                  
If 
\(n>21\)
 and 
n
 is odd, then there exists a 
\(j\in \mathbb {Z}_n^*\)
 such that 
\(n/3< j < 2n/3\)
 and 
\(j\notin \{(n-1)/2,(n+1)/2\}\)
.

                
                  
Proof

                  
We give an elementary proof using only the Bertrand-Chebyshev theorem, which states that for all integers 
\(m>1\)
, there exists a prime 
p
 with 
\(m<p<2m\)
. It follows that this must also be true for all 
real
 
\(m>1\)
, by applying the theorem to 
\({\lfloor {m}\rfloor }\)
 if 
\(m\ge 2\)
. Set 
\(k:= (n-1)/2\)
 as usual. 
Case 1::

                        
\(n = qp\)
 where 
p
 is an odd prime and 
\(q\in \{5,7,9,11\}\)
. Choose two numbers 
\(j_1 < j_2\)
 depending on 
p
 and 
q
 according to the following table: 
 Since 
n
 is sufficiently large, one can readily check that: (i) 
\(n/3< j_1< j_2 < k\)
; (ii) neither 
\(j_1\)
 nor 
\(j_2\)
 is a multiple of 
p
; and (iii) at least one of 
\(j_1\)
 and 
\(j_2\)
 is coprime with 
q
 and hence coprime with 
n
, satisfying the lemma.

                      
Case 2::

                        
not Case 1 and neither 
k
 nor 
\(k+1\)
 is prime. By Bertrand-Chebyshev, there exists a prime 
j
 such that 
\(n/3< j < 2n/3\)
. Then 
\(j\not \mid n\)
 and 
j
 is neither 
k
 nor 
\(k+1\)
, so 
j
 satisfies the lemma.

                      
Case 3::

                        
not Case 1, one of 
k
 and 
\(k+1\)
 is prime, and the other is not of the form 2
p
 for any prime 
p
. By Bertrand-Chebyshev, there exists a prime 
r
 such that 
\(n/6< r < n/3\)
. We must have 
\(r\not \mid n\)
, because we are not in Case 1 (and 
n
 is odd). Then setting 
\(j:= 2r\)
 satisfies the lemma.

                      
Case 4::

                        
not Case 1, one of 
k
 and 
\(k+1\)
 is prime, and the other is of the form 2
p
 for some prime 
p
. By Bertrand-Chebyshev, choose a prime 
s
 such that 
\(n/12< s < n/6\)
. Again, 
\(s\not \mid n\)
 since we are not in Case 1 (and 
n
 is odd). Then setting 
\(j:= 4s\)
 satisfies the lemma.

                      
 Cases 1–4 are clearly exhaustive. 
\(\square \)

                
                  
Proof of Proposition 7.6

                  
Lemmas 
7.8
 and 
7.9
 imply that 
\(\lambda _n\)
 cannot be sPV if 
\(n>21\)
. For 
\(3\le n\le 21\)
, one can check case by case that Lemma 
7.8
 is satisfied if and only if 
\(n\in \{3,5,7,9,15\}\)
. 
\(\square \)

                
The following table gives basic information about 
\(\lambda _n\)
 for the values of 
n
 we are interested in:
$$ \begin{array}{r|l|r} n &  \text{ minimal } \text{ polynomial } \text{ of } {\lambda _n} &  \text{ approximate } \text{ value } \text{ of } {\lambda _n} \\ \hline 3 &  x-1 &  1.000 \\ 4 &  2x^2-4x+1 &  1.707 \\ 5 &  x^2-3x+1 &  2.618 \\ 6 &  x^2-4x+1 &  3.732 \\ 7 &  x^3-6x^2+5x-1 &  5.049 \\ 9 &  x^3-9x^2+6x-1 &  8.291 \\ 15 &  x^4-24x^3+26x^2-9x+1 &  22.881 \end{array} $$
All are sPV except 
\(\lambda _4\)
, which is not an algebraic integer. However, 
\(2\lambda _4 = 2+\sqrt{2}\)
 is sPV.
Figures 
5
–
7
 in Appendix B show the plots (with one exception) of the uniformly discrete sets 
\(Q_{\lambda _n}(P_n)\)
 and 
\(Q_{\lambda _n}(P_{2n})\)
 for 
\(n=5,7,9,15\)
 as in Proposition 
7.6
 (the 
\(n=3\)
 case is trivial, as 
\(\lambda _3 = 1\)
). We order them by increasing 
n
. Voronoi diagrams of some of these plots are in Appendix C. Each displayed set has dihedral 
\(D_n\)
 or 
\(D_{2n}\)
 symmetry, but none has any translational symmetry. Each displayed set is a Meyer set by Corollary 
10.4
. Larger, uncropped versions of these and other plots can be found in [
17
]. The Voronoi diagrams are not the focus of the current paper, but some closely related sets and their Voronoi diagrams are studied extensively in [
20
, 
21
, 
31
,
32
,
33
, 
38
]. Unlike some of the cut-and-project sets studied there, all sets in the current paper are finitely generated.
7.3 
Constructions with Even 
n

                        
One can construct values similar to 
\(\lambda _n\)
 similarly for even 
n
. We call these points 
\(\beta _n\)
. Here we connect the uppermost right vertex of 
\(P_n\)
 (a unit distance to the right of the vertical axis) to the adjacent one down and to the right, and extend it to the real axis. The resulting value is 
\(\beta _n = 1/(1-\cos (2\pi /n))\)
. It would be interesting to know for which of these we have discrete sets. However, 
\(\beta _n\)
 may or may not be an algebraic integer. For example, 
\(\beta _4 = 2 + \sqrt{2}\)
 is an algebraic integer, 
\(\beta _{12} = 1+\sqrt{3}/2\)
 is not, whereas 
\(2\beta _{12} = 2+\sqrt{3}\)
 is. Note that 
\(2+\sqrt{2}\)
 and 
\(2+\sqrt{3}\)
 are two of the strong PV numbers given after Corollary 
5.1
.

                  
Proposition 7.10

                  
The sets 
\(Q_{2+\sqrt{2}}(P_8)-Q_{2+\sqrt{2}}(P_8)\)
 and 
\(Q_{2+\sqrt{3}}(P_{12})-Q_{2+\sqrt{3}}(P_{12})\)
 are both uniformly discrete.

                
                  
Proof

                  
It is clear that, as in Fact 
7.3
, 
\(\rho _{1,1+\zeta _n}(P_n) = P_n\)
 for any even 
n
. Using the values for 
\(\zeta _n\)
 for 
\(n=8\)
 and 12, we thus have 
\(Re (P_8) = \left\{ -\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2},0,1,1+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right\} \subseteq \mathbb {Q}\left( 2+\sqrt{2}\right) \)
, and thus 
\(Re (P_8-P_8)\subseteq \mathbb {Q}\left( 2+\sqrt{2}\right) \)
 as well. Similarly, 
\(Re (P_{12}) = \left\{ -\frac{1+\sqrt{3}}{2},-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2},0,1,1+\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2},1+\frac{1+\sqrt{3}}{2}\right\} \subseteq \mathbb {Q}\left( 2+\sqrt{3}\right) \)
, and thus 
\(Re (P_{12}-P_{12})\subseteq \mathbb {Q}\left( 2+\sqrt{3}\right) \)
 also.

                  
As in the proofs of Propositions 
7.4
 and 
7.5
, the statement now follows from Theorem 
4.12
 and Lemmas 
2.9
, 
7.1
, and 
7.2
. 
\(\square \)

                
Figure 
8
 in Appendix B shows three point sets that do not fit the “odd 
n
” pattern—the 2-convex hull of 
\(P_6\)
 (upper left), the 
\((2+\sqrt{2})\)
-convex hull of 
\(P_8\)
 (upper right), and the 
\((2+\sqrt{3})\)
-convex hull of 
\(P_{12}\)
. Note that 
\(2+\sqrt{2} = \beta _4 = 2\lambda _4\)
 and 
\(2+\sqrt{3} = 2\beta _{12} = \lambda _6\)
. These sets are all Meyer sets by Corollary 
10.4
.
8 
Applications of the Covering Method
In this section, we apply Theorem 
6.12
 to three values of 
\(\lambda \)
, to show that the corresponding 
\(Q_\lambda \)
’s 
equal
 model sets, and are hence Meyer sets. We first apply it to the two non-real values of 
\(\lambda \)
 depicted in Figure 
2
, showing that these two sets are both model sets and hence relatively dense Meyer sets. Secondly, we apply the theorem to the one real quadratic case left unresolved by Theorem 
6.9
, showing that 
\(Q_\lambda = \mathord {\curlywedge }^{(\lambda )}({\left[ {0,1}\right] })\)
 for 
\(\lambda := (-3-\sqrt{17})/2\)
. This last case also makes use of Proposition 
8.1
, below. Finally, we apply Theorem 
6.13
 to one 
real
 
\(\lambda \)
 of degree 3. This is more complicated than the non-real complex 
\(\lambda \)
’s, since there are two conjugates in 
\({\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
.
8.1 
Uniformly Distributed Distributions
If 
\(\lambda \)
 is real quadratic, we can get sufficient bounds on the size of 
X
 in Theorem 
6.12
 based on results in the theory of uniformly distributed distributions (see, for example, Allouche & Shallit [
1
]). The following concepts and most of the following facts are from [
1
, Chapter 2]. Let 
\([a_0,a_1,a_2,\ldots ]\)
 be the continued fraction expansion of 
\(\mu := \lambda '\)
 (noting that 
\(a_0=0\)
).
Footnote 
19
 For 
\(i\ge 0\)
, define 
\(A_i:= {\left[ \begin{matrix}a_i& 1\\ 1& 0\end{matrix}\right] }\)
, and for 
\(k\ge -1\)
 define
$$\begin{aligned} {\left[ \begin{matrix}p_k\\ q_k\end{matrix}\right] } := A_0A_1\cdots A_k{\left[ \begin{matrix}1\\ 0\end{matrix}\right] }\;. \end{aligned}$$

                    (24)
                
The 
\(q_k\)
 satisfy the recurrence 
\(q_{k+1} = a_{k+1}q_k + q_{k-1}\)
 and form a strictly increasing sequence that is bounded below by the Fibonacci sequence; also, 
\(\lim _{k\rightarrow \infty } p_k/q_k = \mu \)
. Letting
$$\begin{aligned} \eta _k := (-1)^k(q_k\mu - p_k)\;, \end{aligned}$$

                    (25)
                
one can show that 
\(\eta _k > 0\)
 for all 
\(k\ge -1\)
 and that 
\(\eta _{k+1} < \eta _k\)
 for all 
\(k\ge 1\)
. In fact, 
\(1/q_{k+2}< \eta _k < 1/q_{k+1}\)
 for all 
\(k\ge 1\)
.
Then, by Corollary 
5.1
, we have
$$\begin{aligned} \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)' = \{1\}\cup \{{\lceil {b\mu }\rceil }-b\mu \mid b\in \mathbb {Z}\} = \{1\}\cup \{\{a\mu \} \mid a\in \mathbb {Z}\}\;, \end{aligned}$$
where 
\(\{a\mu \}:= a\mu - {\lfloor {a\mu }\rfloor }\)
 is the fractional part of 
\(a\mu \)
. (To see the second equality, set 
\(a:= -b\)
.) For integer 
\(n\ge 0\)
, let
$$ \begin{aligned} X_n := \{1\} \cup \{ {\lceil {b\mu }\rceil }-b\lambda \mid b\in \mathbb {Z}\;\mathrel { \& }\;-n\le b\le 0\} \subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\;. \end{aligned}$$
Then 
\( X_n' = \{1\}\cup \{\{a\mu \} \mid a\in \mathbb {Z}\;\mathrel { \& }\;0\le a\le n\}\)
. Much is known about sets of this form for 
\(n\ge 0\)
. By the three-distance theorem (see [
1
, Section 2.6]), there are at most three possible distances between adjacent points of 
\(X_n'\)
—the smallest distance is 
\(\eta _k\)
, where 
\(k\ge -1\)
 is largest such that 
\(q_k \le n\)
 [
1
, Theorem 2.6.2]; the largest distance 
\(d_n\)
 satisfies the bound
$$\begin{aligned} d_n \le \eta _k + \eta _{k-1}\;, \end{aligned}$$

                    (26)
                
where 
\(k\ge 0\)
 is largest such that 
\(q_k \le n+1\)
 [
1
, Theorem 2.6.3].
We now consider choosing 
X
 to be 
\(X_n\)
 for 
n
 sufficiently large so that Eq. (
18
) is satisfied. The intervals on the right cover 
\({\left[ {0,1}\right] }\)
 just when there are no gaps between adjacent intervals. That is, for any 
\(x,y\in X_n\)
 such that 
\(x' < y'\)
 are adjacent in 
\(X_n'\)
, it suffices that 
\((1-\beta )x' + \beta \ge (1-\beta )y'\)
, or equivalently, 
\(y' - x' \le \beta /(1-\beta )\)
. Thus it suffices to choose 
n
 so that 
\(d_n \le \beta /(1-\beta )\)
, and by Eq. (
26
) this will be true if
$$\begin{aligned} \eta _k+\eta _{k-1} \le \frac{\beta }{1-\beta }\;, \end{aligned}$$

                    (27)
                
where 
\(k\ge 0\)
 is largest such that 
\(q_k \le n+1\)
. Thus we first choose the least 
k
 satisfying (
27
), then we set 
\(n:= q_k - 1\)
.
Letting 
X
 be 
\(X_n\)
, although sufficient, is not an optimal choice, because it does not minimize 
\(\max _{x\in X}|x|\)
 and thus the value of 
M
 in Eq. (
19
). To minimize 
M
, notice that any set of the form 
\( W_{m,n}:= \{1\} \cup \{ {\lceil {b\mu }\rceil }-b\lambda \mid b\in \mathbb {Z}\;\mathrel { \& }\;m-n\le b\le m\}\)
 for 
\(m\in \mathbb {Z}\cap {\left[ {0,n}\right] }\)
 works just as well as 
\(X_n\)
 in covering 
\({\left[ {0,1}\right] }\)
. This is because 
\( W_{m,n}' = \{1\}\cup \{\{a\mu \} \mid a\in \mathbb {Z}\;\mathrel { \& }\;-m\le a\le n-m\}\)
 is the image of 
\(X_n'\)
 under the cyclic shift permutation 
\({{c}:{{\left[ {0,1}\right] }}\rightarrow {{\left[ {0,1}\right] }}}\)
 defined for 
\(x\in {\left[ {0,1}\right] }\)
 by
$$ c(x) = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} 1 &  \text{ if } x = 1, \\ (x - \{m\mu \}) \bmod 1 &  \text{ if } x < 1, \end{array}\right. $$
which preserves the set of distances between adjacent points in 
\(X_n'\)
 versus those in 
\(W_{m,n}'\)
. This means that, given our choice of 
n
 above, we can set 
\(X:= W_{m,n}\)
 where 
m
 minimizes 
\(\max _{x\in X}|x| = \max _{x\in W_{m,n}}|x|\)
. If 
\(\lambda > 0\)
, then one can verify that 
\(m = {\lceil {n/2}\rceil }\)
, and likewise if 
\(\lambda < 0\)
, then 
\(m = {\lfloor {n/2}\rfloor }\)
. In the former case,
$$\begin{aligned} \max _{x\in X} |x| = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \lambda n/2 - {\lfloor {\mu n/2}\rfloor } &  \text{ if } n \text{ is } \text{ even } \text{ and } n>0, \\ \lambda (n+1)/2 - {\lceil {\mu (n+1)/2}\rceil } &  \text{ if } n \text{ is } \text{ odd. } \end{array}\right. \end{aligned}$$

                    (28)
                
In the latter case,
$$\begin{aligned} \max _{x\in X} |x| = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} {\lceil {\mu n/2}\rceil } - \lambda n/2 &  \text{ if } n \text{ is } \text{ even } \text{ and } n>0, \\ {\lfloor {\mu (n+1)/2}\rfloor } - \lambda (n+1)/2 &  \text{ if } n \text{ is } \text{ odd. } \end{array}\right. \end{aligned}$$

                    (29)
                
(Verifying the above is made easier by noting that 
\(\lambda \notin {\left[ {-1,2}\right] }\)
.) In either case, if 
\(n=0\)
, then 
\(W_{m,n} = \{0,1\}\)
, so 
\(\max _{x\in X} |x| = 1\)
.
We summarize the foregoing in the following proposition.

                  
Proposition 8.1

                  
In Theorem 
6.12
 where 
\(\lambda \)
 is real quadratic, it suffices to let
$$ \begin{aligned} X := \{1\} \cup \{ {\lceil {b\mu }\rceil }-b\lambda \mid b\in \mathbb {Z}\;\mathrel { \& }\;m-n \le b \le m\}\;, \end{aligned}$$
where 
\(n = q_k-1\)
 for the least 
k
 satisfying (
27
) for 
\(\eta _k\)
 defined by (
25
) and 
\(q_k\)
 defined by (
24
) based on the continued fraction expansion of 
\(\mu = \lambda '\)
, and 
\(m = {\lceil {n/2}\rceil }\)
 if 
\(\lambda > 0\)
 and 
\(m = {\lfloor {n/2}\rfloor }\)
 otherwise. For this 
X
, the value 
\(\max _{x\in X}|x|\)
 is given by Eq. (
28
) or (
29
), provided 
\(n>0\)
.

                
8.2 
Case study: Two Non-real Values of 
\(\lambda \)

                        
We first consider the two non-real values 
\(\lambda \)
 and 
\(\nu \)
 given in Figure 
2
, each sPV by Corollary 
5.2
, yielding uniformly discrete 
\(Q_\lambda \)
. Voronoi diagrams of these two sets are shown in Figure 
13
 of Appendix C.
Fig. 2
Full size image
Two plots illustrating Corollary 
5.2
. The left plot is of 
\(Q_\lambda \)
, where 
\(\lambda \)
 is the root of the polynomial 
\(x^3 + x^2 - 1\)
 closest to 
\(-0.877 + 0.745i\)
. The right plot is of 
\(Q_\nu \)
, where 
\(\nu \)
 is the root of the polynomial 
\(x^3+x-1\)
 closest to 
\(-0.341+1.162i\)
. One can easily show that 
\(Q_\lambda = Q_{\lambda ^2} = Q_{\lambda ^3}\)
Letting 
\(\mu \)
 be the conjugate of 
\(\lambda \)
 in 
\({\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
, let 
\(r:= \lambda - \mu \)
, let 
\(s:= \lambda ^2 - \mu ^2 = r(\lambda +\mu )\)
, and consider the lattice 
\(r\mathbb {Z}+ s\mathbb {Z}\)
. This lattice is discrete, since 
r
 and 
s
 are 
\(\mathbb {R}\)
-linearly independent, and it is interesting to notice using Eq. (
9
) that each point in 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 differs from a point in this lattice by some real displacement between 0 and 1. Similarly for 
\(Q_\nu \)
.
In Propositions 
8.2
 and 
8.3
 we show that equality holds in Corollary 
5.2
 for the values of 
\(\lambda \)
 and 
\(\nu \)
 indicated in Figure 
2
. It would be nice to know in general whether or when equality holds.

                  
Proposition 8.2

                  
Let 
\(\lambda \)
 be the root of the polynomial 
\(x^3+x^2-1\)
 closest to the point 
\(-0.877+0.745i\)
 (see Figure 
2
). Then 
\(Q_\lambda = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
.

                
                  
Proof

                  
We have that 
\(\lambda \)
 is cubic sPV with conjugate 
\(\mu \approx 0.754878\)
. (The exact form of 
\(\mu \)
 is not important.) As in other cases, 
\(\lambda \)
 is itself a unit of 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
, so we set 
\(\alpha := \lambda \)
 whence 
\(\beta = \mu \)
. Since 
\(\mu > 1/2\)
, we may take 
\(X:= \{0,1\}\)
. Plugging into Eq. (
19
), we get 
\(M \approx 13.379361\)
. Letting 
Y
 be as in Eq. (
20
), we get 
\(Q_\lambda (Y) = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
. Thus it suffices to show that 
\(Y\subseteq Q_\lambda \)
.

                  
We can rewrite Eq. (
17
) as
$$\begin{aligned} \mathord {\curlywedge }(P) = \{1\} \cup \{ {\lceil {b\mu +c\mu ^2}\rceil } - b\lambda - c\lambda ^2 \mid b,c\in \mathbb {Z}\}\;. \end{aligned}$$
We check for all 
\(b,c\in \mathbb {Z}\)
 such that 
\({\left| {{\lceil {b\mu +c\mu ^2}\rceil } - b\lambda - c\lambda ^2}\right| } \le M\)
 that the resulting point is in 
\(Q_\lambda \)
. Assuming this inequality, we have
$$\begin{aligned} M&\ge {\left| {{\lceil {b\mu +c\mu ^2}\rceil } - b\lambda - c\lambda ^2}\right| }\\&\ge {\left| {b\mu + c\mu ^2 - b\lambda -c\lambda ^2}\right| } - 1 = {\left| {b(\mu -\lambda ) + c(\mu ^2-\lambda ^2)}\right| } - 1 \\&= |\mu -\lambda |\cdot {\left| {b+c(\mu +\lambda )}\right| } - 1 = |\mu -\lambda |\sqrt{(b+xc)^2+(yc)^2} - 1\;, \end{aligned}$$
where 
\(x := Re (\mu +\lambda ) = \mu + Re (\lambda )\)
 and 
\(y := Im (\mu +\lambda ) = Im (\lambda ) > 0\)
. Set 
\(s:= \mu +\lambda \)
. Letting 
b
 and 
c
 be arbitrary real numbers for a moment, we have 
\(|s|\cdot |b+cs| = |\overline{s}|\cdot |b+cs| = |b\overline{s} + cs\overline{s}| \ge |Im (b\overline{s} + cs\overline{s})| = |bIm (\overline{s})| = |b|\cdot |Im (s)| = |b|y\)
. Then we have,
$$\begin{aligned} \sqrt{(b+cs)^2 + (yc)^2} \ge |b+cs| \ge \frac{|b|y}{|s|} \end{aligned}$$
Thus 
\(|\mu -\lambda |\sqrt{(b+xc)^2+(yc)^2} - 1 \ge |\mu -\lambda |\cdot |b|y/|s| - 1\)
, which means it suffices to consider only those 
\(b\in \mathbb {Z}\)
 such that 
\(|\mu -\lambda |\cdot |b|y/r - 1 \le M\)
, or equivalently,
$$\begin{aligned} |b| \le \frac{r(M+1)}{y|\mu -\lambda |} \approx 11.41\;. \end{aligned}$$
Similarly, 
\(\sqrt{(b+xc)^2+(yc)^2} \ge |c|y\)
, so it suffices to consider only those 
\(c\in \mathbb {Z}\)
 such that 
\(|\mu -\lambda |\cdot |c|y - 1 \le M\)
, or equivalently,
$$\begin{aligned} |c| \le \frac{M+1}{y|\mu -\lambda |} \approx 10.76\;. \end{aligned}$$
A check by computer verifies that all values of the form 
\({\lceil {b\mu +c\mu ^2}\rceil } - b\lambda - c\lambda ^2\)
 are in 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 for integers 
\(-11 \le b \le 11\)
 and 
\(-10 \le c \le 10\)
. 
\(\square \)

                
                  
Proposition 8.3

                  
Let 
\(\nu \)
 be the root of the polynomial 
\(x^3+x-1\)
 closest to the point 
\(-0.341+1.162i\)
 (see Figure 
2
). Then 
\(Q_\nu = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
.

                
                  
Proof

                  
This proof proceeds just as the previous proof but with 
\(\nu \)
 replacing 
\(\lambda \)
. We have that 
\(\nu \)
 is cubic sPV with conjugate 
\(\mu \approx 0.682328\)
. Again, we can set 
\(\alpha := \nu \)
. Since 
\(\mu > 1/2\)
, we may again take 
\(X:= \{0,1\}\)
. This time we get 
\(M \approx 8.424341\)
, yielding the upper bound of approximately 7.06 for |
b
| and 5.24 for |
c
|.

                  
As before, a check by computer verifies that all values of the form 
\({\lceil {b\mu +c\mu ^2}\rceil } - b\nu - c\nu ^2\)
 are in 
\(Q_\nu \)
 for integers 
\(-7 \le b \le 7\)
 and 
\(-5 \le c \le 5\)
. 
\(\square \)

                
8.3 
Case Study: 
\(-\left( 3+\sqrt{17}\right) /2\)

                        
Here we prove that 
\(Q_\lambda = \mathord {\curlywedge }^{(\lambda )}({\left[ {0,1}\right] })\)
 for 
\(\lambda := \left( -3-\sqrt{17}\right) /2\)
. We make use of both Theorem 
6.12
 and Proposition 
8.1
.

                  
Proposition 8.4

                  
Let 
\(\lambda := -\left( 3+\sqrt{17}\right) /2\)
. Then 
\(Q_\lambda = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
, where 
\(P = {\left[ {0,1}\right] }\)
.

                
                  
Proof

                  
The minimal polynomial of 
\(\lambda \)
 and its conjugate 
\(\mu := \left( \sqrt{17}-3\right) /2\)
 is 
\(x^2+3x-2\)
. In this case, 
\(\lambda \)
 is not a unit of 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
, but 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 contains the value 
\(\alpha := 9-16\lambda \approx 65.9848\)
, which 
is
 a unit of 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
. A derivation showing that 
\(\alpha \in Q_\lambda \)
 was found by computer and is given by Table 
3
 in Appendix A. The conjugate of 
\(\alpha \)
 is then 
\(\beta = 1/\alpha = 9-16\mu \approx 0.015155\)
.

                  
We will apply Proposition 
8.1
 in this case. We start with the continued fraction expansion of 
\(\mu \)
, which is 
\([a_0,a_1,a_2,\ldots ] = [0,\overline{1,1,3}]\)
, where the bar indicates that the pattern 1, 1, 3 repeats forever. The table below gives the values needed to calculate 
n
 and 
m
 in that proposition. To get 
\(p_k\)
 and 
\(q_k\)
 for 
\(k\ge 0\)
, we can use the recurrences 
\(p_k = a_kp_{k-1} + p_{k-2}\)
 and 
\(q_k = a_kq_{k-1} + q_{k-2}\)
. The values of 
\(p_k\)
 and 
\(q_k\)
 for 
\(k<0\)
 are given in order to obtain the correct initial values when 
\(k=0\)
 and 
\(k=1\)
. Recall that 
\(\eta _k = (-1)^k(q_k\mu -p_k)\)
. 
 From the table, we get that 
\(\eta _7 + \eta _6 = 9-16\mu = \beta < \beta /(1-\beta )\)
, so 
\(k=7\)
 satisfies Eq. (
27
). Furthermore, this is the least such 
k
-value: 
\(0.023665 \approx \eta _6+\eta _5 = 41\mu -23 > \beta /(1-\beta ) = (2\mu -1)/8 \approx 0.015388\)
. Thus we set 
\(n:= q_7-1 = 72\)
, whence 
\(m = -36\)
. Then, by Eq. (
29
), we have
$$\begin{aligned} \max _{x\in X}|x| = {\lceil {36\mu }\rceil } - 36\lambda = 20 - 36\lambda \approx 148.215901 \end{aligned}$$
Thus we can set 
\( X:= \{1\} \cup \{ {\lceil {b\mu }\rceil }-b\lambda \mid b\in \mathbb {Z}\;\mathrel { \& }\;-36 \le b \le 36\}\)
, and this satisfies Eq. (
18
).
Footnote 
20
 Since 
\(\alpha > 1\)
, we have, from Eq. (
19
), 
\(M = \max _{x\in X}|x| = 20-36\lambda \)
 and thus 
\(Y = X\)
 in (
20
).

                  
To summarize, we have 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P) = Q_\lambda (X)\)
 by Theorem 
6.12
. Finally, a computer run shows that in fact, 
\(X\subseteq Q_\lambda \)
, and thus 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P) = Q_\lambda \)
. 
\(\square \)

                
8.4 
Case Study: 
\(\lambda _7 = (2-2\cos (\pi /7))^{-1}\)

                        
Recall Proposition 
7.6
, in which we characterize those 
\(\lambda _n\)
 (Eq. (
23
)) that are sPV (thus giving rise to uniformly discrete 
\(Q_{\lambda _n}\)
), for odd 
n
. We know that 
\(Q_{\lambda _3}\)
 and 
\(Q_{\lambda _5}\)
 are also relatively dense. In this section we use Theorem 
6.13
 to prove that 
\(Q_{\lambda _7}\)
 is also relatively dense.
For the remainder of this section let 
\(\lambda \)
 denote 
\(\lambda _7 \approx 5.0489\)
, whose minimal polynomial is 
\(x^3-6x^2+5x-1\)
. Denote the conjugates of 
\(\lambda \)
 by 
\(\mu , \nu \in {\left[ {0,1}\right] }\)
, where 
\(\mu < \nu \)
, in particular, 
\(\mu \approx 0.30798\)
 and 
\(\nu \approx 0.64310\)
. For 
\(x \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
, where 
x
 is of the form 
\(a_0+a_1\lambda +a_2\lambda ^2\)
 for 
\(a_0, a_1, a_2 \in \mathbb {Z}\)
, we denote its conjugates by 
\(\sigma _1 = a_0+a_1\mu +a_2\mu ^2\)
 and 
\(\sigma _2 = a_0+a_1\nu +a_2\nu ^2\)
. By Theorem 
4.14
, we have 
\(Q_\lambda \subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
, where 
\(P = {\left[ {0,1}\right] }^{\times 2}\)
. Let 
\(S \subseteq P\)
 denote the 
\((2\nu -1) \times (2\nu -1)\)
 square with corners at 
\((1-\nu ,1-\nu )\)
 and 
\((\nu , \nu )\)
. It is convenient to introduce the parallelogram 
T
 with corners at 
\((0,0), (\mu ,\nu ), (1-\mu ,1-\nu ), \)
 and (1, 1). It is easy to see that 
\(S \subseteq T \subseteq P\)
 and hence 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(S) \subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(T) \subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
. We have,
$$\begin{aligned} \mathord {\curlywedge }(T) = \{x \in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ] \mid (\sigma _1,\sigma _2) \in T\}. \end{aligned}$$
We can characterize the points of 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(T)\)
 as follows. Let 
\(\beta = \mu +\nu -\mu \nu \)
 and define
$$\begin{aligned} n(m):= &   \lceil m/\lambda \rceil = \lceil \mu \nu m\rceil \\ \ell (m):= &   \lfloor \beta m \rfloor + n(m)\\ p(m):= &   n(m) - \ell (m)\lambda + m \lambda ^2. \end{aligned}$$

                  
Proposition 8.5

                  
\( \mathord {\curlywedge }(T) = \{1,\lambda , 1-\lambda \}\cup \{p(m) \mid m \in \mathbb {Z}\}.\)

                
                  
Proof

                  
It is easiest to see this by transforming 
P
 via the inverse of the following matrix 
W
 (which 
\( = V(\mu ,\nu )\)
 as in Lemma 
4.17
):
$$\begin{aligned} W&= \left[ \begin{array}{cc} 1 & \mu \\ 1 & \nu \\ \end{array}\right] \;,&W^{-1}&=\frac{1}{\nu -\mu }\left[ \begin{array}{cc} \nu & -\mu \\ -1 &  1\\ \end{array}\right] . \; \end{aligned}$$
We interpret the column vector 
\([\sigma _1,\sigma _2]^{T}\)
 as the point 
\((\sigma _1,\sigma _2)\)
. Let 
\(\Omega = W^{-1}T\)
. It is easy to see that 
\(W^{-1}\)
 maps 
\((0,0) \mapsto (0,0)\)
, 
\((\mu , \nu ) \mapsto (0,1)\)
, 
\((1,1) \mapsto (1,0)\)
, and 
\((1-\mu ,1-\nu ) \mapsto (1,-1)\)
. Thus 
\(\Omega \)
 is a parallelogram with corners at (0, 0), (0, 1), (1, 0), and 
\((1,-1)\)
. More generally, 
\(W^{-1}\)
 maps 
\((\sigma _1,\sigma _2) = (a_0+a_1\mu +a_2\mu ^2, a_0+a_1\nu +a_2\nu ^2)\)
 to 
\((a_0-\mu \nu a_2, a_1+(\mu +\nu )a_2)\)
. Thus 
\((\sigma _1,\sigma _2) \in T\)
 iff the following conditions hold:
$$\begin{aligned} \begin{array}{ccccc} 0 &  \le &  a_0-\mu \nu a_2 &  \le &  1\\ -a_0+\mu \nu a_2 &  \le &  a_1+(\mu +\nu )a_2 &  \le &  -a_0+\mu \nu a_2+1\; \end{array}\; \end{aligned}$$

                    (30)
                
Note that for 
\(a_2 \not = 0\)
, all inequalities above are proper because 
\(\mu \)
 and 
\(\nu \)
 are irrational. The first inequality shows that 
\(a_0 = \lceil \mu \nu a_2\rceil \)
, and the second 
\(a_1 = -\lceil \mu \nu a_2\rceil + \lceil \mu \nu -(\mu +\nu )a_2\rceil = -\lceil \mu \nu a_2\rceil - \lfloor \beta a_2\rfloor \)
. Letting 
m
 denote 
\(a_2\)
, this yields 
\(a_0 = n(m)\)
 and 
\(a_1 = -\ell (m)\)
. Thus the points in 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(T)\)
 are of the form 
p
(
m
), 
\(m \in \mathbb {Z}\)
. The exceptions are the nonzero corners of 
T
, namely (1, 0), (0, 1) and 
\((1,-1)\)
, which correspond to the values 1, 
\(\lambda \)
 and 
\(1-\lambda \)
. 
\(\square \)

                
                  
Remark

                  
The apparent “outlier" nature of the points 
\(1, \lambda \)
, and 
\(1-\lambda \)
 is an artifact of the definition of 
p
(
m
), which has a 
\(\lambda ^2\)
 term for any 
\(m \not = 0\)
. In that case, the inequalities in Eq. (
30
) are proper. However, for 
\(m = a_2 = 0\)
, the equalities can be met, which accounts for the points 
\(1, \lambda \)
, and 
\(1-\lambda \)
. The one value of 
p
(
m
) that works out for one of the initial points is 
p
(0), all of whose terms are 0. 
\(\square \)

                
We find it is possible to choose a finite number of points in 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(T)\)
 to construct the set 
Y
 of Theorem 
6.13
. We remark that all the points we use are in 
\(T\setminus S\)
.
We now prove the main result of this section. Since 
\(\lambda \)
 is a unit, by Theorem 
6.13
, there exists a finite 
\(Y \subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 such that 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(S) \subseteq Q_\lambda (Y)\)
. Our task here is to explicitly construct such a 
Y
 such that 
\(Y \subseteq Q_\lambda \)
. That, in turn, requires finding an explicit finite cover of the set 
S
, indexed by a finite set 
X
. Once 
X
 is found, the problem reduces to the tedious but routine verification that 
\(Y \subseteq Q_\lambda \)
, and finally appealing to Lemma 
6.16
.

                  
Lemma 8.6

                  
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(S) \subseteq Q_\lambda \)
.

                
                  
Proof

                  
We adopt the notations and conventions of the proof of Theorem 
6.13
. We construct the set 
Y
 and show that 
\(Y \subseteq Q_\lambda \)
. Since 
\(Q_\lambda (Y) = Q_\lambda \)
, the result follows easily by Theorem 
6.13
. Here there are two conjugates 
\(\mu , \nu \)
, the role of the matrix 
\(\Lambda \)
 in the proof of Theorem 
6.13
 is here played by 
W
 defined above, and the sets 
\(J_x\)
 that form a cover are 2-dimensional rectangular regions.

                  
Let 
\(X = \{\lambda , 1-\lambda \}\cup \{p(m)\mid m=\pm 12, \pm 13, \pm 16, \pm 17\}\)
. We claim that 
\(C=\{J_x \mid x \in X\}\)
 forms a cover of 
S
. Following the notation of Theorem 
6.13
, we set 
\(\varepsilon = 1-\nu \)
. Hence the sets in 
C
 are of the form,
$$\begin{aligned} J_x = {\left[ {\sigma _1\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\mu }}(1-\nu ),\;\sigma _1\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\mu }}\nu }\right] } \times {\left[ {\sigma _2\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\nu }}(1-\nu ),\;\sigma _2\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\nu }}\nu }\right] }. \end{aligned}$$

                    (31)
                
These are rectangles of width 
\((2\nu - 1)\mu \)
 and height 
\((2\nu -1)\nu \)
 extrapolated from 
\((\sigma _1,\sigma _2)\)
 with 
\(1-\nu \)
 and 
\(\nu \)
. In Figure 
3
, we show the set 
S
 in red and the sets 
\(J_x\)
 in blue (displaying the index 
x
, which is either 
\(\lambda \)
, 
\(1-\lambda \)
 or 
m
 for the appropriate points 
p
(
m
)). We slightly abuse notation and denote 
\(J_{p(m)}\)
 by 
\(J_m\)
. Using decimal approximations, we list the sets 
\(J_x\)
 in Table 
1
, each one specified by the coordinates 
\((\sigma _1^{(0)},\sigma _2^{(0)})\)
 of its lower left-hand corner and 
\((\sigma _1^{(1)},\sigma _2^{(1)})\)
 of its upper right-hand corner.
Fig. 3
Full size image
A cover of 
S
. 
S
 is in red, the sets 
\(J_{\lambda }, J_{1-\lambda }\)
, and 
\(J_{m}\)
 for 
\(|m| = 12, 13, 16, 17\)
 in blue (labelled with the value of 
m
 rather than 
p
(
m
) for the latter sets). The entire graph represents 
P
Table 1 Coordinates of the 
\(J_x\)
 of Figure 
3
.
Full size table

                  
Observe that for each 
x
 such that 
\(\sigma _2^{(0)} \le 1-\nu \approx 0.35689587\)
, the value of 
\(\sigma _2^{(1)}\)
 exceeds 1/2 (these are the points 
p
(
m
) for 
\(m = 17, -12, 13, -16\)
 and 
\(1-\lambda \)
; for that last point, 
\(\sigma _2^{(0)} = 1-\nu \)
 exactly). Similarly, for each 
x
 such that 
\(\sigma _2^{(1)} \ge \nu \approx 0.64310413\)
, the value of 
\(\sigma _2^{(0)}\)
 is less than 1/2 (these are the reflections 
p
(
m
) for 
\(m = -17, 12, -13, 16\)
 and 
\(\lambda \)
; for that last point, 
\(\sigma _2^{(1)} = \nu \)
 exactly). We also note that those pairs of 
\(J_x\)
 which appear to touch along the vertical boundaries actually intersect. For example, for 
p
(17), 
\(\sigma _1^{(1)} = 0.41618279\)
, which exceeds 0.41611289, the value of 
\(\sigma _1^{(0)}\)
 for 
\(p(-12)\)
. This can be similarly verified for the pairs 
\((p(16), p(-13))\)
, 
\((p(12),p(-17))\)
, 
\((p(13), p(-16))\)
, so that the sets 
\(J_{17}\cup J_{-12}\)
, 
\(J_{16}\cup J_{-13}\)
, 
\(J_{12}\cup J_{-17}\)
, and 
\(J_{13}\cup J_{-16}\)
 are each connected. Thus we claim that if we divide 
S
 into the two subsets 
\(L = \{(x',\sigma _2) \in S\mid 1-\nu \le x' \le \nu ~ \text{ and }~ 1-\nu \le \sigma _2 \le 1/2\}\)
 and 
\(U = \{(x',\sigma _2) \in S\mid 1-\nu \le x' \le \nu ~\text{ and }~ 1/2 \le \sigma _2 \le \nu \}\)
, we find that,
$$\begin{aligned} L\subseteq &   (J_{17}\cup J_{-12}) \cup (J_{13} \cup J_{-16}) \cup J_{1-\lambda }, \end{aligned}$$

                    (32)
                
$$\begin{aligned} U\subseteq &   J_{\lambda }\cup ( J_{16} \cup J_{-13})\cup ( J_{12}\cup J_{-17}), \end{aligned}$$

                    (33)
                
which shows that 
\(S \subseteq \bigcup C = \bigcup _{x \in X} J_x\)
.

                  
To verify Eq. (
32
), given that 
\(\sigma _2^{(0)} \le 1-\nu \)
 and 
\(\sigma _2^{(1)} > 1/2\)
 for each set on the RHS, we only need to verify that each point 
\((\sigma _1,\sigma _2)\)
 with 
\(1-\nu \le \sigma _1 \le \nu \)
 and 
\(1-\nu \le \sigma _2 \le 1/2\)
 is contained in one of 
\(J_{17}\cup J_{-12}\)
, 
\(J_{13}\cup J_{-16}\)
, or 
\(J_{1-\lambda }\)
. Eq. (
33
) can be established similarly, for 
\(\sigma _2 \ge 1/2\)
. Using Table 
1
, we enumerate the cases in Table 
2
. We conclude that 
C
 covers 
S
.
Table 2 The union of the intervals in the left column of the left table is 
\([1- \nu ,\nu ]\)
, so Eq. (
32
) follows. Eq. (
33
) is established similarly, for 
\(\sigma _2 \ge 1/2\)
 (right table).
Full size table

                  
We now determine the set 
\(Y:= X \cup \{y\in \mathord {\curlywedge }(S): |y| \le M\}\)
. Let 
\(Y':= X \cup \{y\in \mathord {\curlywedge }(T): |y| \le M\}\)
. In this case 
\(M = \frac{|\lambda -1|}{|\lambda |-1} \max _{x\in X} |x| = \max _{x\in X} |x|\)
, since 
\(\lambda > 1\)
. Observing that the 
\(\lambda ^2\)
 term dominates, it is not hard to see that |
p
(
m
)| is monotonically increasing in |
m
|. Hence 
\(Y' = \{1,\lambda , 1-\lambda \}\cup \{p(m): |m| \le 17\}\)
. A derivation of each of these points from the starting points 
\(p_0=0\)
 and 
\(p_1=1\)
 is given in Appendix A, Table 
4
. This proves that 
\(Y' \subseteq Q_\lambda \)
, and hence 
\(Q_\lambda (Y') = Q_\lambda \)
. It follows that 
\(Q_\lambda (Y) = Q_\lambda \)
, since 
\(\{0,1\}\subseteq Y \subseteq Y'\)
. Now 
Y
 has been constructed to satisfy the properties of the 
Y
 of Lemma 
6.16
 and, furthermore, 
\(\lambda \)
 is a unit so we can use it in place of 
\(\alpha \)
 in that lemma. Hence 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(S) \subseteq Q_\lambda \)
. 
\(\square \)

                
By Lemma 
8.6
, 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(S) \subseteq Q_{\lambda }\)
, hence since 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(S)\)
 is relatively dense, so is 
\(Q_\lambda \)
. Moreover, by Fact 
4.19
, 
\(Q_\lambda \subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
, so we have,

                  
Theorem 8.7

                  
\(Q_{\lambda _7}\)
 is a Meyer set.

                
This, and many similar results, can be proved via Corollary 
6.23
, although that does not show explicitly that 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 contains a model set.
9 
Applications of Affine Embedding
9.1 
Case study: 
\(\lambda := -(3+\sqrt{13})/2\)

                        
In this subsection we use Theorems 
6.9
 and 
6.12
 to identify points that are in 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 but not in 
\(Q_\lambda \)
, for 
\(\lambda := -(3+\sqrt{13})/2\)
 and 
\(P:= {\left[ {0,1}\right] }\)
. Again making use of Theorem 
6.12
, as well as affine embedding, we also show that 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {R}\)
 and hence a Meyer set.

                  
Proposition 9.1

                  
Letting 
\(\lambda := -(3+\sqrt{13})/2\)
, we have
$$\begin{aligned} Q_\lambda (\{0,1,2\lambda \}) = Q_\lambda (\{0,1,1-2\lambda \}) = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\;, \end{aligned}$$
where 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P) = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)^{(\lambda )}\)
 is the cut-and-project (model) set 
\(\{x\in \mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]: x' \in {\left[ {0,1}\right] }\}\)
 of Definition 
4.13
.

                
                  
Proof

                  
We let 
\(\mu := \lambda ' = (\sqrt{13}-3)/2 \approx 0.302776\)
 be the conjugate of 
\(\lambda \)
, and we note that 
\(\mu ^2 = 1-3\mu \)
. The fact that 
\(\lambda \)
 is already a unit of 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
 means we can set 
\(\alpha := \lambda \)
 (and thus 
\(\beta = \mu \)
) in Theorem 
6.12
 (and obviously, 
\(\alpha \in Q_\lambda \)
 in this case). We have 
\((2\lambda )' = 2\mu \in {\left[ {0,1}\right] }\)
, so 
\(2\lambda \in \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
. In order to apply Theorem 
6.12
, we let 
\(X:= \{0,1,\lambda ,2\lambda \}\)
. One can readily check using decimal approximations that
$$\begin{aligned} {\left[ {0,1}\right] }&= {\left[ {0,\mu }\right] } \cup {\left[ {1-\mu ,1}\right] } \cup {\left[ {4\mu -1,5\mu -1}\right] } \cup {\left[ {8\mu -2,9\mu -2}\right] }\\&= \bigcup _{x\in X} {\left[ {(1-\mu )x',(1-\mu )x' + \mu }\right] }\;, \end{aligned}$$
and thus 
X
 satisfies Eq. (
18
). Letting 
M
 be as in Eq. (
19
), we have 
\(M = \frac{\lambda -1}{\lambda +1}\max _{x\in X}|x| = \frac{2+\sqrt{13}}{3}|2\lambda | = \frac{2+\sqrt{13}}{3}(-2\lambda ) = (19+5\sqrt{13})/3 \approx 12.342585\)
. Then by Eqs. (
8
) and (
20
),
$$\begin{aligned} Y = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P) \cap {\left[ {-M,M}\right] } = \{3\lambda ,\;2\lambda ,\;\lambda ,\;0,\;1,\;1-\lambda ,\;1-2\lambda ,\;1-3\lambda \}\;. \end{aligned}$$
From Theorem 
6.12
, we know that 
\(Q_\lambda (Y) = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
, and thus we are done if we can show that 
\(Y \subseteq Q_\lambda (\{0,1,2\lambda \})\)
. Clearly, 
\(\{2\lambda ,\lambda ,0,1,1-\lambda \}\subseteq Q_\lambda (\{0,1,2\lambda \})\)
. For the other three elements of 
Y
, we have, noting that 
\(\lambda ^2 = 1-3\lambda \)
,
$$\begin{aligned} 3\lambda&= 1-(1-3\lambda ) = 1-\lambda ^2 = (1-\lambda ) + \lambda (1-\lambda ) = 1\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}(1-\lambda )\;, \\ 1-2\lambda&= \lambda + (1-3\lambda ) = \lambda +\lambda ^2 = (1-\lambda )\lambda + 2\lambda ^2 = \lambda \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}(2\lambda )\;, \\ 1-3\lambda&= \lambda ^2 = 0\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}\lambda \;. \end{aligned}$$
Thus 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P) = Q_\lambda (Y) \subseteq Q_\lambda (Q_\lambda (\{0,1,2\lambda \})) = Q_\lambda (\{0,1,2\lambda \}) \subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
, since 
\(\{0,1,2\lambda \}\subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 and 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 is 
\(\lambda \)
-convex. Thus 
\(Q_\lambda (\{0,1,2\lambda \}) = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
. To see that 
\(Q_\lambda (\{0,1,1-2\lambda \}) = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
, observe that 
\(1-2\lambda \in \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 and that 
\(2\lambda \in Q_\lambda (\{0,1,1-2\lambda \})\)
; indeed,
$$\begin{aligned} 2\lambda = 1-\lambda -(1-3\lambda ) = 1-\lambda -\lambda ^2 = (1-\lambda )(1-\lambda ) + \lambda (1-2\lambda ) = (1-\lambda )\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}(1-2\lambda )\;. \end{aligned}$$
Thus we have 
\(Q_\lambda (\{0,1,1-2\lambda \}) = Q_\lambda (\{0,1,2\lambda \}) = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
. 
\(\square \)

                
We know from Theorem 
6.9
 that 
\(Q_{-(3+\sqrt{13})/2} \ne \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
. Proposition 
9.1
 gives us two specific points that are in 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 but not in 
\(Q_\lambda \)
:

                  
Corollary 9.2

                  
Let 
\(\lambda := -(3+\sqrt{13})/2\)
. Then 
\(\{2\lambda ,1-2\lambda \} \subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P) \setminus Q_\lambda \)
.

                
                  
Proof

                  
One readily checks that 
\(2\lambda \)
 and 
\(1-2\lambda \)
 are both in 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
. But if either one (say, 
\(2\lambda \)
) is in 
\(Q_\lambda \)
, then we would have 
\(Q_\lambda = Q_\lambda (\{0,1,2\lambda \}) = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
, and we know by Theorem 
6.9
 that 
\(Q_\lambda \ne \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
. 
\(\square \)

                
Using the idea of affine embedding, it follows easily from Proposition 
9.1
 that 
\(Q_{-(3+\sqrt{13})/2}\)
 is relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {R}\)
.

                  
Corollary 9.3

                  
(to Proposition 
9.1
) 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {R}\)
, where 
\(\lambda := -(3+\sqrt{13})/2\)
.

                
                  
Proof

                  
Observe that 
\(\{0,1,2\lambda \} \mathrel {\hookrightarrow }Q_\lambda \)
. In fact,
$$\begin{aligned} \rho _{0,1-\lambda }(\{0,1,2\lambda \}) = \{0,1-\lambda ,2\lambda (1-\lambda )\} = \{0,1-\lambda ,\lambda \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}(1-\lambda )\} \subseteq Q_\lambda \;. \end{aligned}$$
Thus by Proposition 
9.1
 and Fact 
6.18
 (with 
\(F = \mathbb {R}\)
), we have 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P) = Q_\lambda (\{0,1,2\lambda \}) \mathrel {\hookrightarrow }Q_\lambda (Q_\lambda ) = Q_\lambda \)
, and since 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 is relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {R}\)
, it follows that 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {R}\)
. 
\(\square \)

                
                  
Remark

                  
The last proof shows that 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P) \mathrel {\leftrightharpoons }Q_\lambda \)
 for 
\(\lambda := -(3+\sqrt{13})/2\)
, because clearly, 
\(Q_\lambda \subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 and thus 
\(Q_\lambda \mathrel {\hookrightarrow }\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
. 
\(\square \)

                
10 
Higher Dimensions
For certain 
\(\lambda \)
, we can use the relative density results in 
\(\mathbb {R}\)
 to prove relative density in higher dimensions. We have already shown (Theorem 
6.9
) that 
\(Q_{1+\varphi }\)
 is relatively dense, where 
\(\varphi \)
 is the golden ratio, and similarly for 
\(Q_{2+\sqrt{2}}\)
 and 
\(Q_{2+\sqrt{3}}\)
 in Theorem 
6.9
.
The next two lemmas allow us in some cases to prove relative density in higher dimensions based on relative density in 
\(\mathbb {R}\)
.

                
Lemma 10.1

                
For any 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {R}\)
 and any 
\(S\subseteq \mathbb {R}^n\)
 (for 
\(n\ge 1\)
), if 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {R}\)
 and 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 contains the vertices of a parallelepiped with nonzero 
n
-dimensional volume, then 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 is relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {R}^n\)
.

              
                
Proof

                
We first prove the special case where 
\(S = C_n:= \{0,1\}^n \subseteq \mathbb {R}^n\)
, the corners of the unit hypercube in 
\(\mathbb {R}^n\)
. In this case, we show that 
\(Q_\lambda (C_n) \supseteq Q_\lambda \times \cdots \times Q_\lambda \)
 (
n
-fold Cartesian product) by induction on 
n
.
Footnote 
21
 Assuming 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {R}\)
, the right-hand side is clearly relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {R}^n\)
, which proves the lemma in this special case. Afterwards, we show how the general case follows easily.

                
In the special case, the statement above is trivial for 
\(n=1\)
, where 
\(C_1 = \{0,1\}\)
 and hence 
\(Q_\lambda (C_1) = Q_\lambda \)
. Now assume 
\(n\ge 1\)
 and that the statement above holds for 
n
, i.e., 
\(Q_\lambda (C_n) \supseteq (Q_\lambda )^{\times n}\)
. In 
\(\mathbb {R}^{n+1}\)
, it is easily checked that 
\(Q_\lambda (C_n\times \{b\}) = Q_\lambda (C_n)\times \{b\}\)
 for 
\(b\in \{0,1\}\)
. Whence
$$\begin{aligned} Q_\lambda (C_{n+1}) = Q_\lambda (C_n\times \{0,1\}) \supseteq Q_\lambda (C_n)\times \{0,1\}\;. \end{aligned}$$
That is, for every 
\(v \in Q_\lambda (C_n)\)
, we have 
\(v\times \{0,1\}\subseteq Q_\lambda (C_{n+1})\)
, and from this we get
$$\begin{aligned} Q_\lambda (\{v\}\times \{0,1\}) = \{v\}\times Q_\lambda \subseteq Q_\lambda (C_{n+1})\;. \end{aligned}$$
This holds for all 
\(v\in Q_\lambda (C_n)\)
, and so by the inductive hypothesis, it holds for all 
\(v\in (Q_\lambda )^{\times n}\)
. Thus,
$$\begin{aligned} (Q_\lambda )^{\times (n+1)} = (Q_\lambda )^{\times n}\times Q_\lambda \subseteq Q_\lambda (C_{n+1})\;, \end{aligned}$$
as we wished to show.

                
Now in the general case, we can assume without loss of generality that 
\(S = \{x_b \mid b\in \{0,1\}^n\} \subseteq \mathbb {R}^n\)
, where the 
\(x_b\)
 form the corners of a nondegenerate parallelepiped. We can index the points so that there exists an invertible 
\(\mathbb {R}\)
-linear map 
\({{\ell }:{\mathbb {R}^n}\rightarrow {\mathbb {R}^n}}\)
 that maps each 
\(b\in \{0,1\}^n\)
 to 
\(x_b - x_0\)
. Letting 
\(S':= \ell ^{-1}(S - x_0)\)
, we see that 
\(S' = \{0,1\}^n\)
, and thus 
\(Q_\lambda (S')\)
 is relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {R}^n\)
 by the special case proved above. By Lemma 
2.11
Footnote 
22
 we have
$$\begin{aligned} x_0 + \ell (Q_\lambda (S')) = x_0 + Q_\lambda (\ell (S')) = x_0 + Q_\lambda (S-x_0) = Q_\lambda (S)\;. \end{aligned}$$
The left-hand side is seen to be relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {R}^n\)
 because this property is preserved under invertible 
\(\mathbb {R}\)
-linear maps and under translation. 
\(\square \)

              
                
Lemma 10.2

                
For any 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {R}\)
 and 
\(S\subseteq \mathbb {C}\)
, if 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {R}\)
 and 
S
 includes three non-colinear points, then 
\(Q_\lambda (S)\)
 is relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
.

              
                
Proof

                
For any 
\(z\in \mathbb {C}\)
 such that 
\(Im (z) > 0\)
, define 
\(\Delta _z:= \{0,1,z\}\)
. To say that 
S
 contains three non-colinear points is equivalent to saying that 
\(\Delta _z \mathrel {\hookrightarrow }S\)
 for some such 
z
. Thus it suffices by Fact 
6.18
 to show that 
\(Q_\lambda (\Delta _z)\)
 is relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
 for all such 
z
.

                
Fix a 
\(z:= x+yi\)
 for some arbitrary 
\(x,y\in \mathbb {R}\)
 with 
\(y>0\)
. Since 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {R}\)
, we must have 
\(\lambda \notin {\left[ {0,1}\right] }\)
. Let 
\(\sigma := \lambda (1-\lambda )\)
. Then 
\(\sigma < 0\)
, and both 
\(\sigma \)
 and 
\(2\sigma \)
 are in 
\(Q_\lambda \)
:
$$\begin{aligned} \sigma&= \lambda \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}0\;,&2\sigma&= \lambda \mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}(1-\lambda )\;. \end{aligned}$$
Consider the four points
$$\begin{aligned} a&:= 0\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\sigma }}1&b&:= 1\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\sigma }}0 \\ c&:= 0\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{2\sigma }}z&d&:= 1\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{2\sigma }}z \end{aligned}$$
All four points are in 
\(Q_\lambda (\Delta _z)\)
 by Lemma 
2.17
(1): 
a
 and 
b
 are in 
\(Q_\sigma \subseteq Q_\lambda \subseteq Q_\lambda (\Delta _z)\)
, and 
c
 and 
d
 are in 
\(Q_{2\sigma }(\Delta _z) \subseteq Q_\lambda (\Delta _z)\)
. A little calculation shows that
$$\begin{aligned} a&= \sigma&b&= 1 - \sigma \\ c&= 2\sigma z = 2\sigma x + 2\sigma yi&d&= (1-2\sigma ) + 2\sigma z = 1-2\sigma + 2\sigma x + 2\sigma yi \end{aligned}$$
Thus 
a
, 
b
, 
c
, and 
d
 form the vertices of a parallelogram in 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
, and this parallelogram is nondegenerate, since 
\(\sigma \ne 1-\sigma \)
 and 
\(2\sigma y \ne 0\)
. It follows that 
\(Q_\lambda (\Delta _z)\)
 is relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
 by Lemma 
10.1
. 
\(\square \)

              
The following corollary covers all the sets shown in Figures 
5
–
8
, showing that they are all relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
.

                
Corollary 10.3

                
The following sets are all relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
: 

                  

                    
1.

                    
                      
\(Q_{\lambda _5}(P_5)\)
 and 
\(Q_{\lambda _5}(P_{10})\)
 (recall 
\(\lambda _5 = 1+\varphi = (3+\sqrt{5})/2\)
)

                    
                  

                  

                    
2.

                    
                      
\(Q_{\lambda _7}(P_7)\)
 and 
\(Q_{\lambda _7}(P_{14})\)
 (cf. Eq. (
23
))

                    
                  

                  

                    
3.

                    
                      
\(Q_{2+\sqrt{2}}(P_8)\)

                    
                  

                  

                    
4.

                    
                      
\(Q_{2+\sqrt{3}}(P_{12})\)

                    
                  

                  

                    
5.

                    
                      
\(Q_{\lambda _9}(P_9)\)
 and 
\(Q_{\lambda _9}(P_{18})\)
 (cf. Eq. (
23
))

                    
                  

                  

                    
6.

                    
                      
\(Q_{\lambda _{15}}(P_{15})\)
 and 
\(Q_{\lambda _{15}}(P_{30})\)
 (cf. Eq. (
23
))

                    
                  

                

              
                
Proof

                
Consulting the table in the proof of Proposition 
7.6
, we see that 
\(\lambda _n\)
 has field norm 
\(\pm 1\)
 for 
\(n\in \{5,7,9,15\}\)
 and is thus a unit of 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda _n]\)
. Applying Corollary 
6.23
, we get that 
\(Q_{\lambda _n}\)
 is relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {R}\)
 for these 
n
.
Footnote 
23
 Theorem 
6.8
 also implies 
\(Q_{2+\sqrt{2}}\)
 and 
\(Q_{2+\sqrt{3}}\)
 are relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {R}\)
. We apply Lemma 
10.2
 to all these sets. 
\(\square \)

              
                
Corollary 10.4

                
All the sets mentioned in Corollary 
10.3
 are Meyer sets.

              
                
Proof

                
For 
S
 be any of these sets, we have that 
\(S-S\)
 is uniformly discrete by Propositions 
7.5
, 
7.6
, and 
7.10
. Now we apply Fact 
4.9
(2) to 
S
. 
\(\square \)

              
                
Proposition 10.5

                
The 
\((1+\varphi )\)
-convex hull of a regular dodecahedron in 
\(\mathbb {R}^3\)
 is relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {R}^3\)
.

              
                
Proof

                
Let 
\(\lambda := 1+\varphi \)
 and let 
D
 be a regular dodecahedron in 
\(\mathbb {R}^3\)
. Choose some pair of opposite pentagonal faces 
F
 and 
\(F'\)
 of 
D
. The set of corners of each of 
F
 and 
\(F'\)
 is congruent to 
\(P_5\)
, and so 
\(Q_\lambda (D)\)
 includes a congruent copy of 
\(Q_\lambda (P_5)\)
 in each of the (parallel) planes containing 
F
 and 
\(F'\)
. Each copy contains the corners of a nondegenerate parallelogram (rhombus, actually; see Figure 
4
), and these rhombi can be chosen so that the one in 
\(F'\)
 is a translation of the one in 
F
. We now apply Lemma 
10.1
.
Fig. 4
Full size image
Two opposite pentagonal faces 
\(F = \{a,b,c,d,e\}\)
 (dashed lines) and 
\(F'=\{a',b',c',d',e'\}\)
 (dotted lines) of a regular dodecahedron 
D
 are shown together with two rhombi extending out from each in opposite directions. The rhombus on the right (thick solid lines) lies in the plane of 
F
 and is formed from points 
a
, 
c
, 
e
, and 
f
, the latter of which is seen to be in 
\(Q_\lambda (F)\)
. Similarly, the rhombus on the left (thin solid lines) lies in the (parallel) plane of 
\(F'\)
 and is formed from the points 
\(a'\)
, 
\(c'\)
, 
\(e'\)
, and 
\(f'\in Q_\lambda (F')\)
. (These points are antipodal with respect to 
D
 to the points 
a
, 
c
, 
e
, and 
f
, respectively.) Thus the points 
\(\{a,c,e,f,a',c',e',f'\}\)
 are all in 
\(Q_\lambda (D)\)
 and form the corners of a nondegenerate parallelepiped in 
\(\mathbb {R}^3\)
. Here, 
\(\lambda = 1+\varphi \)
, and 
\({\mathop {\star }}\)
 means 
\(\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}\)

                
\(\square \)

              
11 
Conjectures, Open Problems, and Future Research
We know that 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is discrete for all strong PV numbers 
\(\lambda \)
 by the Discreteness Theorem (Theorem 
4.12
), but we know of no other 
\(\lambda \)
 for which 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is discrete.

                
Conjecture 11.1

                
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is discrete if and only if 
\(\lambda \)
 is a strong PV number.

              
Pinch [
40
, Theorem 15] showed that every real, non-integer element of 
\(\mathcal {D}\)
 must have at least one conjugate in 
\({\left( {0,1}\right) }\)
.
The following open question (and its generalizations) is one of the most interesting.

                
Open Question 11.2

                
For which sPV 
\(\lambda \)
 does set equality hold between 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 and 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 in Eq. (
7
)?

              
We have settled this question for real quadratic sPV 
\(\lambda \)
 (Theorem 
6.9
), strengthening previous results of Masáková et al. [
30
] obtained via different means. When equality holds, 
\(Q_{\lambda }\)
 is a model set and relative density of 
\(Q_{\lambda }\)
 follows immediately. This leads to two related open questions:

                
Open Question 11.3

                
Is 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {R}\)
 for all 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {R}\setminus {\left[ {0,1}\right] }\)
? Is 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
 for all 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {C}\setminus \mathbb {R}\)
? (If not, then for which 
\(\lambda \)
?)

              
Proving relative density of 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 for other sPV 
\(\lambda \)
 will apparently require new techniques. We make three conjectures in this regard. Let 
\(P:= {\left[ {0,1}\right] }\)
, and for 
\(\lambda \in \mathbb {C}\)
, let 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P):= \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)^{(\lambda )}\)
 (Definition 
4.13
).

                
Conjecture 11.4

                
If 
\(\lambda \)
 is real quadratic sPV, then the corresponding cut-and-project set 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 is finitely generated, that is, there exists a finite 
\(Y\subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 such that 
\(Q_\lambda (Y) = \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
.

              
Extending the above, we conjecture the following, further generalizing Theorem 
6.13
 so that it holds for inclusion of the entire set 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 in 
\(Q_\lambda (Y)\)
.

                
Conjecture 11.5

                
For any sPV 
\(\lambda \)
, there exists a finite set 
\(Y\subseteq \mathord {\curlywedge }(P)\)
 such that 
\(\mathord {\curlywedge }(P) \subseteq Q_\lambda (Y)\)
.

              
                
Open Question 11.6

                
Is 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 relatively dense in 
\(\mathbb {C}\)
 for all non-real quadratic sPV 
\(\lambda \)
? Is it periodic?

              
It would also be interesting to pin down 
\(\mathcal {C}\cap \mathbb {R}\)
 and 
\(\mathcal {C}\cap \{ z\in \mathbb {C}\mid Re (z) = 1/2 \}\)
. These two cases may be easier than the general case, as they present symmetries not shared by all 
\(\lambda \)
.

                
Research Plan 11.7

                
Determine which 
\(\lambda >3\)
 and which 
\(\lambda \)
 with 
\(Re (\lambda ) = 1/2\)
 yield discrete 
\(Q_\lambda \)
.

              
Recall that 
\(Q_{[x]}\)
 is the smallest set of polynomials in 
\(\mathbb {Z}[x]\)
 containing 0, 1 and closed under the binary operation 
\((p,q)\mapsto (1-x)p+xq\)
 (Notation 
3.1
). We proved an alternate characterization of 
\(Q_{[x]}\)
: an integer polynomial 
p
 is in 
\(Q_{[x]}\)
 if and only if either 
\(p\in \{0,1\}\)
 or 
\(0<p(\mu )<1\)
 for all 
\(0<\mu <1\)
 (Theorem 
3.6
). This characterization can be used to construct a decision procedure for 
\(Q_{[x]}\)
. What, then, is the complexity of deciding 
\(Q_{[x]}\)
?
There are other interesting (though noncomputational) questions regarding 
\(Q_{[x]}\)
. We know that there are 14 polynomials in 
\(Q_{[x]}\)
 of degree 
\(\le 2\)
 and can list them, but ...

                
Open Question 11.8

                
How many polynomials of degree 3 are there in 
\(Q_{[x]}\)
? What are they?

              
The proof of Theorem 
3.6
 (and especially Lemma 
3.13
) bears some tantalizing similarity to certain proofs of the Weierstrass Approximation Theorem using Bernstein polynomials [
9
] (see [
16
] for example). Despite this, we know of no way to use these concepts to shorten the proof of Theorem 
3.6
.

                
Open Question 11.9

                
Is there a simple proof of Theorem 
3.6
 using known facts about Bernstein polynomials or, more generally, the theory of continuous function approximation?

              
                
Definition 11.10

                
A discrete set 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is 
maximal
 if it is not a proper subset of any discrete 
\(Q_\mu \)
.

              
                
Open Question 11.11

                
Do maximal 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 exist? Is there an easy way to characterize the 
\(\lambda \)
 such that 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 is maximal?

              
                
Open Question 11.12

                
Is there an easy way to characterize the minimum polynomials of strong PV numbers? Short of that, find such polynomials of higher and higher degree. (We currently can characterize all such polynomials of degree 
\(\le 4\)
.)

              

                                

                        
                    

                        

                    
                        
                    

                    

                    
Data Availability

              
              
The datasets generated during the current study are available from the corresponding author on reasonable request.

Appendix: Computer-Aided Derivations
The following table gives a derivation in 
\(Q_\lambda \)
 of 
\(\alpha = 9-16\lambda \)
, a unit of 
\(\mathbb {Z}[\lambda ]\)
, where 
\(\lambda = -(3+\sqrt{17})/2\)
 (minimal polynomial 
\(x^2+3x-2\)
), used in the proof of Proposition 
8.4
. This may not be the shortest derivation possible; it was found by a program that favors combining points with small absolute value. Here, 
\({\mathop {\star }}\)
 means 
\(\mathop {{\mathop {\star }}_{\lambda }}\)
.

 of the unit

 of

, where 
\(\lambda = -(3+\sqrt{17})/2\)
.

Appendix: Plots of Some Discrete 
\(\lambda \)
-Convex Sets
Fig. 5
Full size image
\(Q_{\lambda _5}(P_5)\)
 (left) and 
\(Q_{\lambda _5}(P_{10})\)
 (right), the 
\(\lambda _5\)
-convex hulls of a regular pentagon and regular decagon, respectively. 
\(\lambda _5 = 1+\varphi = \left( 3+\sqrt{5}\right) /2\)
.
Fig. 6
Full size image
\(Q_{\lambda _7}(P_7)\)
 (left) and 
\(Q_{\lambda _7}(P_{14})\)
 (right), the 
\(\lambda _7\)
-convex hulls of a regular heptagon and regular 14-gon, respectively. 
\(\lambda _7 \approx 5.049\)
 and has minimal polynomial 
\(x^3-6x^2+5x-1\)
.
Fig. 7
Full size image
\(Q_{\lambda _9}(P_9)\)
 (top left), 
\(Q_{\lambda _9}(P_{18})\)
 (top right), and 
\(Q_{\lambda _{15}}(P_{30})\)
 (bottom). 
\(\lambda _9 \approx 8.291\)
 and has minimal polynomial 
\(x^3-9x^2+6x-1\)
. 
\(\lambda _{15} \approx 22.881\)
 and has minimal polynomial 
\(x^4-24x^3+26x^2-9x+1\)
.
Fig. 8
Full size image
\(Q_2(P_6)\)
 (top left), 
\(Q_{2+\sqrt{2}}(P_8)\)
 (top right), and 
\(Q_{2+\sqrt{3}}(P_{12})\)
 (bottom). 
\( Q_2(P_6) = \{a+b\eta \mid a,b\in \mathbb {Z}\;\mathrel { \& }\;ab \equiv 0 \pmod {2} \}\)
, where 
\(\eta := e^{2\pi i/3}\)
 is the principal third root of unity. All three sets are Meyer sets. Of these three, only 
\(Q_2(P_6)\)
 is periodic.
